Consolidated sources
Gettysburg Total War animation plan, version 2: what is possible, what is not
Bottom line.
 Version 2 commits to autonomous execution. Claude Code does the work itself, using the owner’s GPU as hard as the job needs, and the plan no longer asks the owner for motion-capture
sessions, scans, LiDAR crews, live-fire recordings, a historian panel or any other physical capture. Every one of the owner’s 14 new demands and the original seven requirements remains binding, with
no exceptions, and each now has its own section, deliverable and test (see the requirement coverage map below). Honest accounting still applies. Four classes of content result. (1) 
Documented
:
army-to-brigade structure, most regiment and battery positions, weather observations, the Warren 1868-69 survey, USGS lidar, the Letterman system, and the weapons and dress of the units for which
a source was retrieved. (2) 
Evidence-limited
: clock times (sources disagree by 30 to 60 minutes and Union and Confederate clocks reportedly differed by about 20 minutes), most company-level
activity, Confederate state-by-state dress, earthwork dimensions, crop and tree state, night practice, and farm-by-farm fence loss. For these the plan extracts everything the free library supports,
records each remaining gap in the Evidence-Gap Register with a confidence tier, and marks it in the product. (3) 
Impossible literally
: the name, face and action of each of roughly 160,000 individuals
at each minute; a single true ordering of chaotic combat; real 1863 audio; the exact 1863 atmosphere. These get the strongest evidence-based reconstruction, labeled, never a claim of exemption. (4)
Merely enormous
: terrain from lidar and Warren contours, a three-day regiment-and-battery timeline, per-unit kits, procedural drill animation, synthesized and physically timed sound, a scrubbable
baked timeline, and an offline film-quality render path. Substitutes for physical capture (public motion datasets, procedural drill animation, synthesized black-powder audio, open-licence textures, lidar
from a public bucket, automated adversarial review) are autonomous but are labeled as modern reconstructions in the product and in the register. 
Nothing described here has been built; this is
research and planning only.
 Research limits that bound this version: only sources reachable through the research tools were read; web-search quotas ran out in every version 2 research pass
(typically after 20 to 45 searches), which capped the depth of the timeline (691 rows in total: 225 for June 26 to July 1, 248 for July 2, 218 for July 3 to July 14, so each span meets 200 rows but the thin
spots are noted per row), earthwork dimensions, hospital rosters, Confederate dress and fence-loss detail; several archives blocked automated fetching; and the primary texts (Official Records Vol. 27,
Bachelder, state monument volumes, regimental histories, memoirs) were located and sized but not yet read in full. Reading them is Phase 1 work in Part D, not something this report claims to have
done.
What changed in version 2
Latest change (owner instruction after review):
 Events away from Gettysburg were removed from the timeline. The default film path now ends with the July 4 events at Gettysburg, then swirls to
the cemetery on November 19, 1863, for a front-row view of Lincoln giving the Gettysburg Address (new section B.12, requirement R22, phase 15, gate Q28). The timeline now holds 691 rows.
The owner’s earlier draft listed inputs the owner would have to supply. The owner rejected that and set 14 demands. This table ties each to the section that now handles it. “Addressed in full” means
the plan, deliverable and test exist in this report. “Addressed with evidence-limited content named” means the plan is complete but the record or the reachable sources are thin in specific places, and
those places are named.

Executive summary: the honest bottom line
How the evidence was gathered, and its limits
Gettysburg Total War animation plan, version 2 | page 2 of 144


=== PAGE 3 ===
Version 1 synthesized ten research-note files. Version 2 adds fifteen more, covering timelines for the approach and July 1, July 1-3 night and July 2, and July 3 through the retreat; Union and
Confederate dress; small arms by unit; artillery by battery; cavalry; earthworks and gun positions; buildings, fences and roads; field hospitals; environment, weather and night; terrain sources; the
primary-source library; and autonomous methods and efficiency. Every researcher states a tool-call count (roughly 20 to 55) and reports that the web-search budget ran out partway. The effects are
specific and are carried as gaps, not guesses:
The Official Records were not reached directly in most passes. The timeline rows rest on Wikipedia, NPS, American Battlefield Trust, Stone Sentinels and secondary summaries that cite the
Official Records. Where a note says “cited by” a historian, the report treats it as secondary.
Searches for the Lady and Culp farms, the poorhouse, Emmitsburg Road fence destruction, Library of Congress photographs, Hunt’s and Alexander’s full report texts, Confederate hospitals,
litter-bearer training and Soldiers’ National Cemetery numbers were planned and never performed. Those are gaps, not evidence of absence.
Some archives refused automated access (loc.gov search endpoints, HathiTrust page views, docsouth, Gutenberg search, parts of NARA and Rumsey). Items from those are marked unverified-
URL in the notes.
Fetched pages were sometimes summarized by a small model; several documentation pages returned only an outline (Epic’s Movie Render Queue command-line page, the Blender command-line
page).
The report therefore reads as a map of what reachable sources say, with the exact places where the next pass must dig.
What is documented
Total War structure and behavior (Part A).
 Engine lineage, logic-ahead-of-display projectile simulation, GPU animation textures, LOD by on-screen triangle size, slot-and-fragment animation,
matched combat, the Warhammer hit-chance formula. Unchanged from version 1.
Gettysburg at formation level.
 Orders of battle to brigade, regiment and battery name; strengths and losses with ranges; the Letterman medical system; the Warren survey (1868-69, 4-ft
contours); the park’s 1863 GIS layers; Adams County lidar (2017 quality-level-2, 1-m DEM, free) and earlier PAMAP lidar.
Timeline volume.
 The master timeline holds 691 rows: 225 for June 26 to July 1, 248 for July 2 (night of July 1-2 through the night of July 2-3) and 218 for July 3 to July 14. Each row is tagged
documented, inferred, plausible-procedural or open gap, and the counts per span are in B.2. It is a large expansion on version 1 but still a floor: the primary texts that would add minute-level
detail have not yet been read.
Weather and sky.
 Observer readings by Rev. Prof. Michael Jacobs (7 a.m., 2 p.m., 9 p.m.): June 26-27 rainy; June 28-30 dry; July 1 cloudy, about 72 to 76 F; July 2 foggy or overcast morning,
clearing, 81 F; July 3 humid, 87 F at 2 p.m., thunderstorm about 6 p.m.; July 4 cool and rainy (1.39 in). Local mean time was UTC minus 5 h 09 min; sunrise about 04:35 and sunset about 19:32
local mean time; full moon on July 1 at 06:46 UT; moonrise followed sunset by 20 to 140 minutes, so moonlight began about 21:00 on July 2 and about 22:00 on the night of July 3-4. The sun and
moon positions were computed by the researcher with an ephemeris, not copied from a source. Jacobs’s original was not read; the figures are secondary.
Structures.
 The McPherson barn did not burn on July 1 (it survives; the house burned in 1895). The Bliss barn and house were burned deliberately on the morning of July 3 by the 14th
Connecticut. The Sherfy barn burned from shellfire (July 2 evening per one account, July 3 per most). The Rose barn burned in 1910 and the Codori barn was torn down in 1882. The stone wall at
the Angle was a pre-existing farm wall, not a Union work. Fences were mostly post-and-rail on roads and boundaries (double fences on the Emmitsburg Road), Virginia worm fences inside fields,
loose stone walls and yard pickets, and were destroyed by fire, shooting, pulling down for works and trampling.
Earthworks as a process.
 Union works began on the evening of July 1 (Wadsworth’s division led by the 6th Wisconsin, and the 123rd New York). The XII Corps main line on Culp’s Hill was built
from about 6 a.m. on July 2 and took roughly 3 to 4 hours (start time disputed; the only height found is about 5 ft). Steuart’s Confederates took the empty lower works the night of July 2 and were
driven out on July 3. Lunettes on East Cemetery Hill existed by 4 p.m. on July 2. Stone breastworks on Little Round Top’s south slope date to July 2 (NPS) with most surviving ones credited to
Weed’s brigade and the Pennsylvania Reserves after the fighting (Wikipedia).
Dress corrections.
 The 5th New York (Duryee’s Zouaves) and its sister 165th New York were not at Gettysburg (the 5th mustered out May 14, 1863). The 140th New York wore regulation blue
(Zouave dress issued January 1864). The 146th New York (Zouave style since June 3, 1863) and the 114th Pennsylvania (Collis Zouaves) wore Zouave dress; the 44th New York is uncertain. The
Iron Brigade’s black hat, nine-button frock coat and white leggings are sourced. The typical Army of Northern Virginia jacket in July 1863 was the Richmond Depot Type II jean-cloth shell jacket;
Type III (kersey, no shoulder straps) is not Gettysburg-era. Wheat’s Tigers were gone by 1862, so Hays’s regiments are dressed as ordinary infantry. The Devil’s Den sharpshooter photograph
was staged (about July 6, O’Sullivan and Gardner). Spencer carbines were not on Buford’s line on July 1; the 5th Michigan and part of the 6th Michigan used them on July 3. About 90 percent of
infantry on both sides carried rifled muskets; the Springfield M1861 and the Enfield P1853 dominate, with a minority of .69 smoothbores.
Hospitals.
 A three-tier system (regimental, division and corps, general) fed by an Ambulance Corps; day-1 hospitals in town and on Seminary Ridge fell to the Confederates; from July 2 a belt of
farms behind Cemetery and Culp’s ridges along Rock Creek, White Run, the Taneytown Road and the Baltimore Pike; Camp Letterman opened July 20 or 22 and closed November 20, 1863.
What is evidence-limited
Timing.
 Conflicts of 30 to 60 minutes persist: Lee’s arrival (1:30 vs 2:30 p.m., July 1), Pender’s assault (3:30 vs about 4:00), the July 3 cannonade length (about 1 hour per Wert to 3 hours per
Coddington), the infantry step-off (about 2:00 vs about 3:00), Camp Letterman’s opening and closing. Stuart’s arrival on July 2 is afternoon per most sources and evening per others. Union and
Confederate clocks reportedly differed by about 20 minutes. Reynolds’s death time, Hancock’s arrival time and Ewell’s decision time are not found. The timeline notes themselves flag weak
sources (a terse unreferenced timeline site and a forum thread, used only as flagged illustrations).
Counts.
 Gun counts for July 3 range 130 to 170 Confederate and 80 to 120 Union. Union casualties are about 23,050; Confederate figures range from 22,709 to 23,231 to 28,063.
Commander and company-officer appearance.
 Only a handful of commanders have sourced descriptions; Lee’s height is unreconciled. Version 2 adds the regulation officer dress (frock or
sack coat, shoulder straps, subdued field dress with slouch hats and few sashes), but individual faces below corps level remain a gap. Per owner demand 10, soldiers are given historically
accurate kit and a generic face, not a photo replica of the real person.
Dress and arms below regiment.
 Union badges (shape per corps, color per division, adopted by order of March 21, 1863) are sourced, but Confederate state-by-state dress, flags, officer dress,
support-troop dress (provost, pioneers, signal, engineers, teamsters) and cavalry brigade-level dress are largely gaps. Regiment-level arms are reliable for only a handful of units; Berdan’s
sharpshooters are the best-covered (dark green, Sharps rifles plus some private target rifles), with a tension between “green” and “modified blue” in one source.
Earthworks and gun positions.
 Dimensions, rail-pile details and Confederate works on July 2-3 are undocumented in the retrieved material; there is no retrieved source for rail breastworks on
Cemetery Ridge or for Warren’s engineers building works. Battery detail is good for Calef, Bigelow, Hart, Smith, Hazlett, Cushing, Wheeler, Latimer, Carter, Alexander and Cabell, and thin for
Weir, Thomas, Cowan, Stevens, Wiedrich, the Culp’s Hill batteries and the Seminary Ridge Third Corps battalions. Crew size is disputed (nine-man crew per NPS Fort Scott vs gunner plus seven
numbered cannoneers).
Vegetation and night practice.
 The Wheatfield held standing wheat on July 2; Big Round Top was heavily wooded; the Peach Orchard existed; Little Round Top was rocky and partly bare
(summit woodland accounts conflict). Crop state and species are mostly inferred from the agricultural calendar. No sourced accounts of campfire rules, lantern use, burning buildings or picket-
fire policy were found; night activity is design hypothesis until the primary texts are read.
Acoustics.
 Acoustic shadows are documented in general; the Pittsburgh claim is dated July 1 in one source and general in another; the “Ellis” attribution is unverified; the 1863 atmosphere is
unknown, so any shadow zone is a scenario.
Total War internals and the shoe story.
 Unchanged from version 1: the “56,000 soldiers” figure is unverified, hit-roll constants differ between forum posts, and the Confederate shoe story first
appears in 1877 and must not be depicted as documented motive.
What is impossible to deliver literally at 100 percent
1. 
The position, face, exact wear and action of each of roughly 160,000 individuals at each moment. The record gives units, not people-by-minute.
2. 
A single true ordering of chaotic combat events within a regiment-minute.
3. 
1863 ground cover beyond what maps, photographs and accounts show: individual tree positions, exact fence lines everywhere, crop height and harvest state per field, ruts, stream channels.
4. 
Real 1863 audio; it can only be synthesized.
5. 
The exact 1863 atmosphere that governed sound travel.
6. 
The face and build of most commanders below corps level and nearly all company officers.
These are not engineering failures. Under the Unbreakable Law, Claude Code never claims an exception for them: it builds the strongest evidence-based reconstruction, records the gap in the register
with a confidence tier, labels the content in the product, and escalates to the owner, who alone decides scope.
What is merely enormous
Terrain from lidar corrected against Warren contours; a three-day regiment-and-battery movement script; per-regiment kits; a procedural drill animation library built from the manuals plus openly
licensed motion datasets; synthesized black-powder audio with correct distance delay; time-keyed terrain states (fences, barns, earthworks) that change at documented moments; a 24/7 sun, moon and
weather driver; a scrubbable event-sourced timeline; offline cinematic render of any camera path with a resumable job runner; a library-ingestion and audit pipeline over a corpus the notes size at
roughly 40 to 70 million input tokens. The notes give no sourced cost or schedule for the whole project; it comes from the vertical-slice benchmark.
Autonomous substitutes and their licensing facts
Version 2 replaces each formerly human-supplied input with a software route (full table in C.10). The facts that matter, from the notes:
Motion.
 CMU Mocap is free for all uses and may be included in commercially sold products but not resold directly. 100STYLE is CC BY 4.0. Mixamo is royalty-free including commercial but
requires an Adobe ID. AMASS, LAFAN1, Motion-X, Horse-10 and anything built on the SMPL body model are non-commercial or restricted, so they are prototype-only and any output derived from
Gettysburg Total War animation plan, version 2 | page 3 of 144


=== PAGE 4 ===
them is tagged non-commercial-tainted. Drill motion written from the Hardee and Casey manuals needs no third-party licence.
Audio.
 Freesound offers CC0, CC BY and CC BY-NC tiers; only CC0 and CC BY are safe for a monetized film. The one acoustics paper retrieved gives no synthesis method, so black-powder
synthesis is an agent-built design and is a modern reconstruction.
Assets.
 Smithsonian Open Access (CC0 for flagged items) and Poly Haven (all CC0, with a documented API) are usable; Megascans status in 2026 is unknown (it was free to end-2024, then paid,
and free items require adding each to a Fab library); the reference Gaussian-splatting code is non-commercial.
Terrain.
 USGS 3DEP lidar is in a public AWS bucket (usgs-lidar-public) accessible with no account; Allmaps georeferences IIIF maps in the browser without an account and publishes its data
under CC0.
Compute.
 Unreal Engine is free under USD 1M gross revenue (the fetched page did not address linear film output); Epic’s command-line rendering supports unattended runs, and because exact
resume behavior was not verified, the project builds its own resumable per-shot job runner with content-hash manifests.
Review.
 Adversarial reviewer subagents, structured claim records needing two sources, and photo-match tests replace the historian panel. No retrieved source shows that LLM review equals
expert historians; products are marked “not reviewed by a historian.”
Quota limits that capped research depth
The search quota ran out in every version 2 pass. The concrete effects are: a timeline that is dense (691 rows) but still lacks minute-level detail for most units; no Official Records text for Hood, Law,
Warren, Sickles, Birney, Humphreys, Geary, Imboden and others; no Gibbon’s Artillerist’s Manual range tables or Blakely specifications; no Confederate hospital roster; and unverified URLs for several
archive items. Version 2 is a plan to close these gaps by reading the free library, not a claim that they are closed.
Account-only owner steps
The only owner acts are one-time legal acts that software cannot perform on the owner’s behalf, listed in Part D: for example an Adobe ID for Mixamo, Epic and Fab login and licence acceptance, any
cloud billing, and Claude usage credits or an API key. Everything else is Claude Code’s job. Each of these steps is avoidable or minimized where a no-account route exists (the public lidar bucket, CC0
sources, CMU Mocap, 100STYLE).
The tension the owner must decide
Hollywood-grade CGI and a free camera at any spot and moment for roughly 160,000 agents are supported separately by the notes (offline rendering; LOD-tiered real-time crowds) but not together at
one fidelity by any retrieved evidence. Part D recommends one data layer feeding two renderers (real-time free-fly and offline re-render of any camera path). That is a reasoned recommendation the
owner should approve explicitly.
Requirement coverage map
Section numbers follow the report map. “Test” means the check that would prove the requirement is met when built; none of it has been run. “Honest limit” names what the record or physics prevents.

PART A. How Total War games render, animate and fight
A.0 Evidence quality in this part
The Total War notes are candid. The rendering note says GDC Vault, Game Anim and Feral pages were video-only or blocked; the projectile note says primary sources (dev blogs, interviews) were not
found and most material is wiki, forum and modding-tutorial text; the melee note says primary CA dev diaries were not located; the gap-fill notes say wiki.totalwar.com and several outlets were
unreachable. Everything below is therefore tagged by what the notes support. “Documented” means a fetched source said it. “Inferred” is the note author’s reasoning. “Not found” means no source
was retrieved, which is absence of evidence from limited searching, not proof of absence.
A.1 Engine lineage and release record
The series runs on proprietary CA engines. Shogun: Total War used “TW Engine 1” (Medieval shared it), Rome: Total War used “TW Engine 2”, and Empire (2009) introduced “TW Engine 3”, known as
Warscape, which Warhammer, Troy and the rest built on; Attila is said to share Rome II’s engine; Medieval III is listed as Warcore with a question mark on the source page (
Wikipedia: Total War
series
). ModDB lists Warscape from Empire through Pharaoh, Pharaoh Dynasties (July 2024, a free addition), and a Shogun 2 Complete Edition entry with a future date that is unconfirmed (
ModDB
).

Currency warning:
 the notes’ news coverage ends in December 2025 and the melee gap-fill note says no search for releases after that date was done. As of
today (October 2026) the status of Medieval III and Warhammer 40,000, and any Warcore technical disclosures, may have advanced and must be re-checked.
A.2 Rendering an army, a unit and a single soldier
What is documented
Shogun 2 (2011).
 A CA coder described “a completely new deferred renderer” with DX11 added while DX9 had to remain fast; an R32F depth buffer in the G-buffer halved performance on some
cards; campaign trees were bound by pixel shading and overdraw and fixed with alpha testing and remodelled low-poly trees (explicitly not imposters), cutting GPU cost by 50%; sea-floor geometry
under deep water was removed (
MCV
). This is campaign-map and environment optimization, not soldier rendering.
Rome II (2012-13).
 A Rome II interview describes land and naval battles merged into one code base, a DX11 renderer using multiple cores, and an animation system that uses “the GPU-computed
system from Fall of the Samurai, based on vertex texture fetch with all keyframes stored in a dynamic texture”, plus GPU support for cinematic meshes with 4 bones per vertex, DX10 texture arrays,
and LOD for scalability (
PC Games Hardware
). The note author infers that this makes per-soldier time offsets cheap in one instanced draw, but the source does not say that.
Warhammer II (2018).
 A CA design article states that battle logic and animation pipelines are decoupled from display so “the logic generates the ‘future’ whilst the display renders the ‘now’”, that
LOD scaling applies to environments and units, that extra techniques were needed because the camera can zoom and rotate freely, and that multicore spreading dates from Warhammer I (
Game
Developer
).
Three Kingdoms (2019).
 The Intel/CA talk is the most concrete rendering source in the notes: a game tick of about 10 fps, a frustum test of all frustums held in a bitfield, small models culled by
projected bounding box, LOD chosen from the approximate on-screen average triangle size, per-mesh instance lists per pipeline stage built per-thread with lockless double-buffering, and multi-layer
blended order-independent transparency (MBOIT) for particles (
Intel PDF
). A Total War: Arena article adds a fixed-rate simulation much lower than render rate, and shared texture assets across
Gettysburg Total War animation plan, version 2 | page 5 of 144


=== PAGE 6 ===
entities (
80.lv
). The note author infers that soldiers are interpolated between simulation ticks; no source says so.
Rome II aims and Unit Cam.
 CA aimed for “thousands of individual people acting and responding” (
PC Gamer
), and the Unit Cam did not require a renderer rewrite because the focus was believable
close-up animation (
PC Games Hardware
).
What was asked for and not found
The user asked how the engines render an entire army, a unit and each soldier. The honest answer is that the following were 
not found in any retrieved source
: per-soldier polygon counts; LOD tier
counts; impostor or billboard use and distances (the Three Kingdoms and Shogun 2 sources never mention impostors); max on-screen soldiers per title; per-game unit-size tables (the Fandom Unit_Size
fetch failed; a community thread claims Warhammer III “Ultra” is the balance basis but offers no official citation (
Steam
)); rendering internals for Rome: Total War, Medieval II, Empire, Napoleon,
Thrones of Britannia, Troy, Pharaoh and Warhammer III. A TWC forum recollection says CA long claimed “up to 10,000 soldiers” and that slowdowns came mainly from AI pathfinding rather than
rendering (
TWC
); it is forum memory and not official. The “56,000 soldiers” Shogun 2 claim on Wikipedia is unverified and uncited in the extraction (
Wikipedia
); the note author reads it as a theoretical
cap, not simultaneous on-screen soldiers.
Two unverified memory items from the notes (not facts): that Rome 1 used 3D models with several LOD levels and a “Huge” unit size setting often quoted as 240 soldiers; and that Rome 1/Medieval II
use a 
descr_model_battle.txt
 with per-LOD entries and distances. These are leads for pack-file or modding-community inspection.
Rendering model, as best the notes support
An army is a set of units; a unit is a group of soldier entities whose simulation runs at a low fixed tick on CPU cores, with results computed ahead of display; each soldier is drawn as an instanced, GPU-
skinned mesh whose animation keyframes sit in a texture, with the LOD level picked from projected size and small objects culled. The first two clauses are documented; the “instanced GPU-skinned”
assembly is the note author’s inference joining the Rome II and Three Kingdoms sources.
A.3 How soldiers are animated
Data-driven slots and fragments (Warhammer-era)
The tw-modding wiki states “the game has no idea what an animation is”: behavior comes from configuration and metadata. Animations layer (walk plus attack plus hand pose). An 
.animpack
 container
holds a 
.bin
 and fragments (
.frg
). The bin holds a database key, a skeleton link, an optional mount entry and fragments, with later fragments overriding earlier slots. A fragment row has a “Slot and
Id” that triggers an action and can link a meta file adding particles, camera shake, hit position and area-of-effect, and toggling visible prop slots. A missing slot can break a capability (for example, a
ranged attack cannot fire). Mounts use either attachment points (near-stationary riders) or synchronized animations with matching slots such as Attack_1 and Rider_attack_1. Tools named are RPFM,
AssetEditor and RME (
tw-modding wiki
). The note’s author found the wiki’s Skeletons, Animations and Meta sections empty. Blend times, per-soldier variation, randomization and formation-based
behavior are 
not documented
 in any retrieved source; the author infers that attack variants are chosen by slot index at runtime.
Motion capture
Shogun 2 used “hundreds of motion-captured animations” with help from the British Kendo Association (
GameSpot
); a student blog (secondary) says it used passive optical capture with two actors
(
Babel blog
). CA opened a Horsham mocap studio in 2012 with 46 cameras and a 12 m by 13 m volume (
MCV
). Rome II drew marching animations and choreographed “matched combat” from a large
pool of captured data (
PC Gamer
); Rome II horses and elephants used pre-captured footage, and impact sounds were recorded with real Roman armour (
PCInvasion
). Three Kingdoms duels used
separate mocap of wuxia martial artists that CA called “a big step up from our matched combat system” (
GamesBeat
); Troy’s hero duels were inspired by the film Troy, with animation sets that vary by
weapon and armour pairing (
Wccftech
). Warhammer creature animation used exaggerated anticipation and holds for top-down readability (
80.lv
). 
No total animation counts exist
 in the notes for
any title.
Warcore animation
Warcore’s animation is rewritten around a graph-based node system with layering, strafing, and complex vehicle movement (
OC3D
).
A.4 Movement, formations and scale
Sourced facts are few. Logic and display are decoupled (Warhammer II article above). Battle AI uses hidden hint-lines around hills and similar features, and designers use hand-made maps to avoid
pathfinding and line-of-sight problems (
Game Developer
). Troy documents terrain effects: heavy infantry are slow and easy to flank but deadly head-on, sand and mud slow troops (mud hurts heavy
troops most) (
GodIsAGeek
), infantry differ in speed and turning, and rain reduces archer range (
Wccftech
). A Three Kingdoms siege map rule sizes streets for about 1.5 ultra-size infantry units abreast
(
tw-modding
).
Not found:
 walk, run and charge speeds; wheeling and turning mechanics; collision between soldiers; slope handling; rout visuals beyond the UI; loose versus tight formation, skirmish and cavalry-
impact mechanics.
A.5 How troops fight: melee, mass, morale, kills
Pairing and matched combat
Shogun 2 troops charge and “engage by pairing off in one-on-one combat” until the outnumbered company’s morale breaks (
GameSpot
). Warscape plays attacker and victim animations together; a
modder reports the victim animation belongs to the attacker’s set and that newer games tie pairs less closely to their surroundings so models can drift (
TWC
). A student critique argues kill animations
were effectively the only engagement mode, causing queues around single targets, poor collision and idle units (
Babel blog
); a Steam poster says deaths look like random falls and that duels were
reduced after Rome II complaints, with no developer statement (
Steam
). Both are opinion. The note author infers pairing is a per-soldier target assignment plus a contact-range check then a
synchronized animation pair, and that earlier games (Shogun 1 through Medieval II) used unsynchronized per-soldier attacks; neither was confirmed.
Hit and damage formulas
Warhammer is the best documented. The official Feature Focus: base hit chance 35%; add melee attack, bonus versus infantry and charge bonus (charge bonus decays linearly over 13 s); subtract
defender melee defence; flank and rear attacks multiply the defender’s melee defence by 0.6 and 0.3; the result is clamped to 8%-90%; a d100 roll hits if roll is at most the chance. Damage splits into
base and armour-piercing (AP); armour reduces base damage only, by a random 15%-30% (0.5x to 1x of the armour value in the worked example); overkill damage is wasted (
Totalwar.com
). The
Fandom wiki restates it (
Warhammer wiki
). In-game Academy notes: melee defence gives no missile protection; flank and rear attacks cause more casualties and leadership loss; Thrones of Britannia
added Charge Reflect and critical hits at 10x damage (
Total War Academy
).
Older titles: a community analysis of Shogun 2 and Rome II gives hit chance clamps of 20-95% in one code excerpt and 15-85% in later tests, and a shield missile block of 5%-55% (poster-derived,
unverified) (
TWC
); Rome I and Medieval II are described as “DnD-like” with most units at 1 HP, details disputed (
TWC
). The constants differ by title and source, so no pre-Warhammer formula should
be treated as established.
Charge, mass, fatigue, morale
A CA developer is quoted in a Steam thread that charge outcome is “governed by mass and velocity” with a mass bonus for braced static units (
Steam
). A Rome II designer said mass matters less uphill
and more downhill, that knockdown is meant to disrupt rather than destroy, and that some knocked-down soldiers rise again (
PCInvasion
). Warhammer leadership: minus 8 when targeted by missiles,
plus 15 while charge bonus lasts, three routs then the unit shatters, damage lowers leadership (
Warhammer wiki
). 
Charge-bonus duration conflicts
 across sources (13 s decay in the dev blog, about
10 s on the wiki, about 30 s from a player). Morale exists in every title; Napoleon shows a morale bar under the regiment flag, broken units show a flashing white flag, shattered units flee the map
(
Total War Wiki
). Artillery bombardment, flanking and general death lower morale (
Wikipedia: Empire
). 
No numeric fatigue formula and no exact morale formula
 were found for any title.
Kill and death animation
Kill animation detail is thin. A Rome II mod restores “special kill animations from an older version”, implying a patch-era change (
ModDB
); Rome II ragdoll was expected to be toned down while
knockdown stayed (
PCInvasion
); Warhammer III’s “Blood for the Blood God III” adds blood and gore (
Wikipedia
). No CA statement on kill-animation counts, ragdoll implementation or fall animations
was found, and nothing on Warhammer monster kills, flying-unit melee, or giant stomps.

A.6 Projectiles: how bullets, arrows and balls fly
The documented model (Warscape-era tables)
Projectiles are real entities with ballistic parameters held in database tables: 
projectiles_tables
 (properties, base reload, missile behavior), 
projectile_audio
 (in-flight sound), projectile display, 
projectile_penetrations
 (how many entities of each size a projectile can pass through, for example “large_3”), 
missile_weapons
, 
land_units
 (primary missile weapon, reload modifier, accuracy), 
kv_morale
 (artillery morale penalties) and 
kv_rules
 (calibration distance in older games such as Empire) (
tw-modding: Missiles and You
).
Categories.
 Arrow and musket (blocked by shields, own volley behavior); misc (ignores shields); artillery (ignores shields, adds spinal dismemberment, applies morale penalties for hits and near
misses).
Trajectory modes.
 
trajectory_sight
 “low” is fixed velocity with variable angle and will not fire if the target is unreachable; “fixed” is fixed angle with variable speed, like a howitzer (90 is
vertical); “dual_low_fixed” uses low when possible and falls back to fixed when blocked. Minimum and maximum angle limit low fire.
Velocity, mass, gravity.
 Faster means flatter and easier against moving targets, with more knockback; mass times velocity drives knockdown. Gravity -1 means none; too much gravity makes
ranges unreachable and the unit will not fire. Flags “can bounce” and “can roll” exist; expiry range must exceed maximum range.
Accuracy.
 Marksmanship bonus plus the accuracy stat. “Calibration distance” is the range beyond which accuracy falls; “calibration area” is the circle the projectile lands in. This is a statistical
landing circle, not a per-pellet path. The projectile is nonetheless a simulated body that can hit terrain, buildings and bodies in flight.
Damage.
 Base and armour-piercing, physical or magic, fire or non-fire; “scaling damage” can rise as the enemy’s health falls; unit cards show DPS averaged over 10 s.
The Warhammer II article adds that every projectile is physically simulated, buildings and terrain block shots, and line-of-sight checks are queued to control cost (
Game Developer
). The Rome II
preview describes fully simulated projectiles (
PC Gamer
).
Empire’s tables
A modder thread (the poster’s belief, not confirmed documentation) describes Empire’s 
units_land_stats
 linking by 
artilleryIDRef
 to 
gun_type_to_projectiles
, one row per ammo type (carcass,
quicklime, shell, percussive shell, shot) with a muzzle-flash reference, each pointing into 
projectiles
. Fields named: 
shot_type
, 
blast_effect
, 
explosion_type
, 
effective_range
, 
minimum_range
, 
max_elevation
, 
muzzle_velocity
, 
damage
, 
penetration
, 
incendiary
, 
marksmanship_bonus
, 
spread
, 
can_bounce
, 
bounce_angle
, 
collision_radius
, 
base_reload_time
. Round shot does not explode and kills by
direct hit; explosions are in 
projectiles_explosions
 (
detonation_radius
, 
detonation_damage
, 
particle_type
, 
particle_number
, 
non_lethal_detonation
); impact graphics are in 
projectile_impacts
 per surface
(sand, mud, grass, water and others); the poster found 
collision_radius
 did not widen the damage area as hoped (
TWC: Modding artillery
).
Empire and Napoleon artillery: ammunition, bounce, impact
Ammunition.
 Guns carry up to five ammo types. A Steam discussion (anecdotal) says canister can kill over a dozen with one shot and is a short-range priority, shrapnel is “essentially long range
canister”, howitzer round shot is poor, explosive rounds carry a large accuracy penalty, and quicklime is for forts (
Steam
). Empire’s Fandom stub gives one gun stat line (3 guns, firepower 126,
range 400, accuracy 50, reload 30) (
Empire TW Fandom
).
Bounce and ground.
 Previews say cannonballs skip along the ground and are devastating against units in a row, shots ricochet off stone, balls stick in muddy ground, and artillery is hard to
move in rain and mud and defenceless at close range (
TWC: GameSpy preview
); these are reviewer observations, not developer quotes. A Medieval II forum post (game inferred by the note
author) says a steep slope can skip a shot about 100 m and non-explosive shots bounce more (
TWC
).
Napoleon.
 New physics creates impact craters when cannonballs hit the ground; powder smoke lingers and reduces visibility; there are far more particles per effect than in Empire; gunpowder
backfires in rain; elevation affects range (
Wikipedia: Napoleon
).
Fall of the Samurai.
 Armstrong and Gatling guns and coastal guns, some aimed in a first-person mode, with offshore artillery as covering fire (
Total War Wiki
).
Impact and damage presentation.
 Artillery has dedicated effects: per-surface impact particles, explosion particle sets, craters (Napoleon), knockback tied to mass and velocity, spinal
dismemberment on Warscape-era artillery, and morale penalties for hits and near misses (sources above).
Historical reference ammo.
 Roundshot flattens “scores of men” bouncing on dry ground but stops in soggy earth; canister is a thin tin case of lead balls, up to 200 g each, breaking apart at the
muzzle; shell is thin-skinned with a timed fuse (
Napoleon Guide
). This describes the real weapons, not game behavior.
Rockets, mortars, siege engines, naval fire
Empire lists howitzer foot artillery, a 4-inch mortar battery, rocket band and rocket troop units without stats (
Empire TW Fandom
). Empire naval: cannonball against hulls, chain shot against sails,
grapeshot against crews; ballistics physically modeled so friendly fire is possible; fleets up to 20 ships; boarding; weather affects visibility (
GameSpot
; 
Wikipedia: Empire
). Empire’s sea uses a
statistical wave spectrum, Fourier transform, vertex deformation and foam (
TechRadar
). Rome II siege: players report artillery aimed at the ground works best against towers and rams, fire acts as an
extra HP pool, and gates have a separate fire HP (2015 anecdotes, possibly patched) (
Steam
).
What was asked and not found: artillery crews, limbers, audio
The notes state plainly that there are 
no sources
 on: artillery crew animations (load, aim, fire, recoil); crew casualties and replacement; limber, move and unlimber behavior; rocket flight and mortar
mechanics beyond the stub data; muzzle flash and smoke VFX details; firing-line animations (fire by rank, kneel and fire, skirmish fire); reload cycles; canister cone geometry; firearm and volley sound
design; gibbing, ragdoll implementation, blood decals, building damage in Empire; drag modelling and friendly-fire resolution; and siege destruction or ballista, onager and trebuchet animation for
most titles. Only fragments exist: an Empire preview says individual soldiers go through musket loading steps including biting paper cartridges and early plug bayonets block firing (
TWC: GameSpy
preview
). These are listed as Open Research Questions in Appendix C.

battle at the crossing with river and at least one bridge or ford (Rome II

restricts movement, weakens cavalry and siege weapons (Rome II)
same
Ambush
Empire: trait-limited, ambushers invisible until they move; Napoleon: any
army from marked forests; spy keeps army visible; ambushed army starts in
column
Total War Wiki
Naval
Empire: first real-time 3D naval; Shogun 2: boarding; Rome II: combined
land-sea; FotS: ironclads, shore bombardment
Wikipedia
Duels / hero combat
Three Kingdoms, Troy
see A.5
Weather
Pharaoh sandstorms, heat, mud; Attila hostile weather; Troy rain
Windows Central
Ground types such as forest and mud are defined in the terrain editor and battle code applies buffs and debuffs; that statement is from Total War: Arena’s developers and may not hold for other titles
(
80.lv
). 
Not found:
 night battle mechanics, Warhammer ambush and forest-hiding rules, flying-unit and monster mechanics, ram, ladder and tower animation, fire spread implementation, wall-
destruction technology, Medieval II and Troy sieges, Napoleon naval specifics, Shogun 2 base-game naval sinking model.
A.8 Cameras, replays, UI, casualties
Rome II offers a free camera, a tactical view that zooms to a top-down overview with simplified icons, and a closest zoom that is over-the-shoulder on a single soldier (
PC Gamer
); Rome II controls
include a cinematic mode (
GamePressure
). Medieval II saves only the last fought campaign battle as 
battle.rpy
 in the temp folder and a cinematic editor plays it (
TWC
). A single Warhammer II forum
poster claims replays are auto-saved after large battles, which others dispute (
Steam
). The note author infers replays are recorded inputs on a deterministic simulation; not confirmed. Third-party
camera mods exist (
GitHub
). GameSpot found camera angles and controls problematic in Rome II sieges and large battles (
Wikipedia: Rome II
). Unit cards show weapon type, troop count and morale
(Three Kingdoms).
Medics, stretcher bearers, field hospitals, carried wounded:
 the notes found 
no source
 showing any of these in any Total War battle. Searches returned irrelevant results and some articles read
did not mention them. This is absence of evidence from limited searching, not proof. The closest behaviors are Lizardmen units below 50% health rampaging, and Rome II knocked-down soldiers who
rise again (
Total War Academy
; 
PCInvasion
). The note author suggests, unsourced, that death is binary per soldier with unit-level replenishment.
A.9 Warcore, Medieval III and Warhammer 40,000
Warcore was announced on 4 December 2025 as “the next evolution of Total War’s proprietary engine” (
Cosmocover
). Reported features: improved lighting, dynamic shadows and SSAO; upgraded
textures and meshes; “ribbon technology” for projectiles and particles; DirectX 12 first with DLSS, FSR and XeSS; Havok physics with dynamically destroyed buildings that change cover and paths;
vehicle suspension physics; a graph-based layered animation system; the first Total War engine for PlayStation and Xbox (
OC3D
; 
PC Games Hardware
). OC3D wrote “Havoc”; Havok is the likely
intended middleware (inference). 
Conflicts:
 PCGH and CA call Warcore an evolution, while a Gaming Trend preview says built from scratch replacing Warscape and adds deformable terrain, cover
and “thousands of units per side” (a previewer’s estimate) (
Gaming Trend
). Wikipedia lists Medieval III for Windows while Sixth Axis only says Total War is coming to PlayStation and Xbox (
Wikipedia
;
Sixth Axis
). Medieval III is in early pre-production under directors Leif Walter and Pawel Wojs with no release date; CA waited for Warcore to greenlight it. Warhammer 40,000 was announced 12
December 2025 for PC and consoles. A CA battles product owner said raw soldier count is a “similar ultimate scale of entities” to Warhammer III, with more reinforcements (
PC Games Hardware
). No
Warcore soldier counts or benchmarks were given by OC3D or PCGH.

A.11 What this means for a Gettysburg recreation (reasoned recommendations, not sourced findings)
1. 
Borrow the architecture, not the combat.
 The decoupling of simulation (low fixed tick, roughly 10 fps in Three Kingdoms) from rendering, frustum and size-based culling, and LOD selected by
projected triangle size are documented patterns suited to a free camera. A scrubbable battle needs even stronger decoupling: the history layer decides outcomes; the visual layer only plays them
back.
2. 
Do not copy stat-driven combat.
 Total War outcomes come from stat rolls (for example Warhammer’s 35% + attack - defence). The user’s requirement of “historically accurate combat
mechanics” is better served by regiment-level outcomes scripted from documented casualties and timing, with individual-level detail filled procedurally. Warhammer’s base/AP split does not map
to musket and bayonet fighting; Empire/Napoleon-style one-HP-ish models are closer (inference in the notes).
3. 
Use the projectile vocabulary as a checklist.
 Muzzle velocity, angle, gravity, bounce and roll flags, expiry, penetration count, calibration circle, per-surface impact effects, explosion radius
and fragment definitions, craters, lingering smoke, and morale effects of near misses cover what a cannon, canister and shell need to look right. Real values must come from Part B’s ordnance
data and ballistic references, not from Total War.
4. 
Do not rely on Total War for crew, limber, medic or wounded animation.
 The notes found none. Those must be authored procedurally from the Hardee and Casey drill manuals plus openly
licensed motion data (Part C.10).
5. 
Mimic the matched-combat idea carefully.
 Paired mocap sequences read well in close-up but the critiques (queues, sliding, clipping, one-on-one only) are exactly the risks for dense bayonet
melee at the Angle or the Railroad Cut; treat as a risk to test early.
A.12 Part A open gaps
All “not found” items above roll into Appendix C: pack-file inspection of Empire, Napoleon, Shogun 2, Rome II and Warhammer packs with RPFM or Pack File Manager; CA GDC talks (2012 GDC
Europe rendering sessions, a 2013 GDC CA talk, a Three Kingdoms art-design session; titles and listings exist, content was not read); the Warhammer lead animator’s BAFTA workshop and a Troy
cinematics talk (video only); Digital Foundry technical breakdowns; modding documentation for Rome 1 and Medieval II; and a re-check of post-December 2025 news.
PART B. The Battle of Gettysburg: what the record supports
B.0 Evidence quality in this part
The Gettysburg researchers say they could not access Official Records vol. 27, Coddington, Pfanz, Harman, Gottfried or Busey and Martin directly. Most timeline text is Wikipedia, which cites those
authors, plus NPS and American Battlefield Trust (ABT) pages. Times are approximate in the sources themselves. The minute-by-minute timeline requested by the user was 
not achievable
 from the
Gettysburg Total War animation plan, version 2 | page 8 of 144


=== PAGE 9 ===
sources reached; it needs Official Records reports, Pfanz, Coddington and Gottfried. Conflicts are shown, not resolved.

Civil War Medicine
The Confederate record is structurally weak: only about one third of company muster rolls survive for the pre-battle period and many casualty reports were lost; “missing” lumps captured, deserters
and unidentified dead (
Stone Sentinels CSA
). The researcher found copy errors in the Stone Sentinels tables (Anderson’s division row duplicates Birney’s exactly; Posey’s brigade percentage does not
match its numbers). Sum of equipped strengths (about 160,000) is consistent with “about 165,000” participants only if non-equipped, staff and rear-echelon men are included (note author’s inference).
Any “number of soldiers to animate” must therefore be a range with a declared basis.
B.2 Master timeline (the backbone for scripting)
This is the integrated chronological table that the scripting layer and the animation engine consume. Part 1 (this file, B.2.1) covers June 26 to the night of July 1-2. B.2.2 (July 2), B.2.3 (July 3-14), B.2.4
(unit arrival and departure ledger) and B.2.5 (conflict register) continue the same schema, so IDs, tiers and column meanings below apply to all of B.2.
Columns.
 1. 
ID.
 
T1 -nnn
, numbered in table order (the “1” is the B.2.1 block; B.2.2 will use 
T2-
, B.2.3 
T3-
). IDs are stable references for scripts, dependency links and the conflict register. Day-header
rows carry “-” and are not events. 2. 
Time window (local).
 Times are quoted as the source gives them, in local civil time of 1863. Local mean time at Gettysburg is UTC minus 5 h 09 min; a modern
EST clock is about 9 minutes ahead of local mean time and an EDT clock 1 h 9 min ahead (environment notes). Sunrise (04:35), sunset (19:32) and moonrise (19:53) on July 1 are in local mean time.
Sources describing July 1 mix Union and Confederate clocks, which one source estimates differed by about 20 minutes (unnamed estimate, Stone Sentinels time note); that offset is not applied to any
row, it is carried as uncertainty. “NIGHT” marks rows between civil dusk and civil dawn. “n/s” means the source does not state the time. A leading “about” sign means approximate. 3. 
Place / road.
Named road, farm, ridge or building, as the sources name it. 4. 
Units.
 Down to regiment or battery where the sources give it; a regiment or brigade listed with a “named” caveat means the source
names only part of the roster. 5. 
Action.
 What the units do, written so a scripter can turn it into a state change. 6. 
Tier.
 
documented
 (a specific source states it), 
inferred
 (reasoned from documented
facts), 
plausible-procedural
 (generated to fill a gap, seeded and reproducible, shown on screen with a marker), 
open gap
 (the record or the reachable sources are silent; the row says what is missing,
never what happened). The source notes’ SOURCED, INFERRED and GAP tags map to documented, inferred and open gap. 7. 
Conflict or range note.
 Every disagreement is shown as a range with
each value and its source; none is smoothed away. Rows whose time is disputed also point to the highlights section after the table. 8. 
Source key.
 Short keys, expanded in “Sources for this section” at
the end of the file.
How conflicts are shown.
 A disputed time is written in the note column as value (source) / value (source). The Time window column shows the earliest and latest plausible values (or the value the
first-listed source gives). The engine treats the row as a time window, not an instant: it picks a canonical time for playback and keeps the alternates as metadata so the owner can switch variants (a
conflict register in B.2.5 will list the full set).
How the animation engine consumes rows (event graph with time windows).
 Each event row is a node with a window 
[earliest, latest]
, a canonical time, a tier and a unit list. Edges are
ordering constraints: a unit’s own rows form a chain (a unit never appears before its first row, and never in two places at once; this implements “units that arrive later must not appear before they
arrive”); named causal links (for example Reynolds’ death, then Doubleday takes command, then Howard learns of it) are explicit edges; and environment rows (weather, sun, moon) are a separate
track keyed to the same clock. Nodes with tier 
open gap
 generate no behaviour and surface as map markers in the evidence layer; 
plausible-procedural
 nodes generate seeded fallback behaviour that is
always visibly flagged. Where two sources disagree, the canonical time is the midpoint of the documented range, unless a primary report by the participant is available, in which case it is preferred and
the others are kept as alternates (this is an engine rule, not a historical claim). Rows tagged NIGHT drive the night renderer: all activity stays visible but dimmed.
Block statistics.
 225 event rows (185 documented, 22 inferred, 3 plausible-procedural, 15 open gap), plus 8 day-header rows. The documented share is high because the source note is built almost
entirely from cited pages; the inferred rows are environment geometry, the dust and fog assumptions and a few identifications; the three plausible-procedural rows are fallback behaviours for pickets,
aid posts and rear-camp night life; the open-gap rows record where the sources are silent.
Scope notes.
 The source note this block was built from could not read the full Official Records, the NPS Day 1 page, the ABT July 1 map pages, or the Howard, Early, Buford, Ewell and Hancock
reports, so the time-of-day coverage of June 26 to 30 and of the night hours is thinner than the daytime of July 1. The shortfalls are listed explicitly in the final subsection rather than filled.

T1-047
Corrections carried from the research that this period depends on.
 - The McPherson barn did not burn on July 1; it survives. The farmhouse burned in 1895. The barn was captured by the 55th
Virginia and used as a hospital (capture date inferred as July 1). - Spencer carbines were not on Buford’s line on July 1 (that is folklore); his men carried a mix of Sharps, Burnside, Gallager, Smith and
Merrill carbines. - The Heth shoe story first appears in 1877, so the Pettigrew raid is shown as a reconnaissance and supply move without a “shoe” subtitle. - Stuart’s absence is firm; his arrival at
Lee’s headquarters on July 2 is carried in B.2.2 with its conflicts (afternoon vs evening). - The stone wall at the Angle is a pre-existing farm wall; no July 1 row builds it. - The Oak Ridge stone wall
behind Baxter and the stone wall in front of the 123rd NY are not described as battle-built in the sources; the table treats them as presumed pre-existing walls (inferred), not July 1 construction.
What the animation must show for each phase
1. 
June 26 to 30 (approach).
 Marching columns on named roads in rain (June 26 to 27) then dry, dusty weather (June 28 to 30): Early’s division on the Cashtown and Hilltown roads and the York
Pike, Gordon and White’s cavalry on the Chambersburg Pike, Rodes’ division from Carlisle to Heidlersburg, Hill’s corps at Cashtown, Union corps converging on Taneytown, Emmitsburg, Marsh
Creek and Littlestown. The militia skirmish and flight on June 26 with the prisoners locked in the courthouse. Burnings: Caledonia Furnace, the Gettysburg rail cars and covered bridge, the Rock
Creek trestle. Buford’s column entering town on June 30 and placing vedettes west and north, with the Cavalry Corps hospital opening in the Presbyterian Church. Night rows: camps in rain on
June 26, camps at Cashtown, Heidlersburg, Emmitsburg, Marsh Creek and Buford’s camp 400 yards east of McPherson Ridge on June 30, Calef’s battery in its hollow, Meade writing the circular
at Taneytown, Stuart’s night march to Dover.
2. 
July 1, 04:00 to 07:30 (pre-contact).
 Full moon and dawn under cloud; Reynolds receiving orders, I Corps marching from Marsh Creek; Heth’s column leaving Cashtown at 05:00; Buford’s
pickets along Marsh Creek (a flagged fallback placement).
3. 
July 1, 07:30 to 10:00 (cavalry delay).
 Dismounted troopers behind fences, every fourth man holding horses, Gamble’s carbines, Devin on the north roads, Calef’s six 3-inch rifles, Heth’s
deploying skirmish lines and Pegram’s guns; Buford and his signal officer in the Seminary cupola.
4. 
July 1, 10:00 to 11:30 (I Corps deploys).
 Wadsworth’s division and Hall’s battery arriving by the Emmitsburg Road, the Iron Brigade in black hats in Herbst Woods, Cutler’s five regiments
split across the pike, Archer’s capture, Reynolds’ fall, the railroad cut charge; aid posts and walking wounded behind the ridge; Burns in the woods.
5. 
July 1, 11:30 to 13:30 (lull and arrivals).
 Heth falls back to Herr Ridge; XI Corps columns entering town and deploying north; Rodes’ division and Carter’s guns on Oak Hill; Howard on the
Fahnestock roof; St. Francis Xavier and other churches receiving amputations; Steinwehr digging on Cemetery Hill by about 14:00.
6. 
July 1, 13:30 to 16:00 (Oak Ridge and Barlow’s Knoll).
 Iverson, O’Neal, Daniel and Ramseur against Baxter and Paul on Oak Ridge, Blackford’s sharpshooters skirmishing tree to tree,
Gordon and Early against Barlow’s Knoll with Hilary Jones’s guns, Pettigrew then Pender against the McPherson and Seminary ridges, the rail barricade and Wainwright’s guns, the Spangler
farm becoming a hospital.
7. 
July 1, 16:00 to 19:30 (retreat and rally).
 Streets clogged with retreating Union troops, Wainwright’s guns galloping down Chambersburg Street three abreast, the 16th Maine rear guard,
prisoners, men hiding in cellars and yards; Hancock and Howard on Cemetery Hill, Slocum and Geary arriving; Confederates occupying the town, the Seminary and Pennsylvania College.
8. 
July 1, 19:30 to 04:35 (night).
 Sunset at 19:32, moonrise 19:53 behind cloud, dusk digging on Culp’s Hill (6th Wisconsin, 123rd NY, Wadsworth’s division) and Cemetery Hill, Coulter’s brigade
moving and building breastworks, III Corps at dusk, Johnson’s division arriving, Lee and Ewell’s “if practicable” decision, Meade arriving after midnight, Humphreys at 02:00, the VI Corps
marching 35 miles, surgeons operating, wounded sheltered at the Klingle farm, the cavalry camp at the wheat field, Lee’s headquarters at the Thompson house. Everything visible but dimmed;
campfire and burial life shown only as flagged fallback behaviour until sources are found.
At every hour the unit ledger (B.2.4) must answer “who is where”: no unit appears before its first row. Where a unit’s march between two camps is not documented (Early’s division on June 30, the III,
Gettysburg Total War animation plan, version 2 | page 21 of 144


=== PAGE 22 ===
V, VI and II Corps on June 27 to 30, Longstreet’s corps), the engine keeps it off-map or in a flagged open-gap state rather than inventing a route.

B.2.2 July 2 (night of July 1-2 through night of July 2-3)
What this section is.
 One integrated, time-sorted table of 248 rows for the span from the afternoon of July 1 (where the July 2 notes begin their overnight sequence) to the first Union artillery fire of
July 3 (about 04:30). It merges the unit-level July 2 timeline with the cavalry, earthwork, hospital, building and fence, battery-by-battery artillery, weather and small-arms notes. Rows stamped July 1
before 20:00 overlap B.2.1, and rows stamped July 3 after 03:00 overlap B.2.3; they are kept so the overnight story has no seam. Nothing here describes anything as built; it is a research table for
scripting.
Columns.
 ID (T2-001 onward, assigned in time order, so insertions renumber and scripts should key on the row’s text and times, not the number); time window (24-hour clock, local mean time, with
the markers below); place or road; units at regiment or battery level where the sources give it; action; evidence tier; conflict or range note; source key (list at the end of the section).
Tiers.
 documented = a specific source states it; inferred = reasoned from documented facts (the reasoning is in the note column); plausible-procedural = a generated fill for a gap, seeded and
reproducible, used only for night life and weather texture that no source describes; open gap = needed by the animation, not found, with the source that would settle it. Mapping from the research
notes: SOURCED to documented, INFERRED to inferred, GAP to open gap.
Time conventions.
 Times are approximate and are in the clock style of the source. Union and Confederate clocks reportedly differed by about 20 minutes (Stone Sentinels), so every
Union/Confederate pair carries that uncertainty. Local mean time (LMT) for the computed astronomy is UTC minus 5 h 9 min. Marker definitions, from the computed July 2 values: 
[NIGHT]
 = after the
end of civil twilight (20:04) and before its start (04:03); [DUSK] = sunset 19:32 to 20:04; [DAWN] = civil twilight start 04:03 to sunrise 04:35. The marker follows the start time of a row, and a row that
spans the night is marked by its start. Moonrise: about 19:53 on the night of July 1-2 (99% lit, cloud largely hid the moon) and 20:38 on the night of July 2-3 (95% lit, cirrus, fairly bright per Nofi).
Source quality.
 Several unit rows come from Wikipedia, the J. F. Epperson timeline (terse, unreferenced, flagged by the research notes as disagreeing with better sources on some times), a forum
thread and blogs; the weak ones are labeled in the source list. The Official Records for Hood, Law, Warren, Sickles, Birney, Humphreys, Barksdale, Greene, Geary, Hunt (July 2), Gibbon, Hays and most
brigade commanders were not retrieved by the research notes, so many times are second-hand through Hessler or Freeman. Plan: Claude Code extracts those reports in the primary-source pass (Part
Gettysburg Total War animation plan, version 2 | page 22 of 144


=== PAGE 23 ===
D) and every row marked with a conflict is re-resolved there; alternatives stay selectable in the viewer (rule in B.2.5).
Coverage summary.
 Tier counts: documented 216, inferred 12, plausible-procedural 6, open gap 14. Night rows: 33 for the night of July 1-2 (documented 21, inferred 3, plausible-procedural 2, open
gap 7) and 37 for the night of July 2-3 (documented 30, inferred 1, plausible-procedural 1, open gap 5). The night of July 1-2 is the thinnest in the record: the sources give arrivals, marches and digging,
but nothing on picket lines, fires, or civilians.
Corrections carried from research (do not reverse).
 The McPherson barn did not burn on July 1 (it survives; the house burned in 1895). The Bliss barn and house were burned deliberately on the
morning of July 3 by the 14th Connecticut. The stone wall at the Angle is a pre-existing farm wall. The 140th NY wore regulation blue; the 146th NY and 114th PA wore Zouave dress. Stuart reached
Lee on the afternoon or evening of July 2 (sources conflict). The Culp’s Hill and Little Round Top works are shown being built, never pre-placed, and today’s profiles are later reconstructions.

T2-061
Jul 2 ~06:00
Culp’s Hill
Greene’s 3rd Brigade
(60th, 78th, 102nd, 137th,
149th NY); Kane’s 2nd
Brigade on Greene’s right;
Wadsworth’s I Corps men
on his left
XII Corps takes position
about 06:00. Method:
felled trees cut to blocks
and stacked as a close log
fence, stones and
boulders, head logs,
cordwood, brush and
boughs placed in front for
concealment, rifle pits; a
traverse at right angles
protects the brigade’s
right where the line drops
into the swale. Works hard
to distinguish at 50 yards.
Height about 5 ft. Slocum
and Geary thought the hill
strong enough without
works; Greene ordered the
work anyway.
documented
Duration: 3 hours to noon
(Gettysburg Daily), about 4
hours (HistoryNet), ‘three
or four hours’
(Capt. Collins, 149th NY),
largely complete about
10:00 (DMNA). Only
height figure is 5 ft;
thickness, head-log size
and tool lists are gaps.
Layout: 60th NY upper
crest, 102nd and 149th the
saddle, 137th the lower
crest, 78th reserve.

B.2.3 July 3 to July 14 (Culp’s Hill, cavalry, the cannonade, the charge, the retreat)
This section is the scripting backbone for July 3 through the Confederate crossing of the Potomac, with a short tail to the closing of Camp Letterman on November 20, 1863. It is research and planning
only; nothing here is built. The table holds 219 rows, sorted by time, and merges the day-3 and retreat timeline with the cavalry, artillery, building/fence/road, earthworks, field-hospital, weather/night
and small-arms notes. Rows dated July 2 evening are carried over from B.2.2 so the Culp’s Hill night fight, the council of war and the hospital night work read continuously; their IDs belong to this
table.
How to read the table
 - 
ID
 is T3-001 and up, in time order. Time windows use the clock times the sources give; the research session treats them as local mean time (LMT). Add about 9 minutes for
the modern EST clock. Sunrise on July 3 was 04:36 LMT, civil twilight began 04:03; sunset 19:32, civil twilight ended 20:04, astronomical twilight ended 21:33 (computed, 
ENV-ASTRO
). Rows with
NIGHT
 fall between about 20:04 and 04:03 (or are explicitly night events); 
TWILIGHT
 and 
DUSK
 mark the margins. Rule B.10 applies: the sky is dimmed but everything and all activity stay visible. -
Tier
 is 
documented
 (a specific source states it), 
inferred
 (reasoned from documented facts), 
plausible-procedural
 (generated to fill a gap, seeded and reproducible, labelled in the product), or 
open gap
(no source reached; the row names the source to acquire). Counts: documented 190, inferred 9, plausible-procedural 3, open gap 16. 53 rows are night rows. - 
Conflict or range note
 carries
disagreements as ranges with their sources. Nothing is smoothed away; where a preferred animation anchor is offered, it is labelled inferred. - 
Source key
 points to the list at the end. Most day-3 and
retreat timing comes from Wikipedia articles, NPS handbook pages, a mirror of Hunt’s report and four NPS-hosted official reports; Official Records text for Geary, Ruger, Colgrove, Gibbon, Webb,
Hays, Stannard, Pickett, Pettigrew, Trimble, Lane, Kilpatrick, Gregg and Stuart’s brigades was not reached (see the gap list). Weak sources (forums, blogs, Wikipedia tables) are flagged in the note
column. - Wikipedia’s per-article figures cite Pfanz, Coddington, Busey and Martin, Trudeau, Wert and Sears; those books were not read directly.
ID
Time window
Place/road
Units
Action
Tier
Conflict or range note
Source key
T3-001
Jul 2 ~12:00-15:00 (carry-
over)
Lee HQ, Seminary Ridge
Stuart (Hampton, F. Lee,
Chambliss; Jenkins’s
brigade under Col.
Ferguson)
Stuart reaches Lee; his
brigades arrive that
evening. Lee’s greeting is
recorded as a reproach for
his lateness.
documented
Arrival hour conflicts:
shortly after noon (W-
ECF); noon-1 pm with
Hampton an hour later
(one W-STU fetch); mid-
afternoon (ABT-MCAV);
afternoon (Stuart’s
report). The task brief says
evening. Preferred: early-
to-mid afternoon; brigades
into position by evening

T3-073

Sound
Sound carries best
downwind (north/north-
northeast) under a light S-
SSW wind; humid
stratified air can enhance
propagation; acoustic
shadows occurred at other
Civil War battles; smoke
drifts NNE and pools in
low ground. No sourced
audibility distances at
Gettysburg.

Table notes
Rows T3-001 to T3-015 carry the July 2 evening and night context from B.2.2. Rows from T3-016 are July 3 and later.
Standing state-of-the-world rows (works at dawn, the Angle wall, fence state, Bliss, Sherfy, Leister) are anchored at the hour the state matters for the animation, with the damage window in the
note column. The time-keyed schedule for fences, buildings and hospitals in B.8 is derived from these rows.
Night rows marked 
plausible-procedural
 (T3-142, T3-143, T3-166) are the only places where the table generates behaviour the sources do not state. They are placeholders that B.10 and Part E
must replace with sourced detail if the primary-source library supplies any.
Conflict highlights for this period
Item
Range of values and sources
Preferred animation anchor (labelled)
Rows
Cannonade start
About 13:00 (NPS-H3, ESH, Cabell, W-PC); Eshleman
puts the signal between 13:00 and 14:00. Signal guns:
two of Miller’s Washington Artillery guns at the Peach
Orchard (NPS-H3, ESH) vs Whitworths on Oak Ridge
(HN-THUNDER).
13:00, Miller’s guns (inferred; majority)
T3-074, T3-075
Cannonade end
About 14:00 (Hess, essentially over); 14:30 (Sears;
Hunt’s report); about 15:00 (Coddington, Wikipedia,
ABT). Duration 45 minutes to over 2 hours; Wert: one
hour most reliable; nearly 2 hours (NPS handbook;
Cabell); about 1.25 hours at II Corps (Hazard); 13:00-
15:00 (Wheeler).
Full exchange 13:00-13:40; Union slowdown from about
13:30-14:00; fire fading by 14:30; quiet to step-off
(inferred)
T3-084, T3-091, T3-094, T3-096
Confederate guns
115 (Hunt’s newspaper figure: 60 Longstreet, 55 Hill);
100-120 bearing (Hunt); 130-140 in line (de Trobriand);
about 140 (NPS-H2); 138-179 (StrategyPage; Pendleton
nearly 150; Alexander 179 incl. about two dozen
howitzers held back; at least 142 fired; reasoned 142-
155); 150-170 (most); 159 (Eicher et al.); 164 (Trudeau);
over 160 (Symonds); about 170 (Clark; Pfanz); Law
claimed 300 on both ridges. About 80 ANV guns did not
fire.
Fire 142-155 guns; carry 115-179 in the register
(inferred)
T3-069, T3-075
Union guns
About 80 answering (W-PC, NPS-H3, History Is Now);
up to 120 deployed (ABT-ART); about 106 on the ridge,
about 85 briefly engaged, about 136 in reserve
(StrategyPage); 34 Union guns disabled (W-CRIDGE).
About 80 counter-battery guns after a 15-minute hold;
100-120 on the ridge (inferred)
T3-084, T3-088
Charge step-off
14:00 (W-PC); near 15:00 (ABT-PC, W-D3); 15:00-15:30
(ABT map); about 13:30 if Eshleman’s ‘about 30 minutes
after the signal guns’ is literal. A 14:00 step-off cannot
follow a 14:30 ceasefire.
About 15:00 (inferred). Likely one source reusing an
adjacent anchor time.
T3-100, T3-103
Advance distance and strength
Nearly one mile vs about 0.75 mile; front shrinks from
about a mile to about 2,200 ft; about 12,500 men
(10,500-13,500); brigades 9, 11 or 9-11; Pickett’s three
brigades about 5,400 (‘one third’ vs 43%).
12,500 men, 0.75-1 mile (inferred)
T3-101, T3-103
Men over the wall
Armistead about 150 (NPS-H5); perhaps 250 (ABT-PC);
2,500-3,000 crossing in one estimate (W-PC).
Show a small band of about 150-250 at the Copse with a
larger surge to the wall; keep 2,500-3,000 as an outer
bound (inferred)
T3-112
Charge casualties
Pickett 2,655; Pettigrew about 2,700; Trimble 885; sum
6,240 (ABT breakdown); with Wilcox about 200 and
Lang about 400 gives 6,840; headline total 6,555
(>=1,123 killed, 4,019 wounded, 3,750 captured);
Union about 1,500. Pickett’s field-officer losses: 26 of
40 or 15.
Carry 6,555 as headline, 6,240-6,840 as range
T3-122, T3-123
Battle casualties
Union 23,049 vs 23,055; Confederate 28,063 (ABT) vs
23,231 (Busey and Martin) vs up to 28,000; dead about
8,000 killed outright vs 8,900 on the field Jul 4 vs 3,100
Union and 4,700 Confederate battle deaths; wounded
14,193 Union and 6,802 Confederate (Letterman) vs
14,501 and 12,355 (NPS essay).
Use Busey and Martin for Confederate totals, Letterman
for wounded (inferred)
T3-150, T3-219
East Cavalry Field
Stuart on Cress Ridge about 11:00; fighting begins ‘just
before noon’ (HN-CAV), about 13:00 (W-ECF), about
14:00-15:00 (ST-GREGG, W-D3, ABT-CUSTER); lasts
about 40 minutes; Hampton wounded by saber in the
head (W-ECF) vs hand (Hampton).
Primary anchor 13:00 with alternate 15:00 flagged
(inferred)
T3-063, T3-079, T3-083, T3-085

Arrival about 13:00; charges about 15:00 (Wikipedia) vs
about 17:00 (HN-CAV); Farnsworth killed after Pickett’s
repulse (Wikipedia) vs overlapping it (HN-CAV); fourth
of five attacks (W-D3).
15:00-17:30 window; 17:00 more plausible (inferred)
T3-078, T3-109, T3-120

Union works start: night of Jul 1-2 (DMNA) vs about

rounds 227,000 vs 250,000.
Animate Jul 3: return 02:00-04:00, guns 04:30, attack
about 05:00, fight to 11:00 (inferred)

daylight (Hunt).
Muhlenberg and Kinzie at 01:00, Rigby by dawn
(inferred)

State schedule that must be correct
1. Night Jul 2 to dawn Jul 3 (T3-001 to T3-035), NIGHT
The Culp’s Hill night fight by muzzle flash; Steuart in
the lower works; the XII Corps return in the dark
(02:00-04:00 window); Meade’s council at the Leister
house at midnight; batteries posted on the Baltimore
Pike, Powers Hill and McAllister Hill; Hunt’s reserve
batteries going up; II Corps men at the wall; surgeons
at lantern-lit tables (Spangler, Weikert); ambulances; a
95%-lit moon rising 20:38 and crossing the south; camp
fires and ammunition resupply behind the lines as
plausible-procedural. All visible but dimmer.
Daylight lighting; any rail or earth reinforcement on
Cemetery Ridge presented as documented; the XII
Corps works at present-day (rebuilt) profile.
Greene’s works about 5 ft high, log and stone, traverse
built; lower-hill works in Confederate hands; Angle wall
already present (pre-existing farm wall); Little Round
Top stone breastworks growing; Bliss and Sherfy
buildings still standing.
2. Culp’s Hill and morning, 04:03 to about 11:30 (T3-
035 to T3-066)
Dawn at 04:36 with overcast, humid air and light south
wind; artillery bombardment 04:30 and 05:30; Steuart’s
attack and three failed waves; Greene’s rotation of
regiments to reload; Pardee Field and the 147th PA
charge; Colgrove’s 2nd Mass and 27th Ind frontal
attack; Confederate withdrawal across Rock Creek at
about 11:00; Jennie Wade killed about 08:00; Bliss
buildings burned (morning); Taney farmhouse shelled;
sniper duels in town; Stuart’s signal guns at 11:00;
Pleasonton’s 08:00 orders; Hunt inspecting at 10:00;
Meade with Warren on Little Round Top.
Pickett’s division in the open before noon (not on the
field at daylight); Spencer carbines on any cavalry other
than the 5th and part of the 6th Michigan.
Culp’s Hill works damaged by shell and bullet fire
(inferred); Bliss house and barn burning then ruined;
Taney farm damaged; Rummel fences intact until 13:00.
3. Midday approach and cavalry, 11:30 to 13:00 (T3-063
to T3-073, T3-078 to T3-085)
Both ridges hot and quiet; Confederate infantry massed
west of the Emmitsburg Road and in Spangler’s Woods;
Merritt leaving Emmitsburg at noon; Stuart on Cress
Ridge; Farnsworth’s brigade and Merritt moving to the
Bushman farm area; Confederate gun lines lengthened
to about two miles.
Stuart and Custer fighting before about 13:00 without
the flagged alternate; Union troops digging new rail
works on Cemetery Ridge.
Sun high (alt 69 deg at 13:00), 73 to 87 F, light S-SSW
wind, dusty dry roads; Emmitsburg Road fences intact
and post-and-rail on both sides.
4. Cannonade, about 13:00 to 14:30-15:00 (T3-074 to
T3-099)
Miller’s two signal guns; a rolling barrage of 142-155
guns; the Union 15-minute hold, then about 80 guns;
Hunt’s slowdown, Hancock’s objection, batteries
withdrawn and replaced; Cushing’s limbers exploding;
Leister house hit, 17 dead horses, Meade moving to
Powers Hill; the Granite Schoolhouse hospital moving;
gunners with sponge, worm, rammer, vent-pricking and
fuze cutting; Confederate rounds bursting long into the
Union rear; low smoke pooling and drifting north-
northeast; Eshleman’s Miller guns forward and being
hit; East Cavalry Field fighting in parallel from about
13:00 with Spencer-armed 5th Michigan.
A clean sky or clear sight lines across the valley;
Confederate fire concentrated on the Angle only; Union
infantry charging.
Smoke, 87 F, heat haze; fences along the Leister farm
burned; Sherfy barn possibly burning (flagged window);
Rummel barn with loopholes and fence damage after
13:00.
5. The charge, about 15:00 to 16:30 (T3-100 to T3-127)
Alexander’s note; Pickett’s ‘shall I advance’; formation
of about 12,500 men; the advance under flank fire from
Little Round Top and Cemetery Hill; the 8th Ohio on
Pettigrew’s flank; Stannard’s wheel; the Emmitsburg
Road fence delay (15:30-16:15); canister at about 100
yd; Cushing’s double canister and death; Armistead
crossing the wall near the Copse and falling mortally
wounded; Webb’s 69th PA and the 72nd PA; Hancock
wounded; flags piled at the wall; retreat across the
fields; South Cavalry Field charges in parallel; Fairfield
action.
Pickett’s men crossing intact fences; Wilcox and Lang
arriving before the main assault broke; Union rail
works.
Emmitsburg Road fences splintered and breached; Bliss
ruins; Codori house and barn; Copse trees under 10 ft;
wall height 2-3 ft; 87 F, heat index 98-105 F, sun in
Union faces at az 259.
6. Evening and night Jul 3, 16:30 to 24:00 (T3-124 to T3-
144)
Confederates falling back to Seminary Ridge;
thunderstorm nearby about 18:00 and wet ground;
sunset 19:32; Armistead at Spangler about 20:00;
hospital night work at every site; moonrise 21:16
behind thin cloud; wounded and dead between the lines
(sources silent); Lee’s order to withdraw at midnight.
Daylight-level lighting after 20:04; an invented truce for
recovering the wounded.
Surface wetness 0.3 after the storm; Sherfy barn
burned if the flagged window resolves that way; Leister
house and barn as aid station.
7. July 4 (T3-145 to T3-167)
Imboden at 01:00; Confederate ambulance trains
leaving by Cashtown and Fairfield; steady cold rain;
Plum Run overrunning its banks; townspeople and
farmers burying the dead; wounded in the open at
Spangler; Kilpatrick moving out at 10:00; the pontoon
bridge at Falling Waters destroyed; Brinton’s supplies
in the evening; Hill’s corps, then Longstreet, then Ewell
marching down the Fairfield Road in rain after dark;
Monterey Pass fighting in darkness and rain.
Sunshine; dry roads; stationary Union army without any
action (sources are silent: use flagged generated
behaviour).
Rain 1.39 in total; mud; dim near-dark moon (88-77%
lit, hidden by cloud); fires of burning horse carcasses
(3,000+ horses, burned with kerosene where possible).
8. July 5 to 14 retreat and pursuit (T3-168 to T3-208)
Capehart’s charge at 03:00; Greencastle civilians
attacking wheels; Meade learning of the withdrawal;
Smithsburg, Granite Hill, Cunningham’s Cross Roads;
Imboden’s wounded train at flooded Williamsport and
the Wagoners’ Fight; Hagerstown house-to-house;
Dry roads on Jul 5-8; a hot July sky (Jacobs never above
76 F, Jul 4-8).
Potomac above 15 ft until about Jul 12; mud; rain Jul 4-
7.

9. Aftermath, Jul 15 to Nov 20 (T3-209 to T3-219)

One table per army and corps or division. Dates are 1863. Night positions are given only where the notes give them; otherwise “gap”. Conflicting times are shown as ranges with source keys, and B.2.5
gives the defaults.

B.2.5 Conflict register
Every disputed time or number found in the timeline notes is listed once below. The recommended default is the value the animation uses when the viewer is on its default setting. Rule: no alternative
is deleted; every value in the “All values” column remains selectable in the viewer, and the chosen value is shown in the on-screen source panel with the rejected ones. A default is chosen for coherence
(earliest consistent with the better-sourced notes), not because an alternative is disproved. Times are local; the Union and Confederate clocks differed by about 20 minutes (see B.2.2), and no row
below corrects for that.
