Notes on the register:
Items C-001 to C-047 apply to the whole battle; row IDs in B.2.2 and the day-1 and day-3 timeline sections cite them in their conflict column.
Where the default is a range (for example C-034, C-046), the viewer shows the range, not a single figure.
Where the default equals the earliest value, the reason is the gating rule in B.2.4: a unit is never shown before the earliest source-supported time.
C-042 note: the default shows the earlier time; it keeps the two sources apart rather than averaging them.
Animation gating rules
A unit is not drawn on the field before the earliest time in its “first on the field” cell. Where a range is given, the default is the middle or the better-sourced value (see B.2.5) and the earliest value
is selectable.
Units listed as never or only partly on the field are not drawn, or are drawn only in the detached role stated.
Unknown arrival times are open gaps: the unit appears no earlier than the next documented event that required it (for example a brigade named in a report), and carries an “inferred” tier tag.
Stuart’s cavalry brigades are gated to the evening of Jul 2 by default for their arrival at Lee’s lines (Hampton’s rear guard and the Union cavalry at Hunterstown, about 16:30 on Jul 2, are exempt
from that gate and are shown at Hunterstown on their own rows); Pickett’s division is gated to the morning of Jul 3 by default.
Hospitals and trains follow their own rows in B.2.2 and the day-3 section.
Open gaps in the ledger
Official Records volumes were not retrieved; unit movements rely on the secondary notes and the NPS report pages.
Per-regiment arrival times for II, III and XII Corps.
Artillery Reserve arrival and positions on Jul 1-2.
Kilpatrick’s and Buford’s night positions.
Confederate rear-area positions, Pickett’s march, and the night positions of Early, Rodes and Johnson for Jun 29-30.
Night positions for I Corps artillery battalions other than Alexander’s.
Where Jenkins was wounded; Imboden’s Jul 2 position.
Leads to verify
Official Records Series I, vol. 27, parts 1-3, for corps and division arrival times.
Longstreet and Alexander reports for the Jul 2 countermarch.
Pickett’s divisional report, if any survives, for the Jul 2-3 movement.
Unit histories for II Corps regiment arrival times.

B.3 Order of battle
Source for all lists: 
Union OOB
 and 
Confederate OOB
 on Wikipedia, as summarized by a fetch tool, which may contain small slips (the researcher flags VI Corps, Cavalry Corps and Howe’s missing first
brigade). Regimental commanders were stripped from the retrieved text. (k) killed, (w) wounded, (mw) mortally wounded. 
Verify every line against the Official Records Series I vol. 27 and
Busey and Martin before use.
 Rough counts by the researcher (not sourced): about 270 Union regiments plus about 65 batteries; about 170 Confederate regiments and battalions plus about 65
batteries in about 15 artillery battalions.
Army of the Potomac (MG George G. Meade)
Army staff (from a separate fetch, not re-verified): Chief of Staff Butterfield, Chief of Artillery Hunt, Chief of Engineers Warren, Chief Quartermaster Ingalls, Medical Director Letterman, Provost
Marshal Patrick, Engineer Brigade Benham.

B.4 Commanders, lieutenant to general: what is documented about appearance
The headline finding
Appearance is documented in the notes for only a handful of men.
 The OOB names are thorough to brigade level and partial at regiment; no company-level (captain or lieutenant) names exist in
these sources except notable battery commanders and individuals with monuments or books. The user’s requirement “every commander, LT to general, with correct appearance” is therefore 
not
currently satisfiable from the sources reached
, and for many individuals may never be (see B.5).

B.5 How many participants can be individually identified
Honest answer from the notes:
 nearly all enlisted men are nameable in principle through state rosters and muster rolls, but no single Gettysburg-wide roster exists, details for most are name,
company, rank and enlistment data, and the Union record is far better than the Confederate. Gettysburg NMP publishes some state rosters (Louisiana, Michigan, North Carolina, Pennsylvania,
Virginia) that are “primarily lists of names with few additional details” and holds no individual service or pension records. Casualty books: Busey and Busey, 
Union Casualties at Gettysburg
 (2013) and
Confederate Casualties at Gettysburg
 (2016); Busey, 
These Honored Dead
 (1988); Krick, 
The Gettysburg Death Roster
 (1993); Coco, 
Gettysburg’s Confederate Dead
 (2003). Databases: the NPS Civil
War Soldiers and Sailors System, the American Civil War Research Database (subscription), the 33-volume 
Roster of Union Soldiers
, the 16-volume 
Roster of Confederate Soldiers
, and NARA service
and pension records (
NPS
). The roughly 45,000 casualties are the best-documented subset (note author’s inference). 
No source gave a count of individually identified participants, a percentage
with photographs, or a count of company officers.
 The researcher’s arithmetic suggests on the order of 10,000+ company officers (not sourced; actual regimental strengths were far below
authorized). Physical descriptions exist in Union enlistment rolls and some Confederate records for some men only.
B.6 Uniforms, equipment and weapons
This section answers requirement 3 and requirement 10 of the owner’s list: what each unit wore, the different kinds of soldier inside each unit (including sharpshooters), and which rifle each unit
carried. It covers infantry, sharpshooters, officers and support troops, and it holds the small arms dictionary, the unit-to-weapon table, the ammunition and drill facts, and the per-soldier kit manifest
that the animation will be built from. Artillery crews and cavalry dress and horse furniture have their own subsections elsewhere in B.6; this part cross-references them only where weapons overlap
(carbines, sabers, revolvers).
Nothing here is built. This is research and planning. The research notes behind this section are thinner than the owner’s standard in several places (regiment-level arms returns, state-by-state
Confederate issue, Union regimental dress). Those places are marked as open gaps and listed again, with the source that would settle each one, in B.6.13.
B.6.1 How to read this section
Tiers.
 Every fact or default carries one of three tiers, the same ones used in B.0.
Tier
Meaning in B.6
Example
documented
A specific source states it for that unit or item
114th PA wore Zouave dress; the 12th NJ monument is inscribed “Buck &
Ball Calibre .69”
inferred
Reasoned from documented facts, with the reasoning stated
Hays’ Louisiana regiments should be dressed as ordinary Army of Northern
Virginia infantry because Wheat’s battalion was gone by 1862
plausible-procedural
Generated to fill a gap, seeded and reproducible, and consistent with the
documented context
The coat mix of a Union regiment for which no dress source exists
Where a research note was tagged SOURCED it maps to documented, INFERRED maps to inferred, and GAP is not a tier: a gap is listed as an open item with the source that would close it. A source-
quality flag is carried beside the tier when the only support is a forum post, a blog, or a Wikipedia table (marked “weak” below). Weak support does not upgrade a row to documented in the product;
the row stays visibly flagged.
Kit manifest.
 A kit manifest is the per-soldier record from which the 3D model, textures, weapon, accoutrements and pose set are assembled. It is generated from the unit’s specification (the unit kit
spec) by seeded random draws, never hand-placed. Each field in the manifest carries its own tier, so the viewer’s provenance overlay can show, for any soldier, which parts of his appearance are
documented and which were procedurally filled. B.6.12 defines the fields.
The appearance standard.
 Every soldier must be historically correct in dress and equipment for his unit, his branch of service and the date. The target is correctness of type, not a likeness of a real
individual. A private in the 20th Maine is not a portrait of a named man; he is a plausible 20th Maine private on that day, with a coat, hat, trousers, shoes, weapon and gear that the evidence allows for
that regiment and that no evidence forbids. Where the evidence is silent about a regiment, the soldier is generated from the documented defaults for his army, corps and branch and is tagged
plausible-procedural. Named commanders are the only exception where individual appearance is pursued (B.6.7), and even there only what a source states is documented.
Date gating.
 Items change over the campaign. A unit that arrived on July 2 is not present before it arrived (B.2.4 holds the arrival ledger). Weapons are date-gated too: for example, Spencers appear
in Michigan hands from June 30 at Hanover and on July 3 at East Cavalry Field, never on Buford’s line on July 1.
B.6.2 Union infantry standard dress and kit
Clothing
Item
What the notes support
Tier / source
Regulation concept
Dark blue coat, lighter trousers, black hat; enforcement was loose and
supply shaped what men wore
documented: [Wiki-UnionUnif]
Frock coat
Dark blue wool, nine front buttons, light blue piping at collar and cuffs;
single-breasted for company officers (7-9 buttons)
documented: [3rdUS], [Wiki-UnionUnif]
Sack coat
Loose, four brass buttons (eagle), fall collar, one inside left breast pocket;
shade from medium to dark blue; lined for volunteers, unlined for regulars
(reenactor page notes many were lined anyway)
documented: [3rdUS], [Wiki-UnionUnif]
Which dominated
By 1863 the sack coat and kepi dominated in the field; frock coats stayed
with early units, officers and the Iron Brigade
inferred from the two sources above; the retrieved notes give no count by
corps
Trousers
Sky blue kersey for enlisted men and regimental officers (high waist, no
pleats); dark blue for generals and staff; the early dark blue trousers were
reversed in December 1861
documented: [Wiki-UnionUnif], [3rdUS]
NCO trouser stripe
Infantry dark blue; sergeants 1.5 in, corporals 0.5 in
documented: [Wiki-UnionUnif]
Fading
Trousers drift from sky blue toward gray-blue with wear
inferred
Shirts
Issue shirt a coarse wool pullover with three buttons, gray most common,
then navy or white; men also wore homemade white linen or plaid flannel;
some early regiments had red firefighter-style overshirts
documented: [Wiki-UnionUnif]. The 3rd US reenactor standard disfavors
white cotton and plaid as issue; that is their rule, not period evidence
Neckwear
Stocks usually discarded for cotton neckerchiefs
documented: [Wiki-UnionUnif]
Shoes
Black leather Jefferson-pattern brogans, rough or smooth side out; no boots
for enlisted men
documented: [3rdUS]. A Wikipedia mention of “Jeff Davis” boots looks like
a sourcing oddity; low confidence, not used
Forage cap / kepi (1858 McDowell pattern)
Dark blue wool, leather visor and chin strap, black or brown liner; infantry
bugle, regiment number and company letter on the crown front; styles
range from tight to the tall floppy “bummer” cap
documented: [3rdUS], [Wiki-UnionUnif]
Hardee (M1858) hat
Black felt; infantry brim pinned up on the left (cavalry and artillery on the
right); brass eagle; sky blue infantry hat cord; bugle device; black ostrich
feather for enlisted dress hat; many found it hot and heavy and preferred
caps or slouch hats
documented: [Wiki-Hardee], [3rdUS]
Civilian hats
Black slouch, pork-pie and bowler hats were common
documented: [Wiki-UnionUnif]
Overcoat
Sky blue, caped, single-breasted for infantry; officers’ dark blue with black
braid. Likely left in wagons in the July heat (not sourced)
documented (coat); the July usage is a gap
Rain gear
Gum (rubber) blankets used as groundsheets and rain gear by infantry;
rubber ponchos more for cavalry and artillery
documented: [Wiki-UnionUnif], [NPS-Accout]
Leggings
Issued to regulars, sharpshooters, Zouaves and the Iron Brigade but soon
discarded
documented: [Wiki-UnionUnif]. Whether any were still worn on July 1-3 is
open
Gettysburg Total War animation plan, version 2 | page 66 of 144


=== PAGE 67 ===
Branch colors
 (shoulder boards, chevrons, hat cord, trouser stripes): infantry sky blue; artillery scarlet; cavalry yellow; engineers yellow chevrons; medical and ordnance dark blue boards with
crimson trouser stripes; sharpshooters green. Documented: [Wiki-UnionUnif].
Rank on enlisted men:
 chevrons on both sleeves in branch color (sky blue for infantry). First sergeants and musicians wore the rectangular sword-belt plate ([3rdUS]). Infantry musicians had
“birdcage” chest braid in the facing color ([Wiki-UnionUnif]); nothing else on drummers and fifers is sourced.
Sashes.
 Crimson sashes for officers and sergeants were regulation, but Hancock’s letter to the Cyclorama painters indicates sashes were generally not worn in the field at Gettysburg ([CWTalk-
Hancock]). Default: no sashes on the line; sashes only on deliberately flashy officers or Zouave sashes where the unit’s dress calls for them (inferred).
Default Union infantry mix (when a regiment has no unit-specific source)
All rows are inferred from the sources above and instantiated as plausible-procedural draws. The manifest stores the numeric weights; the notes provide no counts, so the weights are tuning
parameters with no historical basis and must be replaced the moment a sourced count is found.
Coat: most men in sack coats, a minority in frock coats.
Headgear: most in kepis, with a smaller share of slouch (civilian) hats and a few Hardee hats; officers more often in slouch hats.
Trousers: sky blue faded toward gray-blue; a few replacement pairs in dark blue or civilian cloth are allowed.
Shirt: gray wool, with white and plaid minorities.
Shoes: black brogans only.
Corps XI German regiments (26th WI, 82nd IL, 45th, 54th, 58th, 68th, 119th, 134th, 154th NY, 75th PA): standard blue with kepis, slouch hats and some Hardee hats; crescent badge. Inferred; no
unit source retrieved.
Corps badges
The scheme: a shape per corps and a color per division (1st red, 2nd white, 3rd blue). A fourth division, where one existed, used green (II, III, VI Corps); the XV Corps used yellow; the XIII Corps had
no badge; the XIX reversed the scheme ([CivilWarNews-Badges], [Wiki-Badges]). At Gettysburg no corps had a fourth division in the notes’ order of battle, so green does not occur (inferred; III Corps
had only two divisions). Badges were adopted by order of 21 March 1863, but that is an adoption date, not an issue date. Issue is recorded in the 14th Indiana just before Chancellorsville; many
soldiers made or altered their own, and sutlers supplied many ([AuthCamp-Badges]). Which regiments actually wore a badge in July 1863 is an open gap. Where worn: top of the kepi, left side of the
hat, or over the left breast ([CivilWarNews-Badges], [Wiki-Badges]). One documented example of a hand-made badge: a sky blue kersey square worn on its point as a lozenge in the 3rd Division of the
III Corps (124th NY) ([AuthCamp-Badges]).

Division-color assignment is the documented rule applied to the order of battle, so each cell is inferred. The Katcher table also marks some shapes as never officially adopted; which ones is not stated
in the retrieved text, so the animation cannot yet tell which of these were purely unofficial. Sources that would settle the whole table: Dyer’s Compendium, Bates, Katcher’s corps-badge tables, the
NPS badge pages, and the New York State Military Museum flag and relic collections.
Product rule for badges.
 Badges appear on a minority of soldiers in a regiment, at the three documented positions, with homemade variance (size, color shade, slightly wrong shape) on some of
them. A badge is never added to a unit in a corps where its shape is unsourced without the product tagging that soldier’s badge field plausible-procedural.

Knapsack practice by unit at Gettysburg
The notes show no army-wide rule. Practice varied by unit and by day. All rows come from one forum compilation ([CWTalk-Knapsack]) unless noted, so each is documented only at that source’s
reliability (weak, but it names units and events). A veteran-reenactor discussion agrees there was no firm Gettysburg-specific rule and that dropped packs were often lost and regretted ([AuthCamp-
Knapsack]).

Rules for the animation.
 1. Units named above get exactly the pack state in the table (documented, weak source flagged). 2. For every other Union unit: mixed state (inferred). Most men with
haversack, canteen and a rolled blanket or gum blanket over the shoulder; some with a knapsack on. A unit ordered into action on arrival gets a pile of dropped packs behind its line as a unit-specific
event, not as a blanket rule. The pack pile is an object with a position, an owner unit and a time of dropping, so that the 97th NY pile can later “vanish” (lost). 3. The pack field in each soldier’s manifest
is therefore one of: knapsack on; blanket roll only; no roll (haversack and canteen only); pack dropped (and the pile id); pack used as cover (July 3, 1st MN, 12th MA, 147th NY).
B.6.3 Union distinctive units
The brief’s corrections are applied in the table. In short: the 5th NY (Duryee’s Zouaves) was not at Gettysburg (mustered out 14 May 1863; its sister 165th NY was not there either); the 140th NY wore
regulation blue (Zouave dress issued January 1864); the 146th NY wore Zouave style from 3 June 1863; the 114th PA wore Zouave dress; the 155th PA’s Zouave dress was issued in 1864 and is not
Gettysburg-era despite its monument.
Regiment locations come from the order of battle in B.3 (the Wikipedia order of battle, as extracted in the notes).
Unit (corps, brigade)
What is documented
What is open

Unit-by-unit realism rule.
 A Zouave outfit means the 114th PA (certain) and the 146th NY (certain since 3 June); the 44th NY is uncertain; the 140th NY, 5th NY and 155th PA never receive one at
Gettysburg (inferred from the sourced dates).
B.6.4 Confederate infantry dress and kit
Jackets and the Richmond Depot typology
The typology is from a researcher (Les Jensen), not the Depot’s own labels, and its dating is tentative ([9thVA]).
Type
Description
Gettysburg-appropriate?
Source
Type I
Waist-length, standing collar, shoulder straps, nine-
button front typical, tape or piping on collar, straps and
cuffs (infantry dark/French blue or black), osnaburg
lining with inside breast pocket; photographed by
February 1862; no certain originals survive. A possible
tailor’s copy belonged to Sgt E.C.N. Green, 47th NC,
killed at Gettysburg (cadet gray wool, dark-blue tape,
eight script “I” buttons, no belt loops)
Early type; rare by July 1863
[9thVA]
Type II
Issued from spring 1862 to at least fall 1864; the most
common Army of Northern Virginia issue jacket from
fall 1862 to summer 1864. Six-piece body, standing
collar, shoulder straps, belt loops, osnaburg lining,
mostly untrimmed (some partial red piping on straps),
cotton or wool jean cloth or cassimere, nine-button front
standard (eight common, six or seven occur), no cuff
buttons
Yes: the default
[9thVA]; Wikipedia Richmond Clothing Depot (v1 notes:
jeans, satinette or cassimere)
Transitional fall/winter 1863
Dark blue-gray kersey jackets with Type II details, often
without belt loops (Capt. Marsh, 4th NC; Pvt. Ramsey,
17th VA)
No: postdates the battle
[9thVA]
Type III
No shoulder straps or belt loops; dark blue-gray English
kersey; first issued late winter 1863 or early spring
1864 (the article is internally ambiguous on the year;
the Wikipedia Depot page conflicts on whether partly
1863)
No for most men. Do not use except as a rare flagged
item
[9thVA], Wikipedia Depot page
Related Depot facts: depot trousers were cadet-gray jeans from late 1862, blue-gray European cloth from 1863; the Crenshaw Wool Mill and Tredegar burned mid-1863 (Wikipedia Depot page, v1
notes, secondary). Eastern jackets: Wikipedia says Columbus (GA) jackets had breast pockets and Richmond jackets did not, which apparently contradicts the Type I/II description of an inside breast
pocket; the likely reconciliation is an outside versus an inside pocket, but that is unconfirmed. The Columbus Depot supplied the West and is mostly irrelevant to the Army of Northern Virginia.
Other facts: Benjamin’s 1861 guidance was gray jacket, blue/gray/brown trousers, any slouch hat or cap, any footwear. By 1863 waist-length cadet gray or butternut shell jackets were generally worn,
and frock coats still appeared in Gettysburg photographs. Dyes ranged from bright cadet gray to sumac/logwood shades that faded to butternut. Deep-South jean-cloth jackets typically had five to
seven buttons with variable trim. All from [Wiki-CSUnif] (Wikipedia lead, secondary, used as a lead).
Trousers, hats, shoes
Trousers: early sky blue; later gray, brown or medium blue in US or civilian pattern; men used their own when issue failed; NCO stripe 1 1/4 in cotton. [Wiki-CSUnif]. Conflict: the 1861
regulations give a dark blue stripe for infantry NCOs and regimental officers’ trouser stripe in dark blue cloth ([CSA-Regs]), against Wikipedia’s “light/sky blue”. Carried as a conflict; the product
shows stripes on a minority of NCO trousers and picks the regulation color with the conflict flagged.
Hats: slouch hats (wool or felt, brown/gray/black), straw, plain caps; the prescribed kepi was rare outside private purchase; cavalry often Hardee hats; straw hats allowed for artillery in summer.
[Wiki-CSUnif]
Footwear: “Jefferson bootees/brogans” the standard issue. [Wiki-CSUnif]
Buttons: state buttons common, regimental-numbered buttons rare, “I” infantry buttons common among enlisted men, US eagle buttons from captured stock used; captured US belt plates often
worn upside down. [Wiki-CSUnif]
Gettysburg Total War animation plan, version 2 | page 69 of 144


=== PAGE 70 ===
Rank chevrons: three for sergeants, two for corporals; sash red for infantry and artillery, yellow for cavalry. [Wiki-CSUnif]
Texas: units wore US blue uniforms seized at San Antonio in 1861 as late as 1863 (Wikipedia lead, thin). Not applied to Hood’s brigade without a better source (inferred caution).
Fabrics: kersey, broadcloth, jeans, satinette, cassimere; yellow dye scarce so cavalry used buff. [Wiki-CSUnif]
North Carolina (weak forum only): from 1863 the depot jacket differs from the early-war sack coat; the state provided caps and sometimes imported hats; equipment mostly from the Confederate
central government; eastern NC units were supplied regularly, western regiments (29th, 39th, 58th, 60th) less so; the 27th NC possibly wore English import cloth by October 1863. [AuthCamp-
NC], weak.
The shoe legend caveat
The claim that the battle began over a search for shoes first appears in 1877, when Henry Heth wrote that he heard shoes could be had in Gettysburg. The American Battlefield Trust notes there were
no shoe factories near Gettysburg and that roads brought the armies there ([ABT-Myths]). The animation must not treat the shoe story as a cause of the battle, and must not mass-barefoot the army on
that basis. The notes do not quantify shoe or barefoot proportions and give no eyewitness count, so footwear is Jefferson bootee/brogan for the default, with a small barefoot or worn-out minority
generated plausible-procedural and flagged (the reasoning being the general hard campaign, not a sourced share). Fremantle’s diary (on Project Gutenberg) is the named primary source to read for
the army’s appearance in June-July 1863.

By 1863 the army had captured thousands of Federal arms and equipment

rifles were common alongside CS-made gear

sharpshooters with ammunition
documented: [NPS-Blackford]
Generic load

gum blanket, housewife, over 40 lb with rifle
documented: [NPS-Accout]
Gaps: mess gear (tin cup, skillet, frying pan) and how the troops marched and fought are not sourced; museum collections (ACWM, Smithsonian) were not reached.
Default Confederate infantry (plausible-procedural, built from the above):
 Type II-style gray or butternut jean shell jacket (nine buttons, shoulder straps, no trim, belt loops) for most men; a
minority in civilian coats, frock coats, or captured Union blue items; slouch hats (kepis only for officers and private purchase); mixed trousers in gray, brown, butternut or captured blue; brogans;
blanket roll over one shoulder, haversack, canteen, cartridge box (CS or captured US) and cap pouch; no knapsack for most. Wide color variation inside one regiment is correct. Dark blue-gray
strapless Type III jackets are barred except as a rare flagged transitional item (inferred).

Louisiana correction (brief).
 Wheat’s battalion was gone by 1862, so no Zouave or “Tiger” costume is allowed on any Gettysburg Louisiana soldier.
Flags status.
 Documented: the Army of Northern Virginia square battle flag (St Andrew’s cross design proposed by William Porcher Miles; Gen. Joseph Johnston suggested the square shape; the first
120 silk flags were issued in November 1861 in a pink or rose field with a yellow border; eight later variants include one cotton issue and seven wool issues in deep red; 12 stars) ([FOTW]). Open: the
Gettysburg-specific pattern (the 1863 Richmond-Depot-made bunting issue; the Richmond Depot used captured Norfolk bunting with white or orange edging and cut the cross width from 8 in to 5 in on
later issues, per the v1 notes), sizes by arm, state and regimental colors, Georgia/Texas/Hoke flags, headquarters flags and bearer practice. Leads: the ACWM object record

bearer down), tier plausible-procedural until the regimental pattern is confirmed.
B.6.6 Sharpshooters on both sides
Berdan’s 1st and 2nd United States Sharpshooters (III Corps, 1st Division, 2nd Brigade, Ward)
Uniform.
 Dark green caps, coats, trousers and leather gaiters; no shiny brass ([MHN]). Green frock coats and trousers with black buttons ([AR-Sharpshooting]). A Smithsonian frock coat (c. 1861) is
green wool with the brass buttons replaced by hard black rubber buttons and goes with a green wool forage cap with leather trim ([SI-Berdan]). Early uniform green trousers, frock coats, forage caps
([HistoryNet-Killers]); green boards and chevrons ([Wiki-UnionUnif]); Prussian-style fur-covered knapsacks on a wooden frame ([Wiki-1USS]). 
Conflict:
 Wikipedia says that by the Gettysburg campaign
most men wore modified blue uniforms (not reissued green). The other sources do not date their uniform descriptions to 1863. Both are carried. Default (inferred): mostly green with some blue
replacement items, black-button coats, green kepis and slouch hats; the share is a seeded parameter. Companies came from NY, MI, NH, VT and WI (Company G, Wisconsin, Capt. Edward Drew,
mustered 23 September 1861).
Rifles.
 The Sharps .52 breechloader, about 30 in barrel, double-set triggers on many, sights to 700-800 yd, about 2,000 supplied; Berdan feuded with Chief of Ordnance Ripley; Colt 1855 revolving
rifles were issued first and replaced by Sharps in 1862 (issued from May 1862 per Wikipedia) ([AR-Sharpshooting], [Wiki-1USS]). Wikipedia puts accuracy at about 600 yd and claims 8-10 rounds a
minute. Private target rifles with Malcolm or Morgan James scopes (.36-.45 caliber, up to about 25 lb) were common but Berdan found them impractical; Truman Head (“California Joe”) carried a
scoped rifle of about 32 lb ([AR-Sharpshooting]). Heavy target rifles were kept in wagons, one per company; the two regiments used Sharps rifles ([AR-Guns]). The 2nd USSS is listed with Sharps, a
bowie knife and a Colt pocket revolver ([Wiki-2USS]). Wikipedia also says some carried Whitworth, sporting or target rifles. Colt revolving rifles at Gettysburg: no source, not used. Whether the men
still carried Sharps in July 1863 is stated by most sources but is not directly confirmed for that month in the notes.
Entry test, conflict.
 Ten shots at 200 yd; the group size differs: 5 in. (American Rifleman), a 10-in. target (Gettysburg Daily), “5 in. at 600 ft” (Smithsonian). Treat as unresolved; it affects no scene.
Tactics.
 Groups of four, two firing while two withdraw; tree-to-tree fighting retreat ([MHN]).
What they did at Gettysburg.
 - 
Pitzer’s Woods, July 2 (1st USSS with the 3rd Maine):
 went into the woods (the source says “Berdan’s Woods”; Pitzer’s Woods is the usual NPS name) against Wilcox’s
brigade; about 40 minutes; 19 wounded (6 mortally); 37 lost overall at Gettysburg; Berdan’s horse was shot ([GburgExp-Berdan]). Rounds fired: Berdan’s companies and the 3rd Maine about 95 rounds
per man in about 15 minutes ([AR-Guns]). - 
Slyder farm line, July 2 (2nd USSS):
 about 204 men on a loose line of about 900 yards south of Little Round Top under Maj. Homer Stoughton, posted along
Slyder Lane, then fell back toward Big Round Top and Devil’s Den, pursued by the 15th Alabama; delayed Law’s brigade by about an hour over 400 yards ([MHN], [GburgDaily-2USS]). Because the
ground is thick with boulders, the 2nd’s tactic was skirmish-delay rather than static sniping (inferred). - 
Big Round Top:
 the fall-back toward Big Round Top is documented; some sources say
sharpshooters and 20th Maine skirmishers hid behind Little Round Top and fired on Confederates, but that is one weak secondary source ([GburgExp-Berdan]). - Open: Lt Col Trepp’s role (named only
in a bibliography); the Wisconsin company’s Gettysburg role; the detailed Sharps use at Pitzer’s Woods (standard works: Sword’s article and Marcot’s book).
1st Company Massachusetts Sharpshooters (Andrew Sharpshooters)
Custom muzzleloading target rifles with scopes (makers Hawkins and Morgan James), plus Sharps from after Antietam; mostly swapped W.D. Langdon target rifles for Sharps after Antietam, with some
Merrill rifles remaining ([AR-Guns]). Attached to Hancock’s II Corps on July 2-4. Cleared snipers from the Bliss farm; on July 3 dueled Confederate snipers in the town; during Pickett’s Charge targeted
officers ([Wiki-1MassSS], whose own note says sources are inconsistent in names and spelling). Uniform not described; default is standard blue field dress (plausible-procedural), not Berdan green.
Minnesota Company L
An Army Heritage bibliography states that Company L (Minnesota) was attached to the 1st Minnesota Infantry; the 1st Minnesota note elsewhere lists Co. L Sharps. Treat as a sharpshooter
detachment with Sharps (weak). Many other sharpshooter detachments existed; only this one was identified.
Blackford’s 5th Alabama Battalion (Rodes’ division)
Maj. Eugene Blackford’s own report (17 July 1863) is the key source ([NPS-Blackford]): - July 1: skirmishers tree to tree, repulsed cavalry and a double skirmish line, rallied by bugle, ended the night
on the left of Ramseur’s brigade near the Fairfield road. - July 2: deployed on the Fairfield road from about 9 a.m., then in the edge houses of the town. - July 3: fired at 300-500 yd on artillery,
skirmishers and infantry, averaging more than 200 rounds per man; losses “not more than 20”; ammunition resupplied from cartridge boxes collected in town. - Arms are not named in the report.
Inferred: issue rifled muskets (Enfield or Springfield), not Whitworths, given 200 rounds per man. - The battalion’s uniform is not stated; default is ordinary Army of Northern Virginia infantry dress. A
HistoryNet article on Blackford is a lead not yet read.
Other Confederate sharpshooters, Whitworths, and related
The Confederacy fielded about 16 sharpshooter battalions drawn from regiments; dress was butternut or gray with slouch hats and a mix of issue, civilian and captured clothing. The Enfield
P1853 was the most common prized rifle; also the Enfield musketoon, the Whitworth (.451, hexagonal bore, 36 or 39 in barrels; 100-500 bought; about $1,000 per cased Whitworth with 1,000
rounds, scope about $200 extra) and the Kerr .442; one division got 30 Whitworths and 16 Kerrs ([AR-Sharpshooting], which does not name Georgia or Alabama units).
A popular article gives the Whitworth effective range as about 2,000 yd; this is overstated (Wikipedia gives 800-1,000 yd). Low reliability ([WeAreMighty]).
Whitworth cannons versus Whitworth rifles.
 The Oak Hill marker, via a forum transcription (weak), lists “Two Whitworths, Two 3 Inch Rifles” in Hardaway’s Alabama battery (Hurt’s),
McIntosh’s battalion, on Oak Hill on July 3. The notes infer this is the breech-loading Whitworth cannon, not the sharpshooter rifle; the sources conflate the two. The American Rifleman judges
Whitworth rifle use at Gettysburg unlikely. So no Confederate sharpshooter carries a Whitworth rifle by default; any Whitworth sharpshooter would be a flagged plausible-procedural exception,
and the two guns belong to artillery.
SC sharpshooter battalion: more than 200 rounds each by the morning of July 3 ([AR-Guns]).
Gaps: Wright’s/Georgia, Pegram’s and Wilcox’s sharpshooters; Law’s Alabama sharpshooters at Little Round Top; Confederate sharpshooters at Devil’s Den. The order of battle lists the 3rd
Georgia Sharpshooter Battalion (Wofford’s brigade) and the 1st SC Rifles (McGowan’s), which are named but not described in the notes.
The Devil’s Den photograph, “Home of a Rebel Sharpshooter”
The photograph is staged. It was taken on 6 July 1863, per Frassanito attributed to Timothy O’Sullivan, with Gardner arriving 5 July and later claiming credit. The body of an unidentified Confederate
was moved more than 40 yards to the stone niche (the same body appears in another photograph), a prop rifle was placed (a type a sharpshooter would not use, and it appears in other Gardner
photographs), a blanket under the body may have been used to carry it, and Gardner’s caption (mortal wound from a shell, a November return) is considered implausible. The Library of Congress
record calls the setting a “rocky niche”; the wall was built by Confederates and later rebuilt by the NPS ([Wiki-HomeRebel], [Prologue]). Consequences for the animation: do not stage a “sharpshooter’s
lair” scene on the strength of this photograph; use it as evidence of the terrain and of a body, not of a sniper’s life. Open: the dead man’s clothing is not described in the retrieved sources (LOC record
and Frassanito’s “Gettysburg: A Journey in Time” are the places to look) and the prop rifle’s type is unverified.
B.6.7 Officers and insignia
Union
Frock coat single-breasted with 7-9 buttons for captains and lieutenants; double-breasted with black velvet facings for senior officers and generals, buttons in groups of twos or threes by rank;
shoulder straps (“sardine boxes”); many officers wore sack coats with straps added ([Wiki-UnionUnif]).
Straps: 2nd Lt blank, 1st Lt one gold bar, Captain two bars, Major gold leaf, Lt Col silver leaf, Colonel silver eagle, generals stars ([Wiki-UnionUnif]). Staff often added department initials
between the straps. Generals’ kepi or forage cap with black velvet band; generals and staff in dark blue trousers.
Sword belt rectangular plate; officers’ overcoats dark blue with black braid; crimson sashes regulation but rare in the field at Gettysburg ([CWTalk-Hancock]).
Hancock favored white shirts; his letter to the Cyclorama painters suggests sashes were generally not worn; officers often wore slouch hats ([CWTalk-Hancock]).
Meade on July 3 wore fatigue dress with no shoulder straps, so artillerymen did not recognize him ([GburgExp-Meade]). Meade’s nickname “Old Baldy” refers to his lack of hair, not clearly to the
horse.
Custer: reportedly threw off his hat on July 3 so his men could see his long yellow curls, and his uniform was criticized as gaudy; popular tradition with a Wikipedia citation, source not shown.
Horses Vic and Dandy belong to 1876.
Buford rode an old white horse, “Grey Eagle” ([Wiki-Buford]).
Open (no source retrieved): specific dress of Reynolds, Doubleday, Howard (lost an arm, so an empty sleeve; the notes say Fair Oaks), Sickles, Sykes, Sedgwick, Slocum, Hunt, Pleasonton,
Gibbon, Webb, Hays, Warren, Vincent and Chamberlain; horse furniture; staff aiguillettes. Named leads: LOC carte-de-visite portraits, Troiani and Bradley paintings, horsesoldier.com CDV pages.

Source: [CSA-Regs]. The notes state the sleeve braid as Lt 1, Captain 2, field officers 3, generals 4; the 1st/2nd lieutenant split on braid is not given, so both are shown as the lieutenant’s single braid.
The regulations describe plain braid; Austrian-knot style is what is typically seen (inferred, unsourced). Buttons: general two rows of eight in pairs; colonel and below seven per row. Sashes: general
buff silk net, other infantry officers red silk, cavalry yellow; forage cap with braid rows by rank. Trouser stripes: general two gold lace stripes; staff one gold stripe; regimental infantry dark blue cloth 1
1/4 in. After an early-1864 order officers could buy enlisted clothing, and many wore Type III jackets (post-Gettysburg; not for July 1863) ([9thVA]). By 1863 many field officers wore simple frock coats
or shell jackets with collar insignia only (inferred).
Documented individual dress: 
Pickett
, per a contemporary account, a small blue kepi-style cap, buffed gloves, double-breasted coat with gold buttons, gold spurs, polished boots, riding crop; long
perfumed ringlets and a curling scented beard; black charger “Old Black” ([Wiki-Pickett], Wikipedia secondary). 
Ewell
: about 5 ft 8 in, bald, wooden leg and crutches when dismounted ([Wiki-Ewell]).
Lee
: horse Traveller, a gray gelding with black mane and tail, “somewhat nervous” ([Lee-Letters]). Stuart (memory only in the OOB notes; the cavalry notes carry a red-lined gray cape, yellow sash,
plumed hat, red flower from Wikipedia) and Lee’s own coat: lead only. Open: Longstreet, A.P. Hill, Armistead and the rest.

B.6.9 Small arms dictionary
Wikipedia figures are secondary; where a spec table and body text disagree, both are shown. Cartridges and weights come from the cited source only.
Infantry long arms
Arm
Specs
Notes and conflicts
Source
Springfield M1861 rifle-musket
.58; barrel 40 in (the American Rifleman text says 41;
the v1 notes say about 40-41); overall 56 in; about 9 lb;
percussion; leaf sight (300/500 yd leaves, 100 yd
baseline); about 1,000,000 made including contractors;
Springfield Armory 265,129 from Jan 1861 to Dec 1863;
muzzle velocity 1,000-1,400 ft/s; effective range 200-
400 yd, marksmen to about 500 yd; max 800-1,000 yd
(v1); 2-3 rounds a minute
Conflict: barrel 40 vs 41 in.
 The product uses 40 in as
the catalogued value and records 41 as a range note.
Contractor M1861s (Colt Special, Trenton etc.) are the
same pattern; the Colt Special accepts only the Collins
bayonet
[Wiki-M1861], [AR-Guns]
M1863
Follow-on with Colt-influenced changes (bands,
hammer, bolster)
Not in field issue at Gettysburg: the Irish Brigade is
documented as receiving it only in spring 1864
(inferred). Never assign it
[Wiki-M1861], [28thmass]

tests: half of primers misfired; dropped in 1861); about

Harpers Ferry rifle (M1855 rifle)
33 in barrel, 49 in overall. Confederates captured the
machinery, later used at Richmond and Fayetteville
[Wiki-M1855]
M1841 “Mississippi” rifle
Originally .54, rebored to .58 in 1855 (many); leaf sights
100/300/500 yd (some ladders to 1,100 yd); about
70,000 made 1846-55 (Harpers Ferry, Whitney,
Remington, Robbins Kendall & Lawrence); originally no
bayonet, modified in 1855 for a sword bayonet
Some Union troops kept them to 1863 (the 45th NY
used them past Gettysburg); Confederate cavalry and
sharpshooters used them to war’s end
[Wiki-M1841]
M1842 smoothbore musket
.69; 42 in barrel; 58 in overall; about 9.8 lb; percussion
(earlier flintlocks mostly converted); about 275,000
made 1842-55 (Springfield, Harpers Ferry); paper
cartridge could carry buck and ball; many later rifled
for the Minie ball
Bayonet not stated in the source; a socket bayonet is
inferred, unverified
[Wiki-M1842]
Buck and ball load
One ball plus buckshot; test load .650 ball plus three .31
buckshot; hits good at 50 yd, scattered at 100 yd, most
buck falling short near 75 yd; the 12th NJ monument is
inscribed “Buck & Ball Calibre .69”; 15+ buckshot loads
on July 3; 60-grain powder charge is memory only
[CWNews-Smooth]
Pattern 1853 Enfield rifle-musket
.577; 39 in barrel; 55 in overall; 9.5 lb; ladder sight 100-
400 yd with long-range flip leaf to 1,250 (the v1 notes:
sighted to 900 yd); more than 900,000 estimated into
America; the Confederacy got more of these than any
other small arm
Conflict:
 Craig Barry (forum quote) cites over
1,000,000 exported to both sides. Carry as 900,000-
1,000,000+
[Wiki-P1853], [CWTalk-Enfield]
P1856/P1858 Enfield short rifle
Specs not retrieved; 13 Union regiments (1%) in an
1864 chart with acknowledged errors
Gap
[Barry]
Austrian Lorenz M1854
Originally .54; Union rebores .55/.57/.577/.59; 37.25 in
barrel; 52 in overall; Model I block sight, Model II
folding leaf to 750 m; quadrangular socket bayonet;
Union imported 226,924, Confederate agent Huse about
100,000
Barry’s count of Lorenz in 1864 Union regiments is
“over 80” (a chart figure of 9 is a typo); weight is a gap

Richmond rifle / carbine
Richmond Armory began October 1861 using captured
Harpers Ferry machinery; M1855 form without the
Maynard primer or patch box; carbine 25 in barrel / 41
in overall (from Nov 1862); short rifle 33 in / 49 in;
1,350 short rifles and 5,400 carbines per the infobox
The 34th Virginia Cavalry Battalion had Enfield and
Richmond rifle-muskets (including shorter Richmonds)
on July 3. Fayetteville and Tower: gap

Field finds
Nearly any arm turns up among Confederate

Marc Hermann post, forum, weak
[AuthCamp-CSmuskets]
Overall shares
About 90% of infantry on both sides had rifle-muskets
(Alexander: Army of Northern Virginia about 90% rifled
.54/.58); about 10% of arms captured by I Corps on July
1 were smoothbores; the Army of the Potomac had a
higher rifled share than the Confederates, mixing
imported Enfields, Lorenz and M1861 Springfields. A
forum poster claims about 900 men in A.P. Hill’s corps
carried .69 round-ball muskets: unverified
[AR-Guns], [CWTalk-A.P.Hill]
Barry’s 1864 Union chart, rough proportions only
 (a 1864 snapshot with acknowledged errors): Enfield P53 in 39% of regiments, US Model (1861) 23%, M1842 7%, mixed P53 and US 1861 6%,
US 1841 3%, Enfield short rifle 1%. This is a share of regiments, not of men. Piecemeal reissue explains mixed arms inside one regiment: in February 1863 Michigan units received 50,000 M1861s
while Enfield holders kept theirs ([Barry]).

B.6.10 Unit-to-weapon assignment table
Rule for units not in the table: draw the weapon from a documented-share-based default (the overall shares and Barry’s chart above, normalized to the arms that are date-correct), mark the draw
plausible-procedural, and let a later source overwrite it. For the Union: the rifled .58/.577 types dominate and the M1842 smoothbore is assigned only to regiments the notes name; any other draw of
smoothbore, Lorenz or M1841 is a flagged exception at a share no larger than the documented smoothbore share. For the Confederates: Enfield P1853 or a captured M1861 or M1855 by default; a
minority of smoothbores up to the documented share. Mixed arms inside a regiment are allowed (documented practice).

Data-quality flags.
 The IMFDB claim that “36% of Union regiments at Gettysburg had two or more weapon types” has no citation and was not confirmed; treat as unverified. Barry’s 6% “mixed P53
and US 1861” regiments is a different measure. IMFDB also says Remington 1863 Zouave rifles were never issued; not checked. Busey & Martin does not carry small arms.
Print sources that will settle the open rows:
 1. Dean Thomas, “Ready, Aim, Fire! Small Arms Ammunition in the Battle of Gettysburg,” Appendix I (Union regiment primary armament by regiment).
Gettysburg Total War animation plan, version 2 | page 74 of 144


=== PAGE 75 ===
2. Coates & Thomas, “An Introduction to Civil War Small Arms,” Appendix C (Dean Thomas’s list of the primary armament of every Union regiment at Gettysburg). 3. Official Records ordnance returns
for the Army of the Potomac and the cavalry quarterly returns (the carbine counts above come from a blog quoting these). 4. NARA Record Group 156 (Ordnance Department), including the armorer J.
Dudley’s report on the arms recovered after the battle. 5. For Confederate regiments: regimental and brigade histories and the Confederate ordnance receipts; Marc Hermann’s field-find evidence.
B.6.11 Ammunition, loads and loading drill
Rounds carried.
 Forty rounds in the box, about twenty more in the pockets or knapsack, about sixty going into expected combat; each ten cartridges came with twelve caps ([CVBT-Load], citing Coco
and Time-Life). Army Heritage gives 60-80 rounds in a 50 lb load; NPS gives 30-40 in the Confederate box. Carry: box 40 (Union), 30-40 (Confederate), total 60-80.
Drill.
 Loading a muzzle-loader was a nine-step drill, against five for breech-loaders ([ABT-SmallArms]). A forum (CivilWarTalk loading thread) argues that in combat only eight cycles occur, because
the ninth step (return to the shoulder) occurs only on the first load. The step-by-step list is not in the research notes. Leads to verify from Casey’s 
Infantry Tactics
 (1862) and Hardee’s 
Rifle and Light
Infantry Tactics
: the standard nine commands are, from memory, load; handle cartridge; tear cartridge; charge cartridge; draw rammer; ram cartridge; return rammer; prime; shoulder arms. This is
unverified memory and must be checked before an animation set is built. The drill is the basis of the loading animation; the Hardee vs Casey drill differences are open.
Rates and ranges.
Quantity
Value
Source
Rate
2-3 rounds a minute typical (M1861); Sharps 8-10 a minute claimed
[Wiki-M1861], [Wiki-Sharps]
Smoothbore
Unreliable beyond about 75 yd; buck and ball good at 50 yd
[ABT-SmallArms], [CWNews-Smooth]
Average shooter / trained marksman
About 250 yd / to about 800 yd
[ABT-SmallArms]
Average engagement range at Gettysburg
About 200 yd, from later rangefinder checks; Hobart Ward ordered fire
held until about 200 yd on July 2
[AR-Guns]
Fouling
The Minie ball was undersized to allow for fouling; muskets could become
hard to load after about 40 rounds (forum claim by Rhea Cole, uncited);
barrel too hot to touch after 6-9 rounds; up to 14 loads reported in one
barrel
[CWTalk-Loading], [Wiki-Minie]. Burton ball 1.14 oz (about 500 gr)
Ammunition expended (examples).
 - Berdan’s companies and the 3rd Maine: about 95 rounds per man in about 15 minutes at Pitzer’s Woods ([AR-Guns]). - Blackford’s 5th Alabama Battalion: more
than 200 rounds per man on July 3 ([NPS-Blackford]); the SC sharpshooter battalion more than 200 each by the morning of July 3 ([AR-Guns]). - A cavalryman about a dozen rounds on July 1 ([AR-
Guns]). - 20th Maine: a cited report says “60 rounds” per man, and the regiment ran out and used captured arms ([Colby]); the text is unclear on the exact wording. - Recovered arms after the battle:
armorer J. Dudley’s report on 27,574 arms, “at least 24,000” loaded (cited by a forum poster from RG 156 via a letter; Rhea Cole disputes it as anecdotal). Standard lore of about 24,000 of 37,000
loaded is unverified, and the thread gives 27,574, not 37,000. Carried as a conflict.
Animation rules.
 Fire rate is drawn from the 2-3 per minute range for muzzle-loaders; a soldier’s rounds in the box fall as he fires, and a soldier who has fired the unit’s documented expenditure
(where known) goes to resupply from the dead and wounded, as Blackford’s men and the 20th Maine did. Fouling slows loading after about 40 rounds only as a flagged option (weak source).

Variance rules within a unit
1. 
The unit kit spec defines an allowed set for every field and a weight for each allowed item; the seed draws from it. Allowed items must be backed by a source or flagged plausible-procedural.
2. 
Uniform coherence: a documented unit-wide item (the Iron Brigade hat, the 114th PA fez) is applied to every man of that unit, with only wear variance; documented minority items (a company’s
blue replacement trousers) are applied to a company, not randomly.
3. 
Company-level consistency where evidence is silent: draw by company so that neighboring men look similar, which is realistic because clothing was issued by company (this is a design choice,
tagged plausible-procedural).
4. 
Hard limits: no more than one kind of long arm per soldier; the arm must come from the unit’s assignment row or, for unknown units, the default distribution; officers and NCOs get the
sash/sword only where the rank rules allow and the sash is rare.
5. 
Armies are separated: Confederate soldiers can wear captured Union items (US belt plate upside down, captured Union box, captured blue trousers), but Union soldiers never wear Confederate
items.

All rows are inferred unless the weather values are confirmed in B.10 (the weather source list and the temperature, rain and light schedule are specified there; this section only specifies how kit
responds to them).
Date and time
Wear state
Reason
Before July 1
Marching dust, sweat-darkened shirts, discarded packs (12th NJ, 122nd
NY)
Forced marches; XI and I Corps arrived after hard marches
July 1
Dust-gray cloth, sweat-dark shirts; powder-blackened hands and cuffs for
the units engaged
Hard marching and first fighting
July 2
Hot, shirt-sleeves common; faces and cuffs powder-blackened; hat sweat
stains
Heat and heavy fighting; coats off
July 3
Heat plus smoke: stronger soiling, sooty faces, men in shirt-sleeves
The heat day and the cannonade
July 4
Rain: wool dark and heavy, mud on trouser legs and shoes, gum blankets
and ponchos deployed, hats drooping
Rain on July 4, 1.39 in (Jacobs readings via ENV-Strategy; see B.10)
July 5-14
Mud, rain-stained, wounds and bandages on the withdrawing Confederate
column
Night
Same kit, fires, dim lighting; coats and blankets on
B.10 night spec
The numbers for each level of soiling are tuning parameters (plausible-procedural). No source retrieved quantifies day-by-day soiling; regimental histories and Gettysburg weather logs are the named
sources to close the gap.
Validation test
No soldier ships with an anachronism or a wrong-unit item. A validator runs over the full manifest set before any render and fails the build on any of the following; each failure is logged to the
evidence-gap register rather than waived.
1. 
Presence test:
 a soldier whose unit has not yet arrived (B.2.4) or has already left is absent at that time.
2. 
Anachronism list:
 Type III Richmond Depot jackets; M1863 rifle-musket; Henry rifle; Spencer carbine on any unit before June 30 or on any unit other than the 5th Michigan (all) and part of the
6th Michigan after that date; Zouave items on the 5th NY, 140th NY, 155th PA or any Louisiana soldier; Berdan green on any other unit; Wheat’s Tiger or Coppens’ Zouave dress; green hatband
on the Irish Brigade or medics; Whitworth rifle in infantry hands by default; kepi on a Confederate private by default.
3. 
Wrong-unit items:
 every weapon equals the unit assignment or the documented default distribution for that army; every badge shape belongs to the soldier’s corps; every uniform item belongs
to the unit’s spec; no Union soldier carries a Confederate item.
4. 
Branch correctness:
 artillery has scarlet trim, cavalry yellow, infantry sky blue; medical men have the green band only if they are Ambulance Corps.
5. 
Tier completeness:
 every field has a tier; a field with no tier fails; a documented field must have a source key.
6. 
Reproducibility:
 regenerating the manifest from the same seed yields identical kit.
7. 
Sample audit:
 a seeded sample of soldiers per unit is rendered on a turntable and checked automatically against the unit spec image references; failures produce a report.
B.6.13 Gaps and sources to acquire
Every item below is open in the notes. The source named is the one that would settle it. Where Claude Code can read the source (public web, public-domain scans, digitized books), it fetches and
extracts it itself; where only print exists, the row stays in the Evidence-Gap Register with its tier until a digitized copy is found. No row is dropped.

B.6.14 Artillery
Scope: every piece type on the field, the ammunition and fuzes, the detachment drill as animation states, teams and vehicles, dress, the Union and Confederate orders of battle as far as the notes
reach, battery-by-battery positions, the July 3 cannonade in full, how fire and impact are animated, and what is still missing. Tier tags: documented = a named source states it; inferred = reasoned
from documented facts; plausible-procedural = generated to fill a gap. Note rows tagged GAP in the research notes appear here as open gaps. Many specifications come from Wikipedia articles and a
few from forum threads; those are flagged as weak where they matter. No primary manual (Gibbon, Ordnance Manual) or official report (Hunt, Alexander memoir) was read for the underlying notes, so
every value below is provisional until checked against them (see “Gaps and sources to acquire” at the end of this section).
B.6.14.1 Piece specifications (with the conflicting values shown)
Core table: bore, tube length, tube weight, projectile weight, charge, muzzle velocity and range at 5 degrees elevation. Source: Wikipedia, Field artillery in the ACW (tier: documented, secondary
source).
Piece
Material
Bore (in)
Tube length (in)
Tube weight (lb)
Projectile (lb)

Piece-specific detail that goes beyond the table:
Piece
Additional documented detail
Source
12-pdr Napoleon
Carriage 1,128 lb; bore length 63.6 in; powder 2.5 lb for shot and spherical
case, 2.0 lb for common shell. Round shot 12.25 lb, 4.52 in diameter, 1,680
yd at 5 deg. Spherical case 12.1 lb with 78 balls, 1,135 yd at 3 deg 45 min.
Common shell 12.3 lb, 1,300 yd at 3 deg 45 min. Canister 14.8 lb, 27 iron
balls of about 1.49 in in four tiers. Gun with packed limber 3,865 lb. Every
bronze smoothbore in the Army of the Potomac at Gettysburg was a
Napoleon except two 12-pdr howitzers in the 2nd Connecticut battery. The
Confederates had received 49 recast Napoleons by the battle.
Wikipedia M1857 Napoleon; Wikipedia field artillery
3-inch Ordnance rifle
Carriage 900 lb; bore length 65 in; 1.0 lb charge; 9.5 lb shell; about 1,830
yd at 5 deg and about 4,180 yd at 16 deg; seven lands and grooves, right-
hand twist of one turn in 11 ft. Gun and carriage about 1,700 lb (horse
artillery page) versus 820 + 900 = 1,720 (Wikipedia): consistent.
Wikipedia 3-inch rifle; Regular Cavalry blog
10-pdr Parrott
Cast iron with wrought-iron reinforcing band at the breech; 2.9 in bore on
the 1861-62 model; Confederates copied it (Tredegar made 80 of 2.9 in).
Hazlett’s battery tube plus carriage 1,799 lb (Stone Sentinels) versus 890 +
900 = 1,790: consistent within rounding.
Wikipedia 10-pdr Parrott; Stone Sentinels
12-pdr Whitworth
12-lb hexagonal bolt; effective range 2,600 yd at 5 deg; maximum 10,000
yd at 35 deg (forum claim, weak). Two on the field, in Hurt’s (Hardaway’s
Alabama) battery of McIntosh’s battalion, A. P. Hill’s reserve; near Herr’s
Tavern July 1; on Oak Hill July 3, said by the marker (as quoted in the
thread) to be beyond Union range. The American Rifleman article judges
Whitworth rifle use at Gettysburg unlikely, but that refers to the .451
infantry rifle, not the gun.
CivilWarTalk Whitworths thread (weak); Wikipedia Whitworth rifle

Inventory of guns on the field (StrategyPage, Union / Confederate): 12-pdr howitzer 2 / 26; 24-pdr howitzer 0 / 4; 12-pdr Napoleon 146 / 111; 3-inch rifle 142 / 76; 3-inch Navy rifle 0 / 4; 6-pdr 0 / 1; 10-
pdr Parrott 64 / 44; 12-pdr Blakely 0 / 4; 12-pdr Whitworth 0 / 2; 14-pdr James 0 / 4; 20-pdr Parrott 6 / 10; totals 366 / 282. HistoryNet gives 372 and 281 (653). Tier: documented (secondary); the two
totals conflict.
Known Gettysburg gun types by battery (Stone Sentinels unless noted): Cushing (Bty A, 4th US) six 3-inch rifles; Hazlett (Bty D, 5th US) six 10-pdr Parrotts; 2nd Connecticut four 14-pdr James rifles
and two 12-pdr howitzers; 5th NY (Taft) six 20-pdr Parrotts; W.Va. Bty C four 10-pdr Parrotts; Calef (Bty A, 2nd US) six 3-inch rifles; Smith’s 4th NY six 10-pdr Parrotts (monument); Hart’s 15th NY
four Napoleons; Bigelow’s 9th Mass six Napoleons; Rorty’s 1st NY B four 10-pdr Parrotts (Hazard’s report); Woodruff’s 1st US I six “light 12-pdrs”; Brown’s 1st RI B six light 12-pdrs; Arnold’s 1st RI A
six 3-inch (Hazard’s report, Iron Brigader). Remaining Union and Confederate types are in the order-of-battle tables below.
Ballistic modelling rule (inferred): derive each gun’s range curve from the published 5-degree range plus the 3-inch rifle’s 16-degree maximum of about 4,180 yd. Smoothbores fall well behind rifles
beyond about 1,500 yd, so Napoleons on July 3 had to be forward or near the crest to reach Seminary Ridge (1,400 yd or more) with shell or case. The Whitworth and Blakely breechloaders are
modelled as long-range, flat-trajectory rifles; their July 3 effect from Oak Hill rests on a forum claim, not an official source. Per-elevation range tables are an open gap (see end of section).
B.6.14.2 Ammunition types and fuzes
Type
Description
Napoleon figures
Animation consequence
Solid shot (bolt)
Kinetic, accurate, ricochets from smoothbores on hard
dry ground
12.25 lb
Bounce along ground on dry July soil; stops in soft
earth. Napoleon Guide: bouncing roundshot on dry
ground flattens many men, stops in soggy earth
Shell
Black-powder burst, few large fragments; used against
troops behind cover and to ignite buildings
12.3 lb, 1,300 yd
Ground or air burst puff, fragments; fire on buildings
Spherical case (shrapnel)
Balls with a burst charge, timed to burst over the target
78 balls, 12.1 lb, 1,135 yd
Airburst smoke puff with a downward ball cone, fuze-
cutting by No. 6
Canister
Thin-walled container of iron balls, deadliest at short
range
27 balls (1.49 in), 14.8 lb; effective 350-400 yd
Wide cone from the muzzle; double canister at extreme
close range
Grapeshot
Obsolete
9 balls
Not modelled except if a source shows use
Typical stock for a six-Napoleon battery: 288 solid shot, 288 spherical case, 96 shell and 96 canister (Wikipedia Napoleon; reported to match the 5th Maine battery figure). Tier: documented
(secondary). This is a per-battery stock total across limbers and caissons. The per-chest and per-limber round count (for example 32 rounds per Napoleon limber chest, 50 per rifle chest) is an open
gap: not confirmed from a source.
Fuzes: - Timed (paper or wooden) fuze: the cutter trims the fuze to range before loading; the flame from the main charge ignites it. Described as the most problematic type (Civil War News, The
Artillery Fuze). Example Confederate paper fuze: C.S. Laboratory Richmond, May 1864, about 4 seconds per inch, wrapped in wax paper (a May 1864 example, not July 1863; tier: documented but
date-shifted). - Percussion fuzes: plunger-and-anvil, explode on impact; used with rifled shell. Union 3-inch ammunition on hand in Wheeler’s 13th NY on July 2 was percussion and canister only (13th
NY Battery site). - Confederate reliability: a Confederate officer’s report of December 1863 stated many fuzes exploded early and about half of friction primers failed; Union primers and fuzes failed
less (Civil War News). A Richmond (Brown’s Island) laboratory explosion shut the arsenal temporarily: March 1863 (Civil War News) versus April 1863 (History Is Now Magazine) - date conflict. - July 3
specifics (secondary blog, History Is Now): fuzes burned longer than expected; replacements from Selma and Charleston burned about one second longer than Richmond-made fuzes; later tests
suggested bursts 150-200 yd farther back than intended; one officer blamed softened resin filler mixing with powder in humid weather. The American Battlefield Trust says many shells with defective
fuzes fell short or flew over. Both short and long attested; the dominant claim in the blog is long. - Not found: Bormann fuze, Schenkl and Hotchkiss specifics; which percussion fuze models each side
used at Gettysburg (open gap).
Round totals (open gap): about 32,000 Union and 22,000 Confederate rounds fired are commonly cited but were not verified. Documented per-battery figures: Smith 4th NY 240 rounds (marker);
Wheeler 13th NY 850 rounds with 200 lost with a broken caisson; Bigelow about three tons including 92 canister; McCarthy (Cabell) 20 rounds at an advancing line early July 3.
Union tactical ammunition choices (documented): Bigelow fired solid shot low for ricochet at Trostle’s, then canister (Civil War News 2025); Cushing used double canister at about 100 yd on July 3
(Warfare History Network). Inference for the engine: ammunition choice by range band - solid shot/shell/case beyond about 400 yd, canister inside 350-400 yd, double canister inside about 100 yd.
B.6.14.3 Crew roles and the drill as animation states
Documented sequence (NPS Fort Scott artillery drill page, a Union-pattern light-artillery demonstration): commands are commence firing, load, ready, fire, cease firing. No. 1 sponges while No. 3
thumbs the vent; No. 2 takes the round from No. 5 and puts it in the muzzle; No. 1 rams; the gunner sights; No. 3 moves the trail with the trailspike on the gunner’s direction; No. 5 fetches the next
round from Nos. 6 and 7 at the limber (No. 6 cuts the fuzes); the gunner steps clear and calls ready; No. 3 pricks the cartridge through the vent; No. 4 hooks the friction primer to the lanyard, inserts it
and pulls the lanyard taut on “Fire”; after firing the piece is run back up. CVBT adds: No. 2 also uses the worm staff after firing; No. 6 tends the chest; No. 7 hands rounds; spare men handle horses;
the prolonge is a long rope on the trail used to move the piece without hitching up.
Crew-size conflict (8 versus 9) and how the engine handles it: NPS Fort Scott counts nine (gunner, chief of caisson, Nos. 1-7); CVBT counts roughly eight (gunner plus seven numbered); Wikipedia
states eight crew plus four men for horses and equipment but also gives nine cannoneers in the Napoleon article; the horse-artillery page gives a typical crew of nine. The two counts disagree only on
whether the chief of caisson belongs in the detachment. Engine rule (inferred, so tier: inferred): the working detachment at the piece is the gunner plus Nos. 1-7, which is eight men and common to
every source; the chief of caisson is a ninth soldier stationed with the limber/caisson line, not at the piece. A battery’s detachment size is then a parameter (8 default, 9 including chief of caisson) that
can be changed without redoing animation, because the chief of caisson has no gun-side state. When casualties reduce a crew, the drill degrades by dropping roles in this order (plausible-procedural,
seeded): spare hands and No. 7 first, then No. 6 and No. 5 merge, then No. 2 and No. 4 pair up, with the gunner always last. Confederate crew size is an open gap (often smaller crews, no source
retrieved).

Cycle time (inferred): about 20-30 seconds per round for a trained detachment, consistent with the one documented example of about 2.5 rounds/min averaged over an hour (Waterman at Mine Run,
CVBT; this is not a Gettysburg figure). Canister loading is faster. Recorded Gettysburg rates are an open gap.
Other documented organization: two guns form a section under a lieutenant; a gun plus caisson formed a platoon under a sergeant and two corporals; Calef’s battery ran as right, center and left
sections under Lt. Roder, 1st Sgt. Newman and Sgt. Pergel. Sergeants were chiefs of piece and corporals gunners on the Union side (Wikipedia Union Army). Commands and crew positions (No. 1 and
No. 2 on opposite sides of the muzzle, etc.) are not confirmed from a primary text (open gap).
B.6.14.4 Teams, drivers, limbers, caissons, battery wagon, forge
Item
Documented detail
Source
Teams
Each gun uses two six-horse teams (gun limber and caisson limber). Each
six-horse team had three drivers riding the left horses and holding the reins
of the right. About 700 lb pull per horse. Horse life expectancy under eight
months in service (cited).
Wikipedia field artillery; Regular Cavalry blog
Limber
Carries one ammunition chest; a Napoleon with packed limber weighs
3,865 lb.
Wikipedia
Caisson
Two-wheeled; carries two ammunition chests plus a spare wheel; each
chest about 500 lb; a loaded limber-and-caisson about 3,811 lb.
Wikipedia
Battery wagon and forge
Each battery had one battery wagon and one forge.
Regular Cavalry blog; CivilWarTalk
Reserve caissons, staff
Six reserve caissons, five artificers, two buglers, guidon-bearer, orderly,
quartermaster sergeant in the horse-artillery description.
Regular Cavalry blog
Horse-artillery cannoneers
Each rode an individual horse, with two extra men as horse-holders, so no
gun-side riding on the limber.
Regular Cavalry blog
Battery table of organization (6-pdr mounted battery as quoted from a
manual in a forum)
5 officers, 20 NCOs, 6 artificers, 2 buglers, 52 drivers, 70 cannoneers (150
enlisted). A 12-pdr battery adds 6 caissons (12 total) and 18 drivers. Horses
(12-pdr): draught 36 gun, 72 caisson, 6 battery wagon, 6 forge, 10 spare;
saddle 8 sergeants, 6 artificers, 2 buglers, 2 spare; stated total 149 (the
items sum to 148).
CivilWarTalk (weak: forum quote of a manual)
Typical size
Union batteries usually six guns (some four), Confederate often four; 100-
150 men. This line is unverified background knowledge in the notes.
Notes, flagged unverified
Placement in the engine (inferred): at the piece the detachment of eight; limbers about 10-15 yd behind (second line), caissons farther back (third line), battery wagon and forge in the rear park, horses
and drivers with the limbers. Documented loss counts of horses (Calef 13, Hart 25, Bigelow 65 or 80, Smith 11, Brown 24 killed and 6 disabled, Cabell 67 killed and 13 disabled, Alexander 116) drive
the horse casualty animation; a battery short of horses fields fewer teams (Hart had enough horses for only three guns; Phillips’ limber team of five horses was shot).

Notes and caveats: the Wikipedia page lists no battalion called “Lane” (Lane was an infantry brigadier in Pender’s division), so Lane’s artillery is not identifiable from sources. Allocating guns to
Dearing, Eshleman, Alexander, Walker, Nelson, Garnett, Dance and others needs per-battery returns that were not found; use the 4-gun-per-battery default and the inventory in B.6.14.1 as a total
check (inferred, plausible-procedural where applied). Henry’s battalion arrived July 2; its July 3 positions were not retrieved (gap). Two sources differ on which batteries sat with which corps in the
later phases (Alexander commanded the First Corps guns in the later phases of the battle).

B.6.14.8 The July 3 cannonade in full
Sequence and values, with every conflicting figure carried (sources: NPS Handbook 9, StrategyPage, American Battlefield Trust, HistoryNet, History Is Now, Wikipedia Pickett’s Charge, CivilWarTalk
quoting Hunt, Emerging Civil War, Hazard report):
Element
Documented values and conflicts
Signal
About 1 pm; two guns near the Peach Orchard (Miller’s battery) fired as the signal (NPS Handbook 9). HistoryNet
says Whitworths on Oak Ridge fired the signal. Union gunners waited, then about 80 guns opened (NPS).
Confederate gun line
About a two-mile line (Trust, “around 150”). Cabell’s guns near a small brick house; Carter’s ten rifles at the
railroad cut; Alexander’s battalion with the assault; Hurt’s Whitworths on Oak Hill.
Confederate gun count
138-179 estimates; Pendleton said nearly 150; Alexander’s 179 included about two dozen howitzers held for the
infantry; at least 142 shown to have fired; reasoned range 142-155 (StrategyPage). Other figures: 130-170
(Coddington over 150; Eicher 159; Trudeau 164; Clark and Pfanz about 170, per Wikipedia Pickett’s Charge); 150-
170 (HistoryNet, History Is Now); about 80 ANV guns unused (Wikipedia); a Confederate officer estimated about
300 total.
Union gun count
About 106 deployed on the ridge, about 85 engaged briefly, about 136 held in reserve (StrategyPage); as many as
120 on Cemetery Ridge and Hill (Trust); only about 80 available for counter-battery (History Is Now; Hunt per
Wikipedia); 80 (NPS).
Duration
Nearly two hours (NPS; Cabell “over two hours”); about 1 1/4 hours at II Corps (Hazard); roughly one hour
(History Is Now); one to two hours (HistoryNet); ended around 3 pm (Trust); Wheeler 1-3 pm.
Hunt’s orders
At 10 am inspected batteries and told them to hold fire against small targets, to concentrate on one enemy battery
when the Confederates opened, and to conserve ammunition; at noon described the Confederate line as covering
the whole two-mile front (CivilWarTalk quoting Hunt’s report; not read from the OR). After about two hours he
ordered batteries to slow; withdrew damaged and low-ammunition batteries and prepared canister (Trust). Hunt
pulled several batteries from the center so the Confederates would think they were destroyed and replaced them
with reserve guns (Emerging Civil War).
Hancock dispute
Hancock countermanded the slowdown and ordered return fire; his officers answered they would obey their chief
(Emerging Civil War; Trust). Hancock’s anger is documented in secondary sources; the primary texts of both men
were not read.
Alexander lull
Alexander judged the Union fire weakening and his own ammunition low; sent word to Pickett (NPS Handbook).
Another account: replenishing from trains would take over an hour, so the infantry advanced without close
artillery support (History Is Now). Alexander’s own report: batteries held positions through the afternoon of July 3.
Exact ammunition figures are an open gap.
Fuze defects and overshooting
See B.6.14.2. Gunners aimed high to maximize shrapnel and smoke hung low in windless heat, so most shells flew
into the Union rear (History Is Now).
Leister house hits
Meade’s HQ at the Leister house was made untenable by the bombardment and he moved to Powers Hill
(HistoryNet). Meade-HQ casualties and horse losses are not retrieved.
Union batteries hurt
Cushing’s and Rorty’s badly hurt; Osborn’s guns on Cemetery Hill had a hard time (CivilWarTalk thread); three of
Cushing’s limbers exploded in the morning (Hazard).
Union counter-fire effect
McGilvery’s batteries and Rittenhouse on Little Round Top described as the main fire disrupting Pickett’s men
(forum opinion).
Afterwards
Hunt’s last four reserve batteries ordered up; he rode into the Angle and had his horse shot (Emerging Civil War).
Fire shifted to canister within about 400 yd (Wikipedia Pickett’s Charge).
Animation schedule (inferred; it fits the majority of sources; the two-hour duration seems to compress the whole artillery phase including the slowdown): 1:00 pm two-gun signal; about 30-40 minutes
of full exchange; Union slowdown starting about 1:30-2:00 pm; Hunt/Hancock exchange; Union lull to about 2:45-3:00 pm; then Pickett’s advance. Implementation: a randomized burst-distance error
biased long (+150-200 yd) with a small rate of duds and early bursts for Confederate case and shell (plausible-procedural, seeded); many Confederate rounds burst beyond the ridge among limbers,
caissons, horse lines and the Leister house vicinity, which explains rear-area carnage (inferred).
B.6.14.9 Animating fire, impact and damage
Documented ranges and ordnance data drive every parameter:
Effect
Rule
Basis
Muzzle flash and smoke
Per-gun charge (Napoleon 2.5 lb, 3-inch 1.0 lb, 20-pdr Parrott 2.0 lb);
smoke lingers in windless heat and reduces visibility; powder backfires in
rain only as a visual note
Charge data (B.6.14.1); smoke lingering and visibility as a TW design
analogue (Wikipedia Napoleon: Total War)
Projectile flight
Muzzle velocity 1,054-1,500 ft/s; per-gun range curve from the 5-degree
values; rifles to about 4,180 yd at 16 degrees

=== PAGE 84 ===
Round-shot bounce
Bounces and rolls on dry July ground, sticks in soft ground; slope can skip a
shot a long way; bounce is surface-dependent
Napoleon Guide; Total War analogue (Medieval II forum, Empire modding
thread); inferred for Gettysburg
Case/shell burst
Airburst puff at the fuze-cut range; case sends a ball cone downward (78
balls for a Napoleon); common shell breaks into few large fragments
B.6.14.2
Canister
27-ball cone inside 350-400 yd; double canister at about 100 yd
B.6.14.2
Ground impact
Crater and dirt plume; impact graphics per surface type (grass, wheat,
mud, stone); craters appear in Napoleon: Total War
TW analogues (see below)
Structures
Shell ignites buildings; solid shot ricochets off stone; rail fences splinter
Wikipedia field artillery; Napoleon: Total War preview; terrain sections B.8
Human damage
Round shot kills along its path (penetration through several men); case and
canister cone; knockback by mass and velocity; morale penalty for hits and
near misses
TW modding documentation as the analogue; the 1863 effect remains
modelled from ordnance data
Fuze defect
Long bursts on July 3 for the Confederate side
B.6.14.2
Casualty and horse animation
Crew deaths degrade the drill (B.6.14.3); horses shot in traces; limber and
caisson explosions (three of Cushing’s limbers exploded on July 3)
Hazard; Iron Brigader
Exploding caisson
Documented for Cushing’s limbers; Wheeler’s broken caisson lost 200
rounds
Hazard; 13th NY Battery
Total War sources: the research found the Warscape/Empire modding documentation on projectile parameters (
projectiles_tables
, trajectory modes, calibration area, bounce/roll flags, penetration
counts, impact graphics by surface type, explosion tables) and a few preview comments (cannonballs skip along the ground; balls stick in mud; craters in Napoleon: Total War; smoke lingering). These
are used only as engineering vocabulary; the physical values come from the ordnance data above. Sources for Total War crew animation (load, aim, fire, recoil), limber and unlimber behaviour, casualty
and crew replacement, mortar and rocket mechanics, muzzle-flash VFX detail and firing sound design were not found in any reachable source (tw_projectiles_artillery.md Gaps). Crew and limber
animation is therefore designed from the drill documents above (documented and inferred), not copied from Total War. A source to acquire is the Empire and Napoleon pack files browsed with Pack
File Manager and slow-motion video analysis of the games, which are tasks Claude Code can perform itself.

Inferences for scheduling: the most consistent reading of Stuart’s arrival is early-to-mid afternoon at Lee’s HQ on July 2 with Hampton trailing and fighting at Hunterstown at dusk (inferred); East
Cavalry Field is best shown as a 12-1 p.m. skirmish and artillery phase with a 2-3 p.m. climax (inferred); South Cavalry Field’s charges at 3-5 p.m. after Pickett’s repulse about 4 p.m., so 5 p.m. is more
plausible than 1 p.m. for the charges, with the earlier times being positioning (inferred).

Saber
M1840 “wristbreaker” or M1860 light. M1860: 41 in long, 35 in blade, 2 lb
4 oz alone / 3 lb 10 oz with scabbard; over 300,000 made (Ames about
200,000). Later in the war big saber charges were less common; many left
sabers on the saddle and fought dismounted

Carbines by regiment (as sourced)
Buford’s July 1 screen carried a mix: Sharps (most common; .52 or .54 linen cartridge), Burnside (.54 tapered brass), Gallager (.52 brass, hard to extract), Smith (.50 rubber cartridge, well regarded),
Merrill (.54 paper, fragile and unpopular) and Sharps & Hankins (.52 rimfire; three companies of the 9th NY). The cartridges were not interchangeable. The belief that Buford’s troopers had Spencers
on July 1 is folklore (Civil War News, Bilby, “Carbine Folklore”).

AR
Other carbine facts: Sharps was the most common Union cavalry arm by 1863 (about 90,000 carbines; .52, 475 gr, 50 gr powder; 8-10 rounds/min); Burnside .54 with a 21 in barrel, 7 lb, 43 Union
cavalry regiments in 1863-64; Spencer rifle 47 in overall (30 in barrel) with 7-round tube, .56-56. The Spencer carbine was not produced until October 1863 (AR, Civil War News), which seems to
conflict with Spencer use on July 3: inferred explanation is rifle versus carbine models, to be verified. Henry rifles are not documented at Gettysburg. Shortages of Sharps carbines were noted (Ripley
letter of June 2). Specifications for Gallager, Smith, Merrill, Starr, Joslyn, Maynard and Sharps & Hankins were not retrieved.

B.6.15.5 Horses and horse condition
Fact
Detail
Source
Remounts
About 17,000 remounts to the Union Cavalry Corps May-July 1863; horses
under 6 years accepted from June 1 and many broke down; hard pikes wore
out shoes quickly; bad contractor saddles ruined backs
HistoryNet, Lack of horses
