notes
Animation rule (inferred): horse condition is a per-regiment variable. Union regiments appear with gaunt, dusty or sweat-darkened mounts and a visible share unserviceable; units with a documented
shortfall (6th US, 10th NY, 8th NY company) field fewer mounted men than paper strength.
B.6.15.6 Mounted versus dismounted tactics, and what the animation must show
Documented tactics: - Dismounted: every fourth trooper held his own horse plus three others, removing a quarter of the firepower; breechloaders let men reload under cover (HistoryNet; Wikipedia
Day 1; Civil War News). - Delaying action: Buford aimed to hold the Herr, McPherson and Seminary ridges until the infantry arrived, using a fighting withdrawal (Wikipedia Day 1). - East Cavalry Field:
Stuart planned to pin skirmishers and swing around the flank; Spencer-armed skirmishers held; he then used a mounted charge (1st Va), a counter-charge by the 7th Michigan, and a massed column
charge by Hampton met by the 1st Michigan, with flank blows at the same moment (Wikipedia third-day cavalry). - Mounted charges on bad ground failed: South Cavalry Field (stone fences, rocks) and
Falling Waters (uphill versus entrenched); Kilpatrick overruled Custer’s skirmish approach (Emerging Civil War). - Night and weather: Monterey Pass chaos, hasty barricades and friendly fire;
Boonsboro mud forced dismounting. - Horse artillery was decisive at East Cavalry Field (Union won the duel; shell and canister at Hampton’s column) and at Monterey Pass.
Inferred animation requirements: - Horse holders: one soldier in four visible behind the firing line, holding four horses together, in a depression or woodlot, with horses restive under fire. Riders
remount for retreat. - Dismounted skirmish lines: scattered, behind fences and walls, breechloader reloading kneeling or prone, with a thinner line than an infantry battalion. - Charges: column or line
at the trot then gallop, sabers drawn, a stone or rail fence in the path; crash melee with sabers and revolvers; horses falling. - Dust and noise on dry Pennsylvania roads July 1-3; summer heat; mud and
rain July 4-8; signal guns and bugle calls. The notes carry no sourced description of dust, noise, bugle calls or guidon patterns; all are inference. - No period depictions (Waud sketches, paintings) were
reviewed; compare in a later pass.
B.6.15.7 Cavalry state and behaviour list for the engine
Each cavalry unit holds a time-keyed schedule of these states (plausible-procedural labels, grounded in the actions above):
State
Used for
march_column
Approach marches, Stuart’s ride, retreat, Kilpatrick’s night march
vedette / picket
Buford’s June 30 pickets; July 1 vedettes before Heth
reconnaissance / patrol
Buford June 30 patrols; observation from the Seminary cupola
dismount_deploy
Dismounted skirmish line with horse holders (every fourth man)
dismounted_skirmish_fire
McPherson Ridge, Brinkerhoff Ridge, Rummel farm, South Cavalry Field
fighting_withdrawal
Buford July 1
horse_holding
One-in-four horse holders behind the line
mounted_charge_column / line
Hunterstown, East Cavalry Field, Fairfield, Falling Waters, Monterey Pass
melee_saber_revolver
Hand-to-hand at Rummel farm
remount_retreat
Return to horses and withdrawal
horse_artillery_duel
Tidball’s batteries, Rank’s guns, Stuart’s 13 guns
guard_trains / escort
Buford and Merritt guarding trains; Baker and Imboden with the wagons
pursuit / rear-guard
Hampton at Hunterstown; Jones at Williamsport; Kilpatrick
wagon_train_attack
Monterey Pass, Williamsport
rest_bivouac / night_watch
Nights between actions
casualties / horse_down / wounded_carried
Horses shot, riders down, wounded to the rear
absent (not yet arrived / departed)
Hard rule: no unit is shown before its arrival or after it leaves
Fixed facts for the arrival rule: the Michigan Brigade and Farnsworth’s brigade fought at Hanover June 30 and Hunterstown July 2, then moved to East Cavalry Field and South Cavalry Field on July 3;
the 2nd Brigade of Gregg’s division (Huey) was at Westminster and never at Gettysburg; the 35th Va Bn was with Early’s infantry, not with the main cavalry; Stuart’s three brigades reached the field
after the first day.

B.7 Fortifications, earthworks and gun emplacements (built over time)
B.7.1 The governing rule
No earthwork, breastwork, barricade, lunette or gun pit is pre-placed in the scene.
 Every field work the notes document as battle-built starts the animation as bare ground (or as the pre-
existing farm feature it was built on or beside) and is constructed on screen, by the unit that built it, inside the time window the sources give. A work that the notes cannot attribute to a builder or a
time (the East Cemetery Hill lunettes) is still built on screen, by a labeled stand-in crew, and is flagged “builder unknown”.
The reverse rule matters just as much. Features that existed before the battle are present from the first frame and are never animated as construction. The Angle wall is the clearest case: it was a farm
wall built by local farmers before the battle (NPS-Angle). Section B.7.3 lists every pre-existing wall and fence that must not be given a construction animation.
Tier vocabulary used in this part: 
documented
 (a named source states it), 
inferred
 (reasoned from documented facts, reasoning shown), 
plausible-procedural
 (generated to fill a gap, seeded and
reproducible). Where a research note tagged an item GAP, this part lists it as an open gap and does not give it a tier. Dimensions that the notes do not supply are filled only with a labeled assumption
(B.7.4).

What the table says about Culp’s Hill at a glance:
 the first, rudimentary Union line starts on the evening of July 1 on the north slope (I Corps, 6th Wisconsin first) and the lower summit (123rd NY).
The XII Corps main line is built from dawn on July 2. The line is lengthened and thinned at 18:30, the lower works sit empty until Steuart occupies them after dark, and on July 3 the works are shelled
and retaken.

B.7.4 Construction-state model for the engine
Every built work is an object made of segments. Each segment (roughly one regiment front or a fixed run of yards) moves through these stages. The state is a function of scenario time, so scrubbing
backward un-builds the work.
Stage
What is visible
Typical content
Evidence basis
0. Bare ground
Natural slope, trees standing, no works
Woods and rocks as terrain
documented (no earthwork is pre-placed)
1. Layout
Officers pacing the line; men told off along it; tools
carried up
Greene’s decision to build despite superiors; skirmish
line sent downslope
documented (Greene ordering); procedural (staging)
2. Felling and gathering
Trees felled and cut into blocks; stones and boulders
gathered; cordwood moved; rails pulled from fences
(Seminary)
“Felled trees cut into blocks”; stones and boulders;
nearby cordwood piles
documented (HN-Walls)
3. Stacking
Logs, stones or rails stacked into a close log fence;
earth thrown behind with spades; rifle pits dug;
traverse laid out
“Close log fence”; spades and shovels; rifle pits;
traverse
documented
4. Head logs and concealment
Head logs laid along much of the line; boughs and logs
placed in front
“A man could kneel and fire over the works”; works not
distinguishable at 50 yd
documented
5. Completed
Full-height line, about 5 ft (Culp’s Hill)
Occupied in firing posture
documented (height); procedural (profile)
6. Damaged
Splintered head logs, torn boughs, scattered stone, shell
craters behind
July 3 morning after the shelling and about 227,000-
250,000 rounds fired by Geary’s men
inferred (damage state); documented (rounds, shelling)
Durations derived from the notes.
Greene’s brigade: 3 to 4 hours for the main line (E-05). The engine sets the window start at 06:00 and the end between 09:00 and 10:00 for the default; the documented outer bounds for
“finished” are about 10:00 (DMNA) and about noon (Gettysburg Daily). The default sits inside the overlap of the sources; the playback option “show source conflict” can re-run the dawn start at
the night-of-July-1-to-2 start the DMNA gives.
6th Wisconsin (July 1): about a dozen spades and shovels from the regimental wagon. The engine therefore shows a small digging party working alongside men using bayonets, hands, logs and
stones, with the tool count capped at 12 for that regiment. The number of men without tools is not in the sources; it is a plausible-procedural parameter.
Work did not stop at “completed”: angles, salients, the traverse and extra head logs continue through July 2 (DMNA, HN-King), so stages 3-4 may be re-entered by a segment after it first reaches
stage 5.
Length: about 300 yd morning of July 2 (GD-219), then a single thin line of about 800 yd after 18:30 (GD-219, inferred). Keep the lower-hill works intact but empty from 18:30 until Steuart enters
them.
Labeled assumptions (plausible-procedural, seeded).
 The notes give no thickness, no head-log dimension, no tool inventory beyond the dozen spades, no per-regiment work times for the 78th,
102nd, 137th, 149th NY, and no Kane or Lockwood detail. Where the engine needs these it uses the following, each tagged plausible-procedural and shown in the viewer’s tier overlay:
1. 
The stage shares of a segment’s work window (layout, felling and gathering, stacking, head logs) are drawn from a seeded distribution with the sum fixed to the documented 3-4 hour window; the
split itself is an assumption.
2. 
Each regiment’s segment starts within a seeded offset of the brigade start, so the line does not rise in lockstep; no source gives the offsets.
3. 
Thickness and profile follow the sources’ qualitative description (log fence with stone and earth, about 5 ft high). Any numeric thickness is an assumption and is marked as such.
4. 
Seminary rail barricade: height and rail layout are assumptions; length about 600 yd is the only sourced dimension.
5. 
Tool mix beyond the 6th Wisconsin spades is an assumption.

Engine rules from the table.
 Prepared emplacements (G-03 lunettes; G-12 the in-line ridge battery) are built on screen like any other work. Improvised positions (G-01, G-05, G-06, G-07, G-10, G-11)
get no earth; the engine shows guns unlimbered on natural ground with trail marks and, where sourced, hand-hauling or prolonge retirement. Where “no works described” the engine shows open-
ground battery positions and logs the item as a gap. Gun-crew action states come from the artillery notes (unlimber, sponge-and-worm, load, ram, point, prime and fire, recoil and run-up, canister
variant, prolonge retirement, casualties and caisson explosions).
B.7.6 Present-day Culp’s Hill is not the 1863 profile
The earthworks a visitor sees on Culp’s Hill today are rebuilds. Veterans rebuilt works in the late 19th century (Civil War News). The Iron Brigade works on the north side were reconstructed by the
Civilian Conservation Corps in the 1930s (GD-IronBrigade). NPS and the Gettysburg Foundation thinned 18 acres of woodlot from February 2021 and cleared earthworks from March 2021 (CWN-
Culp). 
The engine must not derive an 1863 profile from the modern lidar surface of these works.
 The same caution applies to the Little Round Top breastworks: NPS rehabilitation
reconstructed them with original stones, and many look as in 1863 photographs (AmHer-LRT), but the reconstruction is still a reconstruction. Use the sourced description (log close-fence with stone
and earth, about 5 ft high) plus period photographs for the profile, and tag it inferred.
B.7.7 Archaeology and survey sources
Culp’s Hill dig, 2021:
 about 500 sq ft excavated near Forbes Rock, described as the first systematic archaeology on Culp’s Hill. Finds: percussion caps, buttons, Confederate Gardner bullets,
and a Sharps bullet embedded in breastworks (HN-Dig).
Kreusch and Balicki (2022):
 metal detection and GIS used to test accounts of the Culp’s Hill fight (tDAR).
Little Round Top, 2017:
 photogrammetry (not lidar) of about 52 acres of the western face with eBee and Inspire 2 aircraft (about 390 ft and 295 ft flights), 29 checkpoints. It found earthworks,
stone walls, railroad beds and “many new objects”, with no breastwork or gun-position details published in the notes (LidarNews; GeoCue).
Photographs:
 O’Sullivan photographed the Little Round Top wall days after the battle (NPS-LRT). Photographs of the Cemetery Hill gatehouse and lunette area are dated July 7, 1863 (GD-
Housch). Gardner photographed the Leister farm wall on July 6 (GD-14708).

B.8 Terrain detail: buildings, fences, roads, field hospitals and the time-keyed state schedule
B.8.1 How to read this section
Tiers.
 documented / inferred / plausible-procedural as in B.7.1; “open gap” for items with no source.
Source keys
 resolve in the “Sources for this section” list at the end of the file.
Time-keyed states.
 Every change of state for a structure, fence, earthwork, tree or field appears again in the schedule B.8.7, which the engine reads.
Corrections carried.
 The McPherson barn did not burn on July 1; it survives (NPS restored it in 1978) and the McPherson house burned in 1895. Bliss barn and house were burned deliberately
on the morning of July 3. The Rose barn burned in 1910, the Codori barn was torn down in 1882. None of these later events belongs in the battle animation.
Weak sources.
 Forum posts (CivilWarTalk) and a women’s-history blog are flagged where used; they never stand alone as the source for a tier “documented” item.

B.8.3 Fences
Types (documented).
 Post-and-rail lined both sides of the Emmitsburg Road and was common across the Pickett’s Charge area. Virginia worm (sawbuck) fences ran inside fields and on rocky ground.
Loose stone walls were low (about 2 ft per one critic; “shin high” at the Angle). Picket fences stood in yards (GD-EmmRd; ECW-PostRail; GD-14708). General post-and-rail dimensions from a non-
Gettysburg source: rails about 11 ft, height about 5 ft, posts 6-6.5 ft (ECW-PostRail); flagged as generic, not site data. Fences were destroyed by shooting, by burning for firewood, by pulling down for
works, and by trampling.
Fence / wall
Where
State change and time
Tier
Source

fences
Both sides of the road, Pickett’s Charge area
Intact morning Jul 3. Slowed the charge
about 15:30-16:15; attackers broke ranks to
climb. Heavily hit by bullets and artillery on
Jul 2-3 (one 16-ft rail had 836 holes; a
Confederate described perforated slabs).
Whether the fences decided the charge is
debated. Exact breach points not

GD-14708
NPS reconstructions are contested.
 The present fences are NPS rebuilds: worm fences were built along the Emmitsburg Road, Rose and Bushman farms in June 2010 because they need no post
holes (GD-EmmRd); more than a mile of fence was rebuilt in the Pickett’s Charge fields, and Bliss/Long Lane fences in 2008 along the historic northern Bliss boundary (GD-Mile). NPS’s new Cemetery
Ridge walls are taller (48-52 in) commemorative style, which a critic calls inaccurate (GD-14708). The NPS rehabilitation restored the Pickett’s Charge area to 12 small fields and rebuilt about nine
miles of fence (NPS-Rehab). 
The engine therefore uses the 1863 vector fence layer, the Warren map and photographs, not the modern fence positions, and marks fence type as inferred
wherever no 1863 photograph shows it.
 Open gaps: Peach Orchard fence layout, Wheatfield stone wall details, Little Round Top rail use, Codori and Klingle pull-down specifics, real 1863 locations
of Virginia worm fences, and a documented list of stone walls versus rail fences by location.

ABT-USCC
Camp Letterman date and count conflicts (carried as ranges).
 Opening: July 20 (American Battlefield Trust) vs July 22 (Civil War News, Wikipedia summary). The National Museum of Civil War
Medicine says construction ran from July 10 to July 22. The NPS essay says about 500 tents with patients moved in July 22-27; the Trust says over 400 tents ten feet apart with 12 patients per tent.
Deaths: over 1,200 (Trust); about 17 per day recalled in October. Patient count on Aug 10: 1,772 (NPS essay) vs over 4,000 (Civil War News). Camp features: dead house, embalming tent, cookhouse,
warehouse tent, cemetery of about 1,200 graves, amputated limbs buried in barrels and later sent to the Army Medical Museum.
Casualty totals as the hospital population (conflicts).
 Letterman: 14,193 Union wounded, 6,802 Confederate wounded taken, 20,995 total; over 650 medical officers, 106 left behind and 544 went
with the army; 47 more reported the night of Jul 9. The NPS essay gives about 14,501 Union and about 12,355 Confederate wounded. The 1914 tablet says these hospitals cared for twenty thousand
wounded of both armies. Corps counts do not sum to 14,193 because many men were counted at several sites and in town (inferred).
B.8.5.3 Night operations and working conditions
Surgeons operated in the open on tables near the field, sometimes under tarpaulins, “day and night” until July 6 (July 7 for II Corps) (NPS-Essay5; Letterman-Exc).
Spangler: four operating tables worked night and day under the barn forebay, lanterns burning, which made the forebay safer for flammable anesthesia. Chloroform was used. Cpl Kiefer
wandered with a lantern the night of July 4 seeking blankets (HN-Spangler; ReconEra; GExp).
Weikert: amputated limbs piled outside a window; Weed carried to the basement; hospital moved east early July 3 (HN-Weikert).
XII Corps: wounded removed, sheltered, fed and dressed within six hours; capital operations within 24 hours (Letterman-Exc).
Surgeons did not wash hands or instruments; gangrene and typhoid spread (HN-Spangler).
Rain: cold, constant rain July 4, men exposed against the barn and in the orchard (HN-Spangler). Tillie Pierce carried water to marching columns until the Weikert spring ran dry on July 1 (HN-
Weikert). Plum Run overflowed and drowned some wounded in low ground (AMS).
Burial: dead in shallow graves beside where they fell or in trenches with headboards where known; Spangler orchard burials recorded by name and unit and later moved to the National Cemetery
(NPS-Essay5; ReconEra).

B.8.7 THE TIME-KEYED STATE SCHEDULE
The engine applies each transition at the documented time. A time window is the interval over which a transition can be shown (a fire spreads, a work rises); when the sources give only a date or
“morning”, the window is the plain reading of the source and the entry is flagged. Scrubbing backward reverses every transition.
Legend:
 Tier D = documented, I = inferred, P = plausible-procedural. “Flag” = window or detail is unresolved.

Rows the engine must never schedule:
 the McPherson barn does not burn on July 1 or at any time in the battle; the Bliss buildings do not burn on July 2; the Emmitsburg Road fences are not
destroyed before July 2-3; no avenue exists before the park era.
B.8.8 What the animation must show; damage-state asset requirements
Must-show list (each tied to the schedule):
1. 
Fence segments on the Emmitsburg Road that shatter, are climbed over, and show bullet and shell damage during the charge (S-062), including the Leister, Klingle, Thompson, Brian, Rummel,
Sherfy and Warfield losses at their dates.
2. 
Bliss barn and house burning the morning of Jul 3 (S-052) and the Sherfy barn fire inside its flagged window (S-037).
3. 
Rummel barn with cut loopholes (S-054); Taney house shelled (S-053); Evergreen gatehouse with moderate damage (S-044).
4. 
The Seminary hall with glass lost and bullet holes (S-012), Wade house with bullet strikes (S-051), Leister house with shot holes, down porch and dead horses (S-059).
5. 
Works rising, stage by stage (B.7.4), with the lower Culp’s Hill works empty then occupied then damaged (S-023 to S-056).
6. 
Hospital life: operating tables by lantern at Spangler (S-049), amputation pile at Weikert (S-047), ambulance flow, rain on exposed wounded (S-070), and burial and horse-burning after July 4.

B.9 Terrain and landscape source catalog
This section answers owner requirement 5 (identify every reputable terrain source for a July 1863 landscape, modern and from the 1860s) and feeds requirements 2, 6, 7 and 13. It lists every source
found, what it gives, how to get it, and how far it can be trusted. The terrain pipeline in B.9.2 turns the catalog into a 1:1 landscape with a provenance tag on every object. Nothing here is built; this is
Gettysburg Total War animation plan, version 2 | page 103 of 144


=== PAGE 104 ===
research and planning only.
Method limits, stated plainly. The research passes had several blocked endpoints: loc.gov search and JSON endpoints were blocked to the research tools, several Rumsey and NARA catalog pages
returned verification screens, and the NPS Little Round Top cultural landscape report PDF failed to fetch. Items that depend on those pages are marked “URL unverified”. Claude Code retries these
during execution (Part D, Part E); the retry results go into the Evidence-Gap Register. Nothing below is presented as verified when the notes say it was not.
Reading the tier column. “Reliability tier” in the catalog below is about use as a 1863 landscape source, not about the quality of the archive:
Tier
Meaning
R1
Measured survey or measured geodata. Positional accuracy can be tested.
R2
Near-contemporary map, photograph or record (1858 to about 1876) made by or near the people who saw the
ground.
R3
Later, derived or secondary product (restoration GIS, books, summaries, Wikipedia-level pages). Useful, but must
be cross-checked.
R4
Artistic or composite work, or a forum or blog claim. Low weight. Never the sole basis for any object.
Evidence classes used elsewhere in the report (documented, inferred, plausible-procedural) apply to the finished 3D objects, not to the sources. A source gives a documented basis only for what it
states.

=== PAGE 109 ===
Gettysburg Compiler 1863 newspaper
The LOC “Gettysburg Compiler” runs 1866 to 1961 ( https://www.loc.gov/item/sn83032044 ), so it is not July 1863
reporting. Chronicling America July 1863 batches exist (JSON via 
fo=json
;
https://www.loc.gov/collections/chronicling-america/ ) but are not terrain sources.
B.9.2 Pipeline for building the 1:1 terrain
This is the planned processing chain. It is a reasoned design built on the catalog above; its steps are not documented practice at Gettysburg except where the Knowles and Musselman precedents are
cited. The rule throughout: every output object carries a provenance record (sources used, tier, error estimate, method) so the registers in Part D can be generated from the data.
1. 
LiDAR ingest.
Pull the 2017 QL2 LAS 1.4 tiles for the park footprint (1.5 km tiles, NAD83(2011) UTM 18N, NAVD88 GEOID12B meters), and the PAMAP 2006-08 LAZ tiles (ID 2574) as a second epoch.
Build a bare-earth DEM at 1 m (the delivered DEM) and a denser one from the ground-classified points where the point spacing allows. Keep the non-ground returns separately: they locate
modern trees, buildings, monuments and walls and are used as masks, not as 1863 geometry.
Reconcile datum and unit differences between the two epochs (meters versus feet, GEOID12B versus the PAMAP geoid note) and record the offset. Differences between the two epochs are a
free estimate of modern processing error.
Cross-check the delivered accuracy against the benchmark: QL2 requires 10 cm RMSEz; the sister-project numbers (NVA 0.094 m, VVA 0.120 m) are not confirmed for Adams, so the
pipeline measures vertical error against surveyed monuments or building corners that appear in both datasets.
2. 
De-modernization.
 The modern surface is edited back toward 1863 wherever evidence says the ground changed. Each edit is a documented polygon with its reason and source.
Remove: park avenues and parking lots, the visitor center and other modern buildings, monuments and their bases (the Gettysburg memorial program began after the war), modern cuts and
fills, quarry changes, trails and Little Round Top road work.
Rail line: the unfinished railroad grade (cut up to about 15 ft deep on the first-day field, per the Wikipedia first-day page) is an 1863 feature and stays; later railroad changes are removed or
reversed. What changed after 1863 is determined by comparing the Warren contours at the cut against the lidar.
Method: compare the lidar DEM to the Warren-derived TIN (the same comparison Knowles used) to flag areas where they disagree by more than the combined error. A flagged area is an
edit candidate and must be explained by a known post-1863 disturbance, or else it goes into the register as an unexplained residual, not silently overwritten.
Cross-references: the structure and avenue inventory comes from B.8; the earthwork reconstruction in B.7 is built dynamically over the time-varying ground, never as a pre-placed feature.
3. 
Re-imposing Warren contours with the documented error model.
Georeference the LOC 1886 IIIF sheets and, if retrievable, the NARA full sheets, using the NPS method as the template: scan at 300 dpi or pull IIIF tiles at full resolution, affine registration
on control points (building corners and fence intersections that have not changed), then rubbersheeting. The Musselman method used nine control points; the pipeline uses as many as it can
identify and reports residuals at each.
Trace the 4-ft contours (automated vectorization with manual QA, as Knowles hand-traced onto Mylar), assign elevations, build a TIN.
Where the lidar surface has been modified by known post-1863 work, replace or blend toward the Warren TIN inside the polygon from step 2. Where the two agree, keep the lidar (its
resolution is far finer than 4-ft contours can give).
Error model, stated from the record: Warren hilltops and ridgelines agree with the modern DEM to within about 50 ft (Knowles); creek beds and rolling ground can differ by far more
(hundreds of feet in places). No RMS for Warren is published, so the pipeline derives its own by measuring contour residuals against lidar on ground believed unchanged (hilltops, rock
outcrops), and publishes the result as the error model. Until that run exists, the 50 ft figure and the “creek beds worse” caveat are the only documented bounds.
Contours date from 1868-69, five years after the battle; treat land cover as “about 1868” until checked against 1863 photographs, Cope (Aug-Oct 1863) and Bachelder.
4. 
Drainage: Plum Run, Rock Creek, Marsh Creek (and Stevens Run).
Initial channels come from the Warren map and the lidar hydrology (flow accumulation on the cleaned DEM), compared with the NHD and DCNR lines. Creek beds are the weakest part of
the Warren data (see the error model), so channel positions are treated as plausible-procedural unless a period map or photograph fixes them.
Width, depth and flow are not documented. No Marsh Creek or Rock Creek flow data was found. Plum Run flooding on July 4 (rain total 1.39 in; wounded in low ground reportedly drowned
per the AMS summary) is a weather-driven state change defined in B.10.1 and B.10.6.
Stevens Run may have been wider in 1863 (forum claim only); keep as an open question.
5. 
Fusing 1863 land cover layers.
Layers: Warren land cover and fences (woods, fields, orchards, buildings, roads), the Hopkins 1858 owner map, the Cope sketches, the Bachelder positions for troop-related labelling only,
Elliot’s annotated hospital and grave map, and the NPS 1863 vector approach reproduced from the LOC sheet.
Field-by-field crop attributes, tree species and fence type come from B.10.3 and B.8, with the 1860 agricultural census and the damage claims (Table 4, rows 39 and 41) as the sources that
would convert inference into documented fact.
Modern layers (NPS vegetation map, soils, Penn Pilot aerials) are used to find where modern woods grew over what was open in 1863 and the reverse. The NPS vegetation map cannot
supply 1863 composition.
6. 
Per-object provenance and tier.
 Every fence run, wall, tree stand, orchard, field, building and rock field is stored with: ID, location, extent, source list, source dates, evidence class
(documented, inferred, plausible-procedural), the specific attribute that is inferred, the estimated positional error, and any conflict. Procedural content (individual trees, rock scatter, grass) is
seeded so it reproduces exactly. The product shows the tier on request.
7. 
Accuracy tests.
Photo-match tests: for each documented 1863 photograph with a known or recoverable camera position (Frassanito-style positions; the NPS Then and Now pages; the Gardner and
O’Sullivan shots of Devil’s Den and the Rose Woods), render the model from the camera position with a matching lens and compare ridgelines, rock positions, wall lines and tree trunks. The
pass criterion and the failures are recorded per photograph. Camera positions beyond the NPS Rose Woods example were not found in the notes; recovering them is a task for the pipeline.
Contour residual maps: difference between the final surface and the Warren contours, mapped across the whole field, split by terrain class (hilltop, slope, valley floor, stream).
Independent checks: georeferencing residuals at control points; the 2006-08 versus 2017 lidar difference; viewshed comparison with Knowles’ published statements (Lee saw little of the
Union forces on Day 2); the Codori-Trostle thicket height test.
Staged photographs (the Devil’s Den sharpshooter) are used for terrain and rocks only, never for the position of bodies or props.
8. 
Honest accuracy bound.
 Based on the record, the plan can state this and no more: hilltops and ridgelines of the final surface should be good to roughly the Knowles figure (on the order of 50 ft
or better; to be confirmed by the pipeline’s own residual maps); stream beds, wet ground and rolling low ground may be off by far more and are tagged lower; fences, walls and vegetation are
reconstructions with the per-object tiers described above; individual tree positions, exact fence lines everywhere, field-by-field crop state, path ruts, channel geometry and smoke density cannot
be known and are plausible-procedural. The product shows these classes rather than hiding them.

B.10 Weather, vegetation, light and the 24/7 day-night spec
This section answers owner requirements 8, 11 and 13. B.10.1 to B.10.4 record what the sources say about the physical environment; B.10.5 is the day-night specification the engine must satisfy;
B.10.6 is a recommended photorealistic pipeline; B.10.7 lists what is still missing. All time-of-day values below are local mean time (LMT) unless marked. Weather and plant states are tagged
documented, inferred or open gap, as in the notes.
B.10.1 Weather by day and time
Primary record. The best primary weather record is that of Rev. Prof. Michael Jacobs of Pennsylvania College, who read thermometers at 7 am, 2 pm and 9 pm and also recorded cloud, wind and
pressure. Every value below reached the research notes through secondary summaries (Strategypage/Nofi, Emerging Civil War, TeachersFirst, AMS, HistoryNet, Coastal Point). Jacobs’s original table
was not read directly (B.10.7).

Humidity. Jacobs measured temperature, wind, cloud and pressure but had no moisture instruments. There are no Gettysburg humidity, dew point, pressure or visibility values. Humidity and the heat
index come from two Harrisburg observers about 35 miles away (via 
Coastal Point
 and 
HistoryNet
). The heat index values are therefore estimates derived from a station 35 miles away, flagged as such.
For rendering only, the notes give a guess of about 75 to 90% relative humidity at dawn and 55 to 70% in the afternoon of Jul 2, and “very humid” for Jul 3 and 4. These are inferred, with no measured
values behind them.
Further sourced detail. Jul 3 afternoon: muggy, no breeze, mostly sunny during the Charge (
TeachersFirst
). A mid-June heat wave caused sunstroke on marches of 25 miles or more, and the campaign
saw heat, violent storms and mud (
Statecollege
). The Potomac above 15 ft and torrential rain from Jul 4 made fording at Williamsport impossible until about Jul 12 (
Wikipedia Gettysburg campaign
).
Pvt. Marcus Toney (1st Tennessee) described drinking pond water with “wiggletails and tadpoles”, evidence of water scarcity (
FOX Weather
). Henry Jacobs recalled that low temperatures benefited
the wounded and that frequent rains cleansed the fields, without dates (
Emerging Civil War
).
Conflicts carried as ranges
Conflict
Sources and values
Handling
Wind speed
Emerging Civil War reports “12 mph” for Jul 1-2; TeachersFirst says about
2 mph. Both agree on direction (south; Jul 3 S to SSW) and “gentle”. The
difference is probably a unit or transcription issue.
Open gap. Render a wind of 2 to 6 mph from S to SSW. The range is
inferred.
Jul 4 rain timing
Strategypage: showers 6 am to noon, heavier 1 to 2 pm, very heavy from 9
pm. TeachersFirst: 6 am, 2:15 to 4 pm and 4 am Jul 5. Same total of 1.39 in.
Render a wet showery all day with the heaviest rain from afternoon to
night. The timing stays a range.
Jul 2 sky
Strategypage: foggy dawn and mizzling afternoon. Boston Globe and
Emerging Civil War: clearing in the afternoon.
Foggy or overcast morning, brief drizzle possible midday, clearing late
afternoon with cirrus by evening (Jacobs 9 pm). Inferred.
Jul 3 “87 F” versus “below average”
AMS says Jul 1 to 3 were slightly below average; Nese/Harding and Jacobs
give 87 F.
Inferred resolution: Jul 1 (76 F) and Jul 2 (81 F) were below early-July
norms, and Jul 3 at 87 F in shade was near the modern normal highs
(modern normals are not 1863 normals). The heat on Jul 3 felt worse
because of humidity, wool, sun and exertion. Use 87 F shade and a heat
index of about 100 F for the Charge.
Jul 2 cloud cover at 2 pm
One TeachersFirst reading shows “3/10” and the summary is ambiguous
about clear versus covered.
Carried as ambiguous.

B.10.2 Astronomy (computed)
Time convention. Local time in 1863 was local mean time; there were no standard time zones. LMT at Gettysburg is UTC minus 5 h 09 m. The modern EST clock is therefore LMT plus about 9 minutes,
and the modern EDT clock is LMT plus 1 h 9 min. Every time below is LMT, which runs about 9 minutes behind the modern EST clock. For example, a sunrise of 04:36 LMT on Jul 3 equals 05:45 on a
modern EDT clock. Source accounts of the battle use whatever clock the writer had; B.2 handles the conflicts in times.
Method and caveat. The values were computed by code (astropy 8.0.2, builtin ephemeris, site 39.83 N, 77.23 W, 170 m; sun thresholds of -0.833, -6, -12 and -18 degrees). A JPL kernel download was
blocked, so the builtin ephemeris was used. The only independent check is the full moon time, which matches the published value to within one minute; rise and set times are not independently
checked against USNO. The local horizon was not modelled: South Mountain to the west would make the apparent sunset earlier by an estimated 10 to 20 minutes, and the Round Tops block local
horizons. The computed times are therefore astronomical-horizon times, and the engine should apply the local horizon from the terrain model, not a fixed offset.

Sunrise is about 04:35 and sunset about 19:32 LMT on all four days (04:36 and 19:31 on the edges). Strategypage gives 0435/1933 for Jul 1 to 0437/1932 for Jul 4 with the time basis not stated; the
computed values agree to within about a minute. Twilight ranges: astronomical twilight begins 02:33 to 02:35, nautical 03:21 to 03:23, civil 04:02 to 04:04; in the evening civil twilight ends 20:04 to
20:05, nautical 20:45 to 20:46, astronomical 21:32 to 21:33.

At the dusk fighting of 7 to 8 pm on Jul 2 the sun was at 4.5 degrees falling to civil twilight end at 20:04, and the East Cemetery Hill assault (about 8 pm) ended in near-dark with the moon still below

Full Moon: 1 July 1863 at 06:46 UT, which is 01:37 LMT on Jul 1 (

). The previous full moon was 1 Jun 23:29 UT (a total lunar eclipse), and new moons were Jun 16 and Jul 15. The computed
opposition (06:45 UT) matches to within a minute.

documented plus inferred
Moonlight level. Epic gives about 0.26 lux for a zenith moon. At 30 to 40 degrees altitude and 90 to 99% illumination, ground illuminance is estimated at about 0.1 to 0.2 lux (inferred; includes phase
and altitude scaling). Under 7/10 cumulus or overcast, a factor of 3 to 10 less. Cloud effects on lux are inferred, not measured.
Stars and smoke. With no moon before about 21:00 LMT on Jul 2 and about 22:00 on Jul 3, the first night hours (astronomical twilight ends about 21:33) were dark enough for stars on a clear sky, but
smoke, haze and cloud mostly hid them. Sources on star visibility: open gap. No account was found of smoke affecting moonlight; the inference is that low smoke near the lines would soften but not
extinguish the moon, and that haze would make moonrise an orange disc.
B.10.3 Vegetation and plant growth
Direct evidence is thin. The honest summary: the Wheatfield held standing wheat on Jul 2; Big Round Top was wooded; the Sherfy Peach Orchard existed; Rose Woods, Herbst Woods and the Culp’s
Hill timber existed; Little Round Top was rocky and partly bare (accounts conflict on summit woodland). Everything about crop stages, grass height, fruit state and species is inference from
agricultural calendar knowledge in the notes and must be verified.
What is sourced (tier: documented unless stated)
Item
What the source says
Source
Wheatfield
Roughly 20 acres, with Rose Woods on its west side; Stony Hill, Houck’s
Ridge and Devil’s Den adjoining. Standing wheat was clipped by
Confederate fire in the fighting (Pfanz, relayed secondhand on a forum, low
reliability); 19th-century wheat is said to be about 4 ft tall, chest-high on a
soldier; whether ripe, cut or shocked is not confirmed. A popular essay (low
reliability, no cited source) says soldiers called it “breast high and ready to
be cut” and that after the fighting it was trodden flat.
Wikipedia second day
; 
CivilWarTalk
; 
Gettysburg Addressed
Ogden wheat field
The tenant farmer Francis Ogden held the wheat in an adjacent field (now
called Rose’s meadow); cavalry camped at its edge on Jul 1; about 50
bushels each of oats and corn were taken from Rose’s stores. The Rose
farm was 225 acres, with Rose Woods, Stony Hill and the Ravine, and the
Ogden field is distinct from the Wheatfield.
Savas Beatie excerpt
Peach Orchard
Joseph Sherfy’s orchard along the west side of the Emmitsburg Road; the
picket line ran along an orchard next to Rose Lane. Sherfy farmed about 50
acres west of the road; 114 trees survived the battle; the orchard was later
replanted (1883 per Wikipedia, 2008 replanting of 72 peach trees per
American Battlefield Trust), so modern trees are not original.
Savas Beatie
; 
Wikipedia Gettysburg Battlefield
; 
Stone Sentinels
; 
American
Battlefield Trust
Orchards in general
44 orchards existed on present park land during the war.
American Battlefield Trust (same)
Big Round Top
Highest point, steeply sloped, heavily wooded.
Wikipedia Gettysburg Battlefield
Little Round Top
Conflict: unwooded but steep and rocky (Battlefield page) versus bare west
slope with lightly wooded summit, east and south slopes (Little Round Top
page). Handling: treat the west face as open boulder and grass, with sparse
trees on the crest and rear slopes.
Wikipedia Gettysburg Battlefield
; 
Wikipedia Little Round Top
Devil’s Den, Plum Run
Boulder-strewn diabase hill at the south end of Houck’s Ridge; Plum Run
valley rocky and broken (“Slaughter Pen”, “Valley of Death”).
Wikipedia Devil’s Den
; 
Wikipedia second day
Logging
Some battlefield areas were logged before the battle.
Wikipedia Gettysburg Battlefield
First-day field
Herbst Woods south of the Chambersburg Pike; fences along the pike;
unfinished railroad cut up to about 15 ft deep.
Wikipedia first day
Fences and road
Fences slowed the Charge; the Emmitsburg Road was sunken about 300 yd
from the Union line; ground slightly undulating.
Wikipedia Pickett’s Charge
Burning barn
A 77th NY soldier recalled a burned barn, with flames spreading through
wheat straw (single source, low reliability).
Gettysburg Addressed
Known corrections (B.2 and B.8 own the detail): the McPherson barn did not burn on Jul 1; the Bliss barn and house were burned deliberately on the morning of Jul 3 by the 14th Connecticut.

What is inferred and must be verified before final use
All of the following are general agricultural and ecological knowledge in the research notes, not retrieved sources.
Subject
Inferred state, early July
Verification needed
Winter wheat
Typically ripens late June to mid-July in south-central Pennsylvania; on Jul 2
the Wheatfield was probably golden-straw or ripening and standing, about
3.5 to 4.5 ft. Some neighbouring fields may already have been cut and
shocked (harvest was season-wide, and the damp Jun 25 to 27 may have
delayed it).
Pfanz; 1860 agricultural census; damage claims
Rye
Earlier and taller (about 5 ft); mostly ripe or cut by early July
Same
Oats
Green-yellow, about 2.5 to 3 ft, headed
Same
Corn
Knee to waist high (about 1 to 3 ft), green, rows hilled
Same
Potatoes
In flower
Same
Timothy and clover meadows
At or past hay-cutting time; some cut, some tall standing, 2.5 to 3.5 ft
Same
Pastures
Grazed short, but many farms had stock and horses requisitioned by
Confederate forces in late June
Same
Orchards
Cherries ripe or ripening, peaches green and unripe (peaches do not ripen
in Pennsylvania in early July), apples small and green; trees in full summer
leaf; low grass or pasture beneath
No sourced fruit-state account for early July 1863
Woodlots
Oak (chestnut oak, red oak, white oak), hickory, chestnut (the blight had
not yet arrived), tulip poplar, black walnut near streams; understory of
dogwood, sassafras and mountain laurel or huckleberry type on the rocky
ridges; dense full-leaf July canopy; heavy understory on Big Round Top; thin
boulder-and-grass on the Little Round Top west face; oak and chestnut
woodlots on Culp’s Hill with dark lower layers and little ground cover in
thinned lots; vines (grape, Virginia creeper, poison ivy) on edges and fences
NPS CLRs; Pfanz; no sourced species list for Culp’s Hill or Big Round Top
Chestnut
Chestnut blight arrived about 1904, so modern lidar canopy and NPS
species lists under-represent 1863 chestnut. This is general knowledge, not
a retrieved source.
Verify
Weeds and wildflowers
Queen Anne’s lace, chicory, daisies, black-eyed Susan, milkweed, thistle,
mullein along fence rows and roadsides
Verify
Fence materials
Rail (chestnut or oak) worm fences, post-and-rail, some stone walls
B.8
Tree height
Must come from species and age assumptions and period photographs;
Warren shows woods by symbol and not height
Photo test (B.9.2 step 7)
Species lists above are labeled inferred. Modern NPS lists (white oak, white ash, northern red oak dominant) describe the park as it is now, after the rehabilitation program, and must not be copied
into 1863.
NPS rehabilitation caveats
The park removes trees that grew in the last 65 to 70 years and replants orchards, so today’s landscape is a partial interpretive proxy for 1863. Replanted orchards exceed the 1863 extent in at least
one case (Gilbert). Park policy aims at the 1863 look, but modern lidar canopy and modern aerials show the post-1863 forest history as well. Use the NPS vegetation map to see where trees are now,
and use the Warren/Cope/photo evidence to decide where they were in 1863.

Source list and evidence class
Pipeline
documented
Damage by fire and trampling. Damage is an event-driven state change on the field polygon. The Wheatfield on Jul 2 starts as standing wheat and receives a trample and shot-down mask as troops
move through it (the sources say fire clipped the wheat and the crop was trodden flat afterwards). A burning building adjacent to straw or hay is a fire spread source: the single-source barn-and-straw
recollection is the only documented basis, so burn spread is plausible-procedural, started only at documented fires (the Bliss buildings on the morning of Jul 3; see B.8 for other fire events). Fences,
orchards and trees are damaged by the same event system as in B.8 and B.7, so damage appears at the correct time and is never pre-placed. The trample mask persists for the rest of the animation
(and is overwritten by rain flattening on Jul 4).
B.10.4 Smoke, dust and sound conditions
Topic
What is sourced
What is inferred or open
Source
Cannonade smoke, Jul 3
The cannonade began about 1 pm and lasted 1 to 2
hours (the end is given as 2:00, 2:30 or 3:00); smoke
concealed overshooting shells and obscured targets for
both sides. Historian Symonds called it possibly the
loudest man-made sound on the continent
(paraphrased).
Humid, light-wind air meant smoke hung and thickened
rather than clearing (inferred).
Wikipedia Pickett’s Charge
Wind and smoke drift
Jul 1 to 3 wind from S to SSW (Jacobs), gentle; no
breeze at the Charge
Smoke drifts toward N to NNE, which is to the right as
seen from Union guns on Cemetery Ridge looking west.
With 0 to 6 mph, smoke from batteries should drift
slowly NNE, pooling and layering in low ground in front
of Seminary Ridge and along Cemetery Ridge; white-
grey near guns, brown or black from burning buildings;
Strategypage; Emerging Civil War; TeachersFirst

=== PAGE 114 ===
visibility down to tens of meters inside batteries
(inferred)
Acoustic shadow
General evidence only: wind currents, topographic
obstructions and wind, temperature and pressure
conditions can silence a battle near it while distant
listeners hear it; Civil War examples include Iuka,
Seven Pines, Gaines’ Mill, Perryville and Five Forks.
The page does not mention Gettysburg.
No Gettysburg-specific account was found. A humid,
calm, warm day with a south wind: sound carries best
downwind (north), so listeners to the north (toward
Harrisburg) are likelier to hear it, and those south or
southwest (toward Maryland) may be in a weaker zone.
A thunderstorm about 6 pm on Jul 3 added thunder.
Audibility distances: open gap.
Wikipedia Acoustic shadow
Dust
Jun 28 to 30 dry; ground “mostly dry” Jul 1 (Boston
Globe summary)
Roads (Chambersburg Pike, Emmitsburg Road,
Taneytown Road) were dusty on Jul 1 to 2 and trampled
to dust under marching columns; mud after the Jul 3
evening storm and the Jul 4 rain. No direct Jul 1 to 2
dust accounts were found (open gap).
Boston Globe

None found

n/a

B.10.5 THE 24/7 DAY-NIGHT SPEC
This is the binding specification for owner requirement 11. In one sentence: the animation runs the full battle around the clock; night looks clearly like night, with a dimmed, darker sky; yet every
soldier, fire, building, gun and bit of activity remains visible, only dimmer and cooler in tone. A user scrubbing to 2 am on Jul 3 must see the units that were there and what they were doing, never a
black screen.
Acceptance criteria
1. 
Time of day follows the real clock, driven by one timeline value (LMT minutes), from before Jul 1 dawn through Jul 4 night, with the computed ephemeris table for the sun and moon (B.10.2).
2. 
Night reads as night. Sun below -0.833 degrees means sky darkened according to the twilight bands; at astronomical night the sky is clearly dark with stars (where cloud permits) and the moon
where it is above the horizon.
3. 
Readability floor. At every instant, a viewer can identify units, activity and terrain features in the free camera at gameplay distance. This is an artistic exposure choice, applied on top of a
physically motivated light setup, and is stated openly (the physical moon would show almost nothing in shadows).
4. 
Light sources that exist historically and are tied to the timeline are visible and plausible: fires, lanterns, burning buildings, muzzle and shell flashes. Where the sources are silent, the source is
inferred and labelled.
5. 
Cloud cover per night follows B.10.2 (overcast Jul 1 to 2, cirrus Jul 2 to 3, 7/10 cumulus with gaps Jul 3 to 4, wet Jul 4 to 5), so the nights are not identical.
6. 
Night does not hide the Evidence tier display: unit markers and tier tags stay legible at night.
Rendering recipe (UE5)
The facts from the Epic documentation are cited in the sources; the parameter values for the readability boost are starting values to tune, not Epic figures (inferred design).
Step
Setting
Source of the number
1. Ephemeris drive
Bake a per-minute table of sun and moon altitude and azimuth for Jul 1 to
5, 1863 from the astropy computation. Convert to rotation of two
directional lights (pitch = minus altitude, yaw = azimuth minus the map’s
north offset). Time axis is LMT. This avoids relying on the Sun Position
Calculator plugin, whose documentation page could not be fetched:
whether it accepts the year 1863 and a fractional time zone of -5.149 h is
an open check, and it likely handles only the sun, not moon phase.
inferred design (plugin check is a gap)
2. Two atmosphere lights
Sky Atmosphere with Atmosphere Sun Light Index 0 for the sun and Index
1 for the moon.
Epic docs
3. Sun
About 120,000 lux at zenith (disc 0.545 degrees); the sky contributes about
20% of the total illuminance.
Epic docs
4. Moon
Physical value about 0.26 lux at zenith (disc 0.568 degrees); the second
light has a weaker sky effect because multiscattering is not evaluated for it.
Scale intensity by the computed illumination fraction and by altitude.
Epic docs; scaling inferred
5. Moon phase
Moon-disc texture or material scaled by computed illumination; waning
gibbous, 99% (Jul 1) to 68% (Jul 5); bright limb toward the sun, so the lit
side faces the west-northwest horizon after the moon rises in the east.
inferred from computed elongation
6. Sky Light
Real-time capture on; Epic notes that the 9-frame time slicing supports
time-of-day change.
Epic docs
7. Stars
Skydome material (Unlit, Opaque, Is Sky) with a star and Milky Way
cubemap, faded by moon brightness and cloud cover.
Epic docs for material option; fade design inferred
8. Exponential height fog
Fog density and color per hour; a second fog layer for valley fog; volumetric
fog on; set height fog inscattering to black and use Mie scattering as
height-fog contribution where desired. Higher density in Plum Run, Rock
Creek and Marsh Creek valleys at the Jul 2 dawn.
Epic docs; density values inferred
9. Auto exposure
Histogram mode. Min EV100 about -4 to -2, with Speed Up about 1 to 2
stops per second, so night brightens automatically while days stay normal.
Exposure compensation is log2 (+1 doubles). A night-only compensation
curve of +1 to +2 keyed to sun altitude. Local Exposure must be configured
when using Lumen.
Epic docs for the mechanism; numbers are the notes’ starting values
(inferred)
10. Physical check
A full moon at 0.26 lux is about EV100 -3 (the notes’ estimate, from
log2(0.26/2.5)), so without compensation the shadows would show almost
nothing.
inferred
11. Moon boost and tint
Moon intensity boost 5 to 20 times with a cool blue tint, slight desaturation
(Purkinje shift) and low contrast.
inferred design
12. Fill light
A dim, shadowless fill directional light, or raised Sky Light intensity, for
overall readability.
inferred design
13. Lumen and emissive fires
Campfires about 2000 to 2500 K, flickering; point lights with attenuation
radii 5 to 15 m. Lumen culls small emissive meshes from the Lumen Scene,
so only screen traces see them: enable Emissive Light Source in the level
instance or raise Lumen Scene Detail so small fires contribute global
illumination. Foliage is in the Surface Cache only if Nanite; software
tracing needs Affect Distance Field Lighting for foliage. Software tracing
range is 200 m by default (to 800 m); Far Field 1 km needs World Partition
HLOD1.
Epic Lumen docs; light values inferred
14. Hospital lanterns
Minimal lantern lights at field hospitals.
inferred
15. Cloudy and stormy nights
Same boost but with lower moon-disc visibility and softened shadows on Jul
1 to 2; Jul 3 to 4 night with rain particles and lightning as a very short high-
intensity directional pulse.
inferred
16. Transitions
One timeline value drives light rotation and intensity, fog density and color,
cloud coverage, rain and wetness, and exposure compensation. Cross-fade
weather presets over 10 to 30 simulated minutes to avoid popping.
inferred design
Limits: the physical moon value is for a zenith moon; the boost factors are artistic, not physical, and the product declares this in its settings (a “physically dim” toggle may be provided for comparison,
but the default is the readable setting required by the owner).

B.10.6 Photorealistic environment pipeline
These are reasoned recommendations built on the Epic documentation read in the research notes; they are design proposals, not a description of anything built. The parameter values are starting
points to tune.
Component
Recommendation
Grounding
Physically based sky
Sky Atmosphere (Rayleigh for blue sky and red sunsets; Mie for haze and
sun halo; ozone absorption) with the two-light sun/moon arrangement of
B.10.5. Raise the Mie scattering and aerial perspective view distance for
the hazy distant ridgelines of Jul 2 to 3.
Epic Sky Atmosphere docs; the haze setting is inferred
Volumetric clouds
Material-driven volume clouds, cloud-shadow casting on the atmosphere
light, coverage as a material parameter driven by the hourly weather
timeline: 100% stratus on Jul 1 (light grey, base about 800 to 1,500 m,
inferred); partial clearing with cirrus on Jul 2 evening; 40% cumulus at 2
pm and 70% by evening with a storm anvil at 6 pm on Jul 3; overcast with
steady rain on Jul 4. Clouds appear in reflections only through the Sky
Light, and ray-marched hardware RT is not supported.
Epic Volumetric Cloud docs; coverage values from Jacobs readings (B.10.1)
Procedural foliage by species
PCG graphs with per-biome rules: Surface Sampler on the landscape,
Transform Points for rotation and scale variation, Static Mesh Spawner for
trees, attribute filters (custom attributes allowed), hierarchical generation.
Woodlots use the species weights of B.10.3 with understory density falling
off by slope and rock mask; orchards use a grid with a spacing attribute;
fence lines are splines with rail-fence assets; hedgerow and weeds along
fences. Trees with summer leaf from a SpeedTree-style or Megascans-style
library, Nanite where possible so Lumen’s surface cache includes foliage.
Epic PCG and Lumen docs; species weights inferred
Grass and crop simulation
Wheat (amber, 1.1 to 1.3 m), oats (about 0.8 m), corn (0.4 to 0.9 m rows),
hay meadow (0.8 to 1.0 m), and cut stubble variants as a per-field attribute;
instanced meshes or card-based grass with per-instance wind from the
global wind vector. A per-field state attribute (standing, cut, trampled,
burned) and a dynamic trample mask (runtime virtual texture or painted
heightfield mask) give the temporal change the owner requires.
inferred design; crop heights from B.10.3
Wind
One global wind vector (S to SSW, 2 to 6 mph, gusts before the Jul 3 storm)
feeds foliage materials, smoke and dust.
B.10.1
Wet surfaces and rain
A global wetness parameter from 0 to 1 driving darker albedo, lower
roughness and puddle masks: 0 on Jul 1 to the afternoon of Jul 3, about 0.3
after the 6 pm storm of Jul 3, 1.0 on Jul 4. Rain via Niagara, mud on roads
from the evening of Jul 3, puddles on Jul 4, and Plum Run flooding as a
spline-based water body with a raised level (July 4). Powder misfires and
mud-bound artillery on Jul 4 are secondary-source claims and are handled
as such in the combat model.
Rain documented (B.10.1); parameters inferred
Volumetric smoke
Niagara smoke from artillery bursts (white-grey) and building fires (dark
orange and black); a persistent low smoke haze volume tied to firing
intensity, feeding a local volumetric fog density scalar (Local Fog Volume if
available; that documentation was not fetched). Muzzle flashes as short
strong point lights with light function; burning buildings as emissive
meshes plus flicker lights and Niagara embers.
Smoke drift direction and persistence: B.10.4 (inferred)
Dust
Niagara dust emitters under marching columns and gun wheels on Jul 1 to
2 and Jul 3 daytime, reversed to mud after the Jul 3 evening storm.
Dry road conditions are sourced to a Boston Globe summary; amounts
inferred
Fog
A second exponential height fog layer for the Jul 2 dawn, denser in the
B.10.1 (documented that dawn was foggy; valley placement inferred)

HathiTrust and the Internet Archive, the NPS Seminar Papers and cultural landscape reports on npshistory.com, and the state monument volumes. Their reading is a Claude Code task, and what the
library does not contain stays in the register with a confidence tier.

B.11 Items to treat as unverified
The following are in the notes only as “background, unverified” or forum material and 
must not be presented as fact
: the Zouave, Bucktail, sack-coat and corps-badge specifics; Confederate battle-
flag details; battery sizes, team sizes and crew roles beyond the nine-man Napoleon crew figure; farm-hospital names; Reynolds’s death time; the commander details recalled from memory; Whitworth
performance; Wheatfield crop state; Devil’s Den geology; Stevens Run width; the Pittsburgh-heard-it claim as a precise July 3 fact; the Ellis acoustic attribution; the shoe legend; the “2% mortality”
Letterman figure; the Custer hat sentence; the “nearly 8,000 killed outright” figure.
B.12 Epilogue: the July 4 close and the November 19, 1863 dedication (requirement R22)
What this section is.
 The owner added this after reviewing version 2. The animation’s main film path ends with the events of July 4 on and around the battlefield (the wounded and dead being
collected, the informal truce, the Confederate withdrawal that begins that evening in rain). At the end of July 4 the animation does a swirling time jump to November 19, 1863, and shows the
Gettysburg Total War animation plan, version 2 | page 116 of 144


=== PAGE 117 ===
dedication of the Soldiers’ National Cemetery from the point of view of a person in the front row, with an accurate version of Lincoln taking the stage and giving the Gettysburg Address. Nothing is
built; this is the research and the specification.
Scope rule.
 The battle is July 1 to 3, 1863. Events outside Gettysburg (for example operations in other theatres) are not shown and are not in the timeline. July 4 material that happened at Gettysburg
(casualty collection, burial, field hospitals, the truce, the withdrawal) stays, and rows T3-145 to T3-167 are the July 4 backbone. The rows after July 4 (T3-168 onward: Monterey Pass, the pursuit to the
Potomac, Camp Letterman) remain in the timeline as optional chapters that a viewer can scrub to, but they are not on the default film path, which goes from the end of July 4 straight to November 19.
Evidence quality.
 This section rests on two secondary pages that Claude Code fetched (the Wikipedia article on the consecration, and a student blog post in the Gettysburg Compiler citing Klement’s
1993 book), one eyewitness-compilation page, and search results for further pages that were not fetched. The Wikipedia page itself flags several claims as unverified. Primary texts (Hale’s report, the
Associated Press and Philadelphia Press reports, Lamon, Hay, Nicolay, Wills’s papers, the five Lincoln manuscripts) were not read. They are the Phase 1 targets for this section. Everything below is
tiered, and everything the fetched sources do not state is listed as a gap, not filled.
B.12.1 The jump (a presentation device, not a historical claim)
Item
Specification
Tier
Start
Last frame of the default film path: the night of July 4 (T3-162 to T3-167):
rain, Confederate columns leaving on the Fairfield road, Union camps,
churches full of wounded.
documented (rows)
Transition
A camera rise and swirl (spiral time-lapse), with the world state
interpolated through the documented intervening changes: August
purchase of the 17 acres, the layout work, burial parties, the start of
reinterments (T3-218), then the season turning from midsummer to late
autumn. It is labeled in the viewer as a time jump, not as history.
plausible-procedural (device)
Landing
A front-row viewpoint at the dedication, in the audience facing the
platform, on the morning and afternoon of November 19.
the choice of view is the owner’s instruction
Scrubbing
November 19 is its own time chapter on the same scrubbable timeline; the
viewer can scrub within it and can leave the front-row viewpoint into the
free camera.

B.12.4 Lincoln and the front-row viewpoint: what is known and what is not
Question
What the fetched sources support
Gap and plan
Where Lincoln sat
A photo analysis reportedly shows him seated to the left of several men
wearing sashes on the speakers’ platform (EWH).
Exact seat, and who was beside him. Source: the Frassanito analysis and
the enlarged-photo literature.
What Lincoln wore, his hat, gloves, mourning band, boots
Not stated in any fetched source.
Not asserted here. Common accounts exist but were not retrieved, so they
are in Leads to verify. The film does not use them until two sources are
read in the Phase 1 audit.
His height, face, posture, gait
Not stated in the fetched sources.
Photographs from 1863 (including the two procession photographs
identified in the Library of Congress in 2006 per W-CONS, and Gardner’s
portraits in November) are the references; build from them under the same
photo-reference rule as other commanders (B.4, B.5).
His voice and accent
One source says he had a pronounced Kentucky accent (W-CONS). No
recording of Lincoln exists (inferred; confirm in the audit).
The voice is synthesized from the descriptions, labeled in the viewer as a
reconstruction, never as a recording.
The words he spoke
He spoke very slowly and Hale took it down (W-CONS). Lincoln wrote
several manuscript copies afterwards that differ in wording, and the text as
delivered, as printed by reporters, and as later revised are not the same.
The count of 271 words in the T3-218 note refers to one of them.
The wording of each version is held as a selectable alternative in the
conflict register. Default: the text reporters printed on the day, with the
revised copies available. This is open until Hale, the Associated Press and
Philadelphia Press reports and the manuscripts are read.
Delivery
Under two minutes (EWH); a few minutes at most (W-CONS).
Pace the performance to the shorter range; reaction per E-011.
What the people near the front looked like and did
A photo shows boys at the edge of the crowd and men on the platform at a
distance (EWH).
The front row is unidentified: the viewpoint is a generic period audience
member, not a named person. Seats, distance to the platform and sight
lines are computed from the platform footprint once S-083 is settled.
The viewpoint
A camera at eye height among the standing or seated audience in front of
the platform, facing it, with ambient crowd sound, bands, the speakers,
wind and the distant gunfire-free quiet of the grounds.
Spatial audio per R06: speech intelligibility at the front row is a computed
result, not a guess.
The ceremony’s duration
Several hours, dominated by Everett.
The film shows the music and prayer briefly, compresses Everett with a
visible time-skip, and plays Lincoln’s remarks in real time.
B.12.5 Leads to verify for this section (not asserted as fact above)
Lincoln’s clothing and accessories on the day (the commonly cited black suit, tall hat and white gloves were not in any fetched source).
Lincoln’s height and the descriptions of his voice as high-pitched (not in the fetched sources).
Where Lincoln stayed on the night of November 18 (the Wills house claim is flagged by the source itself).
The exact start time of the ceremony, and the number of words in each of the five manuscripts.
The identity and position of the photographers on November 19 (not named in the fetched sources).
Hale’s contemporaneous text and the Associated Press and Philadelphia Press texts, from Chronicling America and the Library of Congress Lincoln papers.

B.12.6 What this adds to the plan
New requirement R22 (see Part D.1 and Part E Section A) with its own phase (D.7 phase 15) and gate (Q28).
A second world state for the viewer: the battlefield in November 1863, built from the same terrain with a different time-keyed state set (S-080 to S-088 above), a different season, and the
cemetery ground.
A crowd of civilians, soldiers and widows in period civilian and military dress, with reduced animation needs (mostly standing, seated, listening).
A character build of Lincoln from photographs under the likeness rule used for commanders, and a synthesized voice labeled as such.

PART C. Feasibility and production technology
C.0 Evidence quality in this part
The production-technology researcher states that Epic and Unity documentation, Creative Assembly talks, Weta budgets, LiDAR specifications and musket acoustics were 
not retrieved
, and that items
tagged “background, unverified” come from training knowledge. Those items appear here only as flagged hypotheses. No independent evidence was found that Claude Code has completed a project of
this scale.
C.1 Crowds and engines: what scale is credibly reachable
Documented patterns.
 A community Unreal “Mass Battle” plugin combines Mass, Niagara GPU particles, vertex animation textures (VAT) with LODs, the AnimToTexture plugin and a nav-mesh flow
field; it lists State Tree support, replication and mobile as unfinished and gives no agent counts, frame rates or hardware (
UE forum
). Ultimate Epic Battle Simulator 2’s developer says the GPU
handles rendering, culling, animation, transforms and AI, and a commenter claims “hundreds of thousands to millions” of characters (anecdotal, stylized, no official cap) (
Steam
). Massive Software
agents are driven by per-agent “brains” with variables such as likelihood to fight or gait choice, and a custom Horse & Rider agent was built for Return of the King (
VFX Voice
). The Total War lineage
(Part A) supplies the closest game-industry precedent: low-rate simulation, interpolated rendering, LOD by on-screen size.
What is not supported.
 No retrieved source gives hard agent-per-GPU numbers, Mass scaling numbers, Nanite or Lumen limits for skinned or foliage-heavy scenes, Unity DOTS or Godot limits, Total
War or Bannerlord unit caps, or per-agent memory budgets. “Real-time 160,000 individually animated Hollywood-quality agents is unsupported by any retrieved evidence; it is an engineering-heavy
LOD and impostor problem, not a switch” (researcher’s takeaway). Unverified background the researcher offered, to be checked against Epic and Unity documentation before the plan relies on it: that
Epic’s City Sample used Mass for tens of thousands of pedestrians and vehicles on console-class hardware, that Nanite historically did not support skinned meshes, and that Total War battles typically
cap near 10,000-40,000 on-screen soldiers with simplified models. For an offline deliverable, Massive, Golaem or Houdini crowds with an offline renderer is the proven route (the Lord of the Rings
precedent), whereas a real-time free camera at that fidelity is “a different, harder product”.
C.2 Baked playback versus live simulation; scrubbing three days
The researcher’s reasoning, which this report endorses as a recommendation (not a sourced finding): a historically scripted battle is better served by authored or baked per-agent trajectories (position,
facing, action state, event times) than emergent live AI, because accuracy needs controlled outcomes and scrubbing needs random access. Massive precedent supports directed rather than fully
emergent behavior. Back-of-envelope (researcher’s arithmetic): 160,000 agents at 1 sample per second and about 32 bytes is about 5 MB/s, or about 1.3 TB over 259,200 s of three days raw; storing
waypoints and action events, lower rates when idle, and delta compression could cut that by one to two orders of magnitude to tens of GB. This is an order-of-magnitude estimate only. Random-access
scrubbing needs periodic full snapshots plus deltas; floating-point nondeterminism makes live re-simulation unsuitable for exact replay unless fixed-point or seeded deterministic code is used.
Recommended architecture: an offline history layer (units, orders, timings, positions with per-datum confidence), a deterministic offline simulation that fills individual-agent detail, and a baked cache
streamed at runtime. No source covers Sequencer, Alembic, USD or Geometry Cache sizes, or Mass and Chaos determinism.
C.3 Character pipeline: scanning, capture, animation
Only horse capture was costed: Massive’s horse capture was about 180 actions, about $100,000 before processing, four Arabian horses flown from Sydney, a roughly 150-camera stage plus a ranch day
(
VFX Voice
). Massive supports motion-tree transitions between captured actions. 
Version 1 background, superseded by C.10 (no physical capture is planned):
 costs of human reenactor capture,
MetaHuman licensing and crowd suitability, photogrammetry costs, Xsens and Vicon pricing, cloth and muscle simulation cost, and artillery-crew animation precedent were 
not retrieved
.
Unverified background, superseded by C.10 (no capture sessions are planned; these actions are authored procedurally or taken from open motion datasets): a clip list covering the nine-step loading
drill, firing, bayonet charge and melee, falling and dying variants, wounded crawl, artillery crew drill, horse-artillery limbering, saber charge, officers, drummers and color bearers, with dozens to a
few hundred base clips and variation by blending, speed and offset randomization; inertial suits are cheaper and field-usable while optical stages are better for horses and contact; population variety
through modular mesh kits and per-instance material parameters. Historically correct distinctness per regiment is a data problem (Part B), not a rendering problem.
C.4 Provenance and the limits of “100%”
The headline numbers are unsettled even at the top: one secondary table gives engaged Union 93,921 and Confederate 71,699 (total 165,620) and casualties 23,055 and 23,231, with the source
column only “Various” and no uncertainty (
FortWiki
); Part B shows alternative figures. NPS rehabilitation drew on 1863, 1868 and 1872 maps, photographs, sketches, aerials, reports, letters, diaries
and newspapers (
NPS
). The 1993 film 
Gettysburg
 shot on the real battlefield with thousands of volunteer reenactors on a $20M budget and 254-minute runtime (
Wikipedia
). The researcher’s
recommended provenance schema (claim, source, confidence tier, render-time flag) is formalized in Appendix B.
C.5 Environment: terrain, vegetation, weather, smoke
Terrain geometry is obtainable from LiDAR (Part B.8). The ground is today’s ground with 1863 corrections: post-1863 change (roads, quarrying, monument grading, railroad cuts, development) must
be reconciled with 1860s maps and photographs. Sub-field vegetation, fences and crops are partially documented. The researcher’s suggestion, adopted as a recommendation: scatter plausible
vegetation procedurally within documented polygons and label it inferred or procedural. Black-powder smoke, dust and fire are visual-effects problems (Niagara volumetrics or Houdini Pyro) that are
expensive but not evidence-limited; real-time density across 160,000 firing agents needs LOD’d approximated volumes (researcher’s inference). Unverified background: 3DEP QL2 is nominally about 2
points/m2 and about 1 m DEM.
C.6 Audio and acoustic shadows
Documented.
 Acoustic shadows arise from absorption, wind shear, temperature gradients and inversions, and repeated reflection, creating zones of audibility and silence; sound from Gettysburg not
heard about 10 miles away was heard clearly in Pittsburgh, about 150 miles away (
Acoustical Society of America
). A popular summary adds that on July 2 Ewell never heard Longstreet’s artillery
signal, which Ross attributes to ridges and hot ground refracting sound upward, and says Pittsburgh residents reportedly heard the battle on July 1 while Taneytown, 12 miles away, did not; that article
does not mention Charles Ellis (
Today I Found Out
). Other cases: Seven Pines, Fort Donelson, Iuka, Chancellorsville. 
Caveats:
 the Ellis attribution is unverified; the Pittsburgh claim is dated July 1 in
one source and described generally in another; the ASA says about 150 miles whereas the assignment said 100+ miles; the 1863 atmosphere is unknown, so any shadow-zone rendering is a scenario.
Hypotheses to validate (unverified background):
 speed of sound about 343 m/s at 20 C (about 3 s per km; a cannon 1.5 km away is heard about 4.4 s after flash); sound propagation by ray tracing
with refraction, ground impedance and wind and temperature profiles (Wwise Spatial Audio, Steam Audio, or an offline parabolic-equation solver); black-powder sound for muskets, Napoleons and
Parrotts is to be synthesised and physically timed, or drawn from openly licensed archives (C.10); voice culling and aggregate “wall of fire” beds for thousands of simultaneous sources. No Gettysburg-
specific acoustic study, NPS study or live-fire measurement literature was found.

C.7 Cost, team and comparables
Game-Labs founded 2013 shipped Ultimate General: Gettysburg in October 2014 (about 1.5 years) with teams of 3-5, using Unity (
ITC.ua
); the player community debates its map accuracy (
Game-Labs
forum
). 
No Weta shot counts, Total War team size or budget, cloud GPU prices or full-project cost were retrieved.
 The researcher’s unsourced guess is a multi-year, multi-million-dollar
studio effort. This report carries no cost figure; the vertical-slice benchmark in Part D is the route to a real estimate.
C.8 What Claude Code can and cannot do (rewritten)
Evidence for the workflow
Documented (Anthropic).
 The best-practices guide says context fills up fast and performance degrades as it fills, and recommends verification criteria (tests, builds, screenshots, goal conditions,
stop hooks), an explore-plan-implement-commit flow, a short and pruned CLAUDE.md, skills for on-demand knowledge, hooks for must-always actions, subagents with separate context, and scale-out
through 
claude -p
, worktrees and batch fan-out. It names failure patterns (kitchen-sink session, repeated correction, over-long CLAUDE.md, trust-then-verify gap) and notes that a reviewer asked for
gaps will usually find some, so reviewers should be restricted to correctness and requirement-affecting findings (
best practices
). The long-running harness guidance uses an initializer session, 
init.sh
,
a progress file, an initial git commit and a JSON feature list where agents may only flip 
passes
, one feature per session, with browser automation for end-to-end tests (
harnesses
). Claude Code can run
unattended work through scheduled tasks, GitHub Actions and 
claude -p
; scheduled tasks cannot ask clarifying questions, so prompts must define success (
common workflows
). Interrupted headless
runs can be resumed with 
--continue

--resume

).

Publishing decision and final sign-off
A scope or release decision belongs to the owner under the Unbreakable
Law
Owner requirements
An agent should not create accounts or click through licences on the owner’s behalf without explicit instruction (the notes’ policy stance). Everything else (downloading CMU, 100STYLE, Poly Haven,
Smithsonian CC0 and USGS lidar; running Blender or Unreal once installed; synthesizing audio; writing animation, schemas, queues and review scripts) needs no owner action.
Conflicts and caveats in the licensing facts
SMPL conflict.
 4D-Humans, WHAM and Motion Diffusion Model are MIT-licensed code but need the separately registered SMPL model, whose licence bars commercial use and commercial
neural-net training. The code licence does not cure the model licence, so their outputs are tagged non-commercial-tainted.
Freesound.
 CC0, CC BY (credit required) and CC BY-NC (no earning money) coexist in one library; remix rules limit relicensing; large AI-training downloads are directed to a Data Packs Portal.
Each clip’s licence must be stored and filtered at export.
Megascans.
 The only source is an October 2024 article saying free through end-2024, then paid from USD 0.99 per asset; current 2026 status is unknown.
Unreal.
 Free under USD 1M annual gross revenue; royalty above USD 1M lifetime gross for products with engine runtime; seats at USD 1,850 per year above that. The fetched page did not say
how linear film output is treated, so the owner’s intended release route must be checked against it.
Poly Haven
 permits use through its documented API but its terms prohibit scraping or data mining without permission; the plan uses the API only.
Library of Congress
 “Free to Use” sets cover images, posters, maps and movies, with nothing stated for audio.
BBC sound effects
 terms were not verified and are treated as not usable.
What it cannot do, or cannot claim
It cannot guarantee historical truth (hallucination risk, so every claim needs a retrieved source span), cannot judge photorealism or period feel except through machine tests and photo-match
comparisons, cannot create accounts or accept licences, and has no independent evidence of completing a project of this scale. It must not state that LLM review equals a historian panel: no retrieved
source supports that. Outputs are marked “reviewed by automated subagents; not reviewed by a historian”. Large binaries need Git LFS or Perforce because checkpoints track only changes made
through Claude’s file tools.
Efficiency practices (documented, from the Anthropic pages)
Costs scale with context size; use 
/clear
 between unrelated tasks, 
/compact
 with focus instructions, and keep CLAUDE.md under about 200 lines. Skills load on demand; CLAUDE.md loads every

 returns the run cost;

 gives validated output;

 cap spend, with possible

C.9 Feasibility verdict by requirement
Verdict key: 
enormous but possible
 = achievable by Claude Code autonomously, large; 
possible with labeled reconstruction
 = achievable to the record’s limit, with the remainder generated
procedurally and marked; 
literally impossible
 = the record or physics lacks the data, so the strongest labeled reconstruction replaces it and the gap is registered.
The 14 demands
#
Demand
Verdict
Reasons
1
Remove owner-supplied inputs; Claude Code does all,
using the GPU
Possible with labeled reconstruction
Physical capture is replaced by public datasets,
procedural animation and synthesis (C.10). Substitutes
are modern reconstructions and are labeled. Account
logins, licence acceptance and billing remain legal acts
of the owner
2
Usage efficiency with rapid accurate progress
Enormous but possible
Documented practices (small contexts, subagents,
