recorded in the asset manifest with its licence, source and method, marked “modern reconstruction” or “substitute” in the Evidence-Gap Register, and shown with that mark in the product’s overlay.

Rules that apply to every substitute
1. 
Each asset and each claim carries a record of source, licence, method and tier; a script rejects a build containing any asset without a licence entry or with a non-commercial tag in a release
export.
2. 
A substitute is never reported as documented. It is “inferred”, “plausible-procedural” or “modern reconstruction”.
3. 
The in-product overlay lets a viewer switch between documented, inferred and substitute content.
4. 
Where a substitute cannot reach the record’s limit (for example, the individual face of a company officer), the gap is registered and escalated, and the owner decides whether to reduce scope.
Claude Code never decides that for the owner.

PART D. The A-to-Z plan for pursuing the project with Claude Code (version 2)
How to read this part. Part D is the plan. Part E holds the operating instructions that Claude Code follows (the Unbreakable Law and Sections A to Z). Part C.8 to C.10 holds the feasibility verdicts and
the autonomous substitutes. Evidence vocabulary follows Part B.0: 
documented
 (a specific source states it), 
inferred
 (reasoned from documented facts), 
plausible-procedural
 (generated to fill a
gap, seeded and reproducible). Statements about sources and tools carry the labels used in the research notes: 
sourced
 (a page was fetched), 
inferred
 (the note-writer’s reasoning), 
gap
 (not found or
not verifiable). Nothing here is described as built. This is research and planning only.
Two ground rules govern everything below.
1. 
The owner supplies nothing but one-time legal acts.
 Version 1 listed human roles and inputs (reenactors, survey crews, recordists, historians, GPU provisioning). The owner rejected that
list. In version 2 Claude Code does the work itself, with the owner’s GPU used as fully as safe operation allows. Where physical capture is impossible for software, an autonomous equivalent
replaces it and is labeled as such in the product. The only owner actions are in D.8.
2. 
No exceptions, no fabrication.
 The record is incomplete and the owner accepts that. The plan therefore commits to extract everything the reachable library supports, to place every remaining
gap in the Evidence-Gap Register with a confidence tier, and to mark it in the product. Claude Code never claims knowledge the record lacks and never drops a requirement. A belief that an
exception exists is a problem to solve or escalate (Part E, The Unbreakable Law).
D.1 The 21 binding requirements
The owner’s seven original requirements and the fourteen version-2 demands are all non-negotiable. They are restated here in intent, not verbatim copy. Identifiers O1 to O7 and V1 to V14 are used
throughout Parts D and E.
The original seven
ID
Requirement in intent
O1
A Total War-style, Hollywood-grade CGI animation of the entire Battle of Gettysburg, start to finish, that anyone
can fly around freely and watch any spot at any moment.
O2
A 1:1 3D recreation of the 1863 battlefield: terrain, rocks, hills, fences, earthworks, gun emplacements, trees,
buildings, roads.
O3
Historical accuracy, as close to 100% as the record allows, in visuals, sounds, combat mechanics and environment,
at hyper-realistic quality.
O4
Individual units in hyper-realistic combat; every unit’s uniform and equipment correct; commanders from
lieutenant to general with correct appearance.
O5
Preparatory and supporting events animated: Buford scouting, soldiers building earthworks, casualties, casualties
carried to field hospitals.
O6
Empty woods where nothing is happening, with accurate gunfire and artillery audible from anywhere at the
correct distance.
O7
The entire battle, start to finish.
The fourteen version-2 demands
ID
Demand in intent
V1
Remove every owner-supplied-input dependency. Claude Code does everything itself and uses the owner’s GPU as
hard as needed.
V2
Bake in extreme efficiency with the owner’s usage while making rapid but accurate progress.
V3
Research what each unit wore, the different soldier types inside each unit (sharpshooters included), their
uniforms, equipment and weapons, and name the specific rifle when a unit carried one (Springfield variants,
Sharps, and so on).
V4
Greatly expand the master timeline, which is the backbone for scripting.
V5
Identify every reputable terrain source for a July 1863 landscape, modern and from the 1860s.
V6
Add far more detail on every 1863 terrain element: buildings, fences, earthworks, defenses, gun positions, field
hospitals and so on.
V7
Show fence damage at the right time if a fence existed at the start and was damaged during the battle. Do not pre-
place earthworks; show them being constructed.
V8
Photorealistic weather and environmentals.
V9
Include cavalry.
V10
Every soldier historically accurate in dress and equipment (not a photo replica of a real person). Every unit shows
what it was actually doing at each time, including marching to fighting positions. Day-2 arrivals must not appear
before they arrived.
V11
A 24/7 day and night animation. Night is clearly night with a dimmed sky, but everything and all activity stays
visible, only dimmer. Show what soldiers did at night (fires, digging).
V12
Users can navigate back and forth to different points in the battle.
V13
Research how trees and plant growth looked during the battle, as accurate as all known knowledge allows.
V14
Read the full free online library of primary sources, witness accounts and biographies, audit it for accuracy, and
take historical accuracy as far as that library allows.
V15 | (Added after the version 2 review.) The film ends its July 4 section at Gettysburg, then swirls to the Gettysburg cemetery on November 19, 1863, and shows Lincoln taking the stage and giving
the Gettysburg Address from the front row. Events away from Gettysburg are not shown. |
The owner also states that the PDF must not be delivered until every item is honored, and accepts that 100% of the battle is not known and that no one can know every soldier’s action at every
moment. The plan’s response is the same everywhere: extract what the library supports, register the rest with a tier, never claim more.

Per-source propagation is physics; acoustic

dates).
Audio engine derived from the same cache:
per-source delay, attenuation, absorption,
terrain occlusion, voice culling, aggregate
beds. Silent-woods rule: no ambient fire
where the cache has no source.
Audio-delay unit test (delay equals distance
over c at the day’s temperature); loudness
falls monotonically with distance; woods
with no active sources emit only natural
ambience.
1863 atmosphere is unknown, so shadow
zones are scenarios. All gunfire sound is
synthesized or built from open-licence
samples; none is a recording of 1863.
O7
Sources span June 26 (Early at Gettysburg)

26 to July 14 at plan level, with the battle
days at the finest grain). The owner is told
the window is a scope decision only the
owner can narrow (D.8).
Coverage matrix: every timeline row either
has a visual realization or a register entry.
The notes did not reach minute-level
everywhere. Fine grain exists for some
events; coarse grain elsewhere is labeled.

Connecticut; McPherson barn did not burn
July 1; the Angle wall was pre-existing.
Fences slowed the Charge.
Damage and construction are events in the
history layer; the visual state is a function of
time. Earthworks are never pre-placed: each
is a construction event with stages.
Scrub test: render a fence before and after
its damage event; render an earthwork at t
before start (absent) and at stages between
start and finish.
Exact dates and damage time for many
fence segments are not recorded; windows
are stated and defaults are inferred.

Jacobs’ readings, Nofi, HistoryNet and AMS
summaries (secondary; original table not
read). Humidity never measured at
Gettysburg.
Weather presets keyed to hourly timeline;
sun and moon from computed ephemeris;
storm on July 3 near 6 pm; July 4 rain 1.39
in (B.10).
Weather timeline matches the B.10 table;
sun azimuth/altitude within tolerance of the
computed table at test times.
Humidity, wind speed (conflict 2 versus 12
mph) and pressure are unmeasured or
conflicting; those are inferred and labeled.

Cavalry units appear in the timeline checks;
gait cycle foot-contact test.
Horse mocap is replaced by procedural gait;
no open horse mocap with a commercial

A unit exists in the world only inside its

Hard test: zero agent instances for any unit
before its documented arrival; trajectory
continuity check from arrival point to
fighting position.
Marching routes and route timings are

inferred.

Computed ephemeris (LMT), moon phase

thin (gap).
24/7 lighting driven by the ephemeris table,
exposure and moon-boost rules so night is
dimmer but everything stays visible; night
activities entered as scheduled state (fires,
digging, picket, hospital work, burning
buildings).
Night frame luminance test: the luminance
histogram stays inside the “readable” band
at all night hours; every night activity cites a
source or a plausible-procedural tag.
Night practice is mostly inferred. The
“everything visible” requirement is met by
an exposure choice, not by physics (a real
full moon is far darker), and the product
says so.
V12
Event-sourced caches with snapshots
support random access (inferred design,
D.3).
Time scrubber jumps to any simulated
minute; chapter markers; speed control;
bookmarks.
Jump test: from any time t1 to t2, the world
state equals the state reached by playing
forward; jump latency within budget.
The cache must be built before navigation
works at full detail; first-time jump after a
rebuild may need the snapshot load time.

D.3 Architecture (reasoned recommendation)
The architecture has three layers that communicate only through versioned files. This is a recommendation; its parts are inferred from the sourced pattern (Total War-style separation of logic and
display, Massive-style directed simulation, VAT crowds, event-sourced caches).
D.3.1 The history layer (source of truth)
Independent of any renderer. It contains:
1. 
Battle Knowledge Base.
 Every claim from the pipeline in D.4: subject, predicate, place, time window, unit, quantity, source id, page, short quote offset, source tier, confidence tier (documented,
inferred, plausible-procedural), and links to conflicting claims.
2. 
Event graph.
 Nodes are events (arrival, march, deployment, fire opening, assault, withdrawal, order given, fence damaged, building burned, earthwork stage). Edges are causal or ordering links.
Every node has a 
time window
 (earliest, latest, default) and 
alternatives
 when sources conflict. The default view selects one alternative; the viewer can switch.
3. 
Unit manifests.
 For each regiment, battery and cavalry unit: strength (with source range), commander, brigade/division/corps, presence window (arrival and departure), and per-soldier-type
counts (line, skirmisher, sharpshooter, color guard, musician, officer, teamster, medical).
4. 
Kit manifests.
 For each unit and soldier type: headgear, coat, trousers, shoes, equipment, haversack/knapsack, canteen, cartridge box, weapon variant, bayonet, sidearm, flags. Every line has a
claim id or a gap id.
5. 
State schedule.
 Time-keyed state for every structure, fence, field, earthwork, road, hospital and vegetation unit: intact, damaged, burned, trampled, built, in construction, occupied as hospital.
Evaluated as a function of time.
6. 
Provenance tiers
 on every row (documented, inferred, plausible-procedural) and the Evidence-Gap Register.
D.3.2 The simulation layer
Deterministic, seeded and time-addressable. It expands regiment-level history into individual-agent detail.
1. 
Determinism.
 Every random choice uses a seeded generator addressed by (run seed, agent id, time bucket), so any agent’s state at any time is computable and repeatable (inferred design; a
counter-based generator avoids replaying from the start). Floating-point nondeterminism across machines argues for fixed-point or tightly controlled code (sourced reasoning in the production
notes, inferred).
2. 
Trajectories.
 Each unit follows its event-graph path with formation geometry from the drill manuals; individual agents get offsets within formation from the seed. Marching, halting, deploying,
resting, cooking, digging, burying and hospital work are agent states, not special cases.
3. 
Loss-constraint solver.
 Takes the documented regimental losses (Fox as baseline, OR returns of casualties as cross-check, Busey and Martin cited only) as constraints. It assigns individual
casualty events across the unit’s documented engagement windows so that totals match the documented killed/wounded/missing counts within their stated source range, with intensity weighted
by documented fighting windows. It is deterministic, so the same seed yields the same casualties. Outputs are tier plausible-procedural at individual level and documented at regimental total.
4. 
Scripted known fates.
 Named individuals with documented fates (commanders killed or wounded, color-bearers, and so on, as recorded in B.4 and the ledger) are pinned: the solver treats them
as fixed events and fits the remainder around them.
5. 
Wounded flow.
 Casualty events spawn the evacuation chain (stretcher or walking wounded, ambulance, regimental and divisional hospital, Letterman system) with times from the medical notes
where documented.
6. 
Cache.
 Event-sourced log with periodic full snapshots and deltas. The naive size is about 1.3 TB (160,000 agents, 1 sample per second, 32 bytes, 3 days); waypoint and event storage with delta
compression is estimated at tens of GB (order of magnitude, the production note’s own arithmetic, inferred).
D.3.3 The presentation layer
1. 
Real-time free-fly viewer
 with LOD tiers: skinned near, VAT middle, impostor far (inferred pattern from the sourced plugin and UEBS2 evidence). Free camera anywhere.
2. 
Offline film-grade re-render.
 A saved camera path (position, orientation, lens, time range) is re-rendered at film quality from the same cache, so the viewer path and the film path never
disagree about history. Each path is reproducible from its input hash.
3. 
Time scrubber.
 Jump to any moment, forward or back, day or night. Chapter markers for key events, a mini-timeline showing unit presence, and bookmarks.
4. 
24/7 lighting.
 Sun and moon rotations from the precomputed ephemeris table (per minute, July 1 to 5, 1863, local mean time), Sky Atmosphere with a second atmosphere light for the moon,
volumetric clouds, exponential height fog, exposure rules for dim-but-visible nights (B.10). Hourly weather presets cross-fade over 10 to 30 simulated minutes.
5. 
Weather presets
 for each documented period: overcast July 1; foggy July 2 dawn clearing by afternoon; hot humid July 3 with a storm near 6 pm; rainy July 4 with flooded low ground.
6. 
Provenance overlay
: any element can be colored or labeled by its tier; an info panel links to the claim ledger.
7. 
Audio
 from the same cache: per-source delay, attenuation, absorption, occlusion, voice culling and aggregate beds, with scenario toggles for atmosphere.
D.3.4 Why this satisfies scrubbing back and forth (V12)
Scrubbing needs random access to any time. A live emergent simulation cannot provide that. The architecture does: simulation is run offline and stored as a snapshot-plus-delta event log. To jump to
time t, the viewer loads the nearest earlier snapshot and applies the deltas up to t, then evaluates the state schedule (fences, buildings, earthworks, weather, light) at t, which is a pure function.
Because everything is seeded and deterministic, rewinding gives the same world as playing forward. Day-2 arrivals do not appear before their arrival because presence windows gate the existence of
agents in the state, not just their visibility (V10).
The same design supports night. Lighting is a function of time; the moon and sun tables cover every minute; the state schedule carries night activities.
D.4 THE SOURCE AUDIT PIPELINE (demand V14)
The owner requires that Claude Code reads the full free library, audits it, and takes accuracy as far as the library allows. This section is the method. All counts and token figures are estimates from the
research notes unless stated otherwise. Items are labeled (sourced), (inferred) or (gap).

D.4.2 Print-only and unreachable sources
These are not ingested. They are listed as acquisition targets in the Source Manifest and cited so that a human or a later acquisition can settle them. Their content enters the claim ledger only when it
is reachable in a free, lawful form.

are checked first.
D.4.3 Pipeline stages
1. 
Source Manifest.
 One row per source: id, title, edition, URL pattern, witness type (text layer or image layer), rights status, access method, reachability status (read, partial, blocked, print-only),
expected size (labeled estimate) and tier. The manifest is a file under version control and is the stop-condition reference.
2. 
Acquire.
 Polite throttled download. Internet Archive returned a 429 rate-limit after about six rapid metadata fetches (sourced), so the downloader sleeps between requests and retries with
backoff. All raw bytes are stored once, content-addressed by hash.
3. 
Normalize and clean OCR.
 Dehyphenate, remove running heads, repair long-s and common confusions (rn/m, 1/l), reassemble table columns, keep page images for tabular casualty returns.
Low-confidence words flagged from the ABBYY confidence data where available. For the OR, diff eHistory against the HathiTrust witness to find transcription errors; eHistory has visible errors
(sourced, a garbled Couch entry).
4. 
Segment.
 Split on report headers such as “No. 1.—Report of …” and carry the key 
serial/page
. The page-to-report map is built by the pipeline because no source supplies it. Chunk by report or
section, not by fixed token window, carrying metadata (source, tier, author, date written, side, page).
5. 
Map: extract to structured JSON.
 Each chunk goes to a small model that returns schema-validated JSON: author, rank, unit, date written, location, events (time, place, unit, action), numbers
(strength, casualties), people named, hedges (“about”, “I think”), uniform and equipment mentions, weather and terrain mentions, and a quote 
span
 (character offsets, not copied text). Each
claim carries the page citation (
serial/page
 or printed page). Schema validation is enforced with the CLI’s JSON-schema output option (sourced).
6. 
Resolve entities.
 An alias table maps unit and person names (“20th Maine”, “Twentieth Maine Vols.”). Unresolved names go to a queue for a mid-tier model, then to the adjudicator tier.
7. 
Reduce: claim ledger.
 Merge by unit and time window. The ledger rule is: a claim reaches tier 
documented
 when at least two independent sources support it, or one Tier 1 source. Single-
source claims are kept and marked single-source. Derivative sources (a later account that copies an earlier one) are linked and do not count as independent (inferred rule).
8. 
Reliability tiers
 (from the research notes, inferred weighting): Tier 1 contemporaneous orders, dispatches and returns; Tier 2 official reports written within weeks, noting many were written
later, some in 1864 or after; Tier 3 letters and memoirs written years later (Bachelder correspondence, SHSP, Century articles, state monument volumes written 1889 to 1914); Tier 4 later
compilations and popular accounts. The date written, not the date published, is recorded. Wartime letters and reports of later memoirists rank above their memoirs. Lost Cause material in SHSP
and postwar memoirs is tagged for known distortion risks (inferred, general knowledge not sourced in the notes).
9. 
Conflict detection.
 A script groups claims by event and flags differences in time, place, number and sequence. Each flag becomes a conflict record with all alternatives and sources. The default
timeline selects an alternative by tier and independence; the others stay visible. Known conflicts already in the version-2 timeline are used as test cases (Rodes and Ewell timing, Stuart’s arrival,
the July 4 rain timing, Pickett’s Charge start).
10. 
Adversarial review.
 Fresh-context reviewer subagents see only the claim, the cited span and the criteria. Their task is to refute each high-impact claim and to confirm that the quoted span exists
on the cited page. Reviewers are told to report only correctness and requirement-affecting findings, because a reviewer asked for gaps will otherwise invent some (sourced, Anthropic best-
practices). The writer and reviewer never share a session.
11. 
Citation checker.
 A script verifies that the cited page and span offsets exist in the stored extract and that numbers in the claim appear in the span.
12. 
Regression checks.
 A fixed test set includes the known corrections (5th NY Duryee’s not at Gettysburg; 140th NY in regulation blue; 146th NY and 114th PA in Zouave dress; McPherson barn
not burned July 1; Bliss barn and house burned the morning of July 3 by the 14th Connecticut; the Angle wall pre-existing; Spencer carbines not on Buford’s line July 1; the Heth shoe story first
appearing in 1877; Cope’s survey Aug-Oct 1863; the Devil’s Den sharpshooter photograph staged on July 6; Louisiana Tigers as ordinary ANV infantry). After any ledger change the whole set
must still pass.
13. 
Gap registration.
 Every question the library cannot settle becomes an Evidence-Gap Register entry with a confidence tier and the source that would settle it.
14. 
Stop condition.
 The pipeline stops when (a) every Source Manifest row is in a terminal status (read, blocked with reason, or print-only), (b) every timeline row and every manifest line has been
Gettysburg Total War animation plan, version 2 | page 126 of 144


=== PAGE 127 ===
queried against the ledger, (c) new-claim yield, measured as new documented claims per thousand pages, stays under a set threshold for a run of consecutive sources in each category, and (d)
the adversarial review pass has closed all high-impact items. Budget caps (D.5) can halt earlier, in which case the report states what was not reached.
D.4.4 Scale and cost of the audit (estimates)

With a small model run in parallel, this is a question of hours of wall clock, not days (inferred). The hard part is quality control, not compute.
D.4.5 Honest scope of the audit
1. 
The pipeline reads everything in the Source Manifest that is free, lawful and reachable. That is a large corpus but not the whole literature.
2. 
The audit cannot make the record complete. Hours and positions in the OR and in memoirs conflict, and there was no common timekeeping in 1863 (inferred). The audit lists conflicts; it does not
remove them.
3. 
LLM-based review reduces error and finds contradictions, but no source in the notes establishes it as a valid replacement for historians. The product is marked “not reviewed by historians” unless
the owner decides otherwise (D.8).
4. 
The research that produced these notes was itself limited by a lapsed web-search quota and by blocked fetches. The pipeline will reach more than the notes did, but the notes’ unreachable items
are carried as gaps, not as evidence of absence.
D.5 THE EFFICIENCY PLAN (demand V2)
Goal: rapid but accurate progress with minimum waste of the owner’s usage and GPU. Each item is tagged 
sourced
 (Anthropic documentation or the Anthropic engineering post, as fetched in the
notes) or 
inferred
 (the note-writer’s or this plan’s design from those facts).

receives a share. The driver script tracks spend from the

D.5.2 Model routing by task (a recommendation, inferred from documentation guidance)

Model names and availability depend on the owner’s plan. Model and effort are chosen at session start and not alternated mid-task, because switching invalidates the prompt cache (sourced).
D.5.3 Context discipline (sourced unless marked)
1. 
Context size drives cost; clear between unrelated tasks (
/clear
); after two failed corrections clear and restart with a better prompt.
2. 
Use 
/compact
 with focus instructions or a “Compact instructions” section; compact at natural breaks, because compaction invalidates the cache.
3. 
Keep CLAUDE.md short (the docs suggest under about 200 lines); it loads every session. Put domain knowledge in skills, which load on demand (a skill’s description is always in context, its body
loads on invocation, bundled scripts execute without being loaded).
4. 
Use hooks to preprocess verbose output so Claude sees a short result (for example, grep errors from a long log).
5. 
Keep project knowledge in files and the database, not in chat: progress file, claim ledger, feature list. Retrieve just in time with lightweight references (paths, queries).
6. 
Sub-agents return condensed summaries (often 1,000 to 2,000 tokens, per the engineering post).
D.5.4 Subagent use and its cost (sourced)
Delegating verbose work to subagents keeps the main context small, but their own requests still count toward usage. Agent teams use roughly seven times more tokens than a standard plan-mode
session, so they are avoided for bulk work. Subagents have a 5-minute prompt-cache TTL by default. Worktree isolation (
isolation: worktree
) allows parallel sessions without collisions.
D.5.5 Prompt-cache facts (sourced)
1. 
The cache expires after inactivity. The one-hour TTL applies on subscription within plan usage; the TTL is five minutes with usage credits, an API key or cloud providers unless 
promptCacheTtl
 is
set to one hour.
2. 
Switching models, toggling MCP servers (without tool search), compaction and many images invalidate the cache.
3. 
The cache is effectively scoped to machine and directory. Parallel sessions in one directory share it.
4. 
Inference: batch related work in short bursts, set the TTL deliberately, and do not alternate models within a task.
Gettysburg Total War animation plan, version 2 | page 127 of 144


=== PAGE 128 ===
D.5.6 Headless, capped, structured runs (sourced)
1. 
Each work item runs as 
claude -p
 with 
--output-format json
 (returns 
total_cost_usd
) and 
--json-schema
 for validated structured output.
2. 
--max-turns
 limits agentic turns. 
--max-budget-usd
 stops a print-mode run at an estimated spend; subagent spend counts and the cap can overshoot, so per-item caps are set below the phase
share.
3. 
--bare
 skips auto-discovery for faster, reproducible scripted runs; it requires 
ANTHROPIC_API_KEY
 because it does not use the subscription login (D.8). 
--no-session-persistence
 exists for stateless
runs. 
--allowedTools
 and a permission mode fit unattended runs.
4. 
A phase wrapper (inferred design) sums 
total_cost_usd
, compares to the phase cap, and stops.
D.5.7 
/batch
 fan-out with a pilot (sourced)
Bulk work (for example one extraction job per report or one manifest per regiment) can run through 
/batch
 (splits a change across 5 to 30 subagents in worktrees) or a loop of 
claude -p
 over a file list.
Always test on two or three items first, review, then run all. Concurrency is capped to avoid rate limits (inferred).
D.5.8 The long-running harness pattern (sourced from Anthropic’s engineering post)
1. 
An 
initializer
 session creates 
init.sh
, a progress file and the first git commit.
2. 
A 
JSON feature list
 holds every feature with a 
passes
 flag starting false. Agents may change only 
passes
, because models corrupt JSON less than Markdown.
3. 
One feature per session.
 Each session starts by reading the git log, the progress file and the feature list, runs a baseline check, works one feature, tests it end to end, commits and logs.
4. 
End-to-end checks (screenshots, headless renders) are preferred over unit tests alone; the post found bugs unit tests missed.
5. 
In this project the feature list is the 21 requirements decomposed into testable features (for example “unit X is absent before its arrival”, “fence segment F shows damage after event E”). The list
is the control surface for drift (D.9).
D.5.9 Caching and determinism so reruns skip finished work (inferred)
1. 
Extract once.
 Read each source once with the small model and write schema-validated records. Later tasks query records and never re-read source pages.
2. 
Content-addressed artifacts.
 Key = hash(inputs, tool version, parameters, seed). If the key exists, skip. This applies to extraction chunks, terrain tiles, textures, LODs, sim chunks and render
frames.
3. 
Deterministic seeds
 for crowd placement, casualty assignment and procedural vegetation, so reruns reproduce identical output and only changed intervals re-simulate.
4. 
Bake once.
 LOD generation, navigation data, cloth and crowd caches, light bakes and terrain tiles are produced per asset version and reused. Simulation state is checkpointed so a change re-
simulates only the affected interval.
D.5.10 Resumable job runner and queue (inferred)
1. 
A small job runner (Python) keeps a manifest of completed chunks (input hash plus output path), a priority list, retry counts and a watchdog. Claude Code does not need to be running during a
render; it starts the queue and checks results later.
2. 
Render and bake jobs are per shot or per frame chunk. Blender’s command-line options include frame ranges and placeholder and overwrite options for resuming; Unreal’s Movie Render Queue
lists command-line rendering and a “resume or skip existing renders” topic (both from page outlines only, so exact syntax is unverified: gap). The project’s own manifest provides resume
regardless of renderer support.
3. 
Queue priority by milestone
: items for the current vertical slice first; low-resolution previews before finals; final quality only for shots that pass review.
4. 
Thermal and power
: see D.6.
D.5.11 Vertical-slice order (inferred, standard practice)
Run the whole pipeline end to end on a small bounded slice before scaling, with the review gate included, so schemas, caches and tests are proven on little data. The order is Day 1 McPherson Ridge
first, then Little Round Top, then the charge, then the full battle (D.7 phase 14).
D.5.12 Stop conditions and spend caps per phase (inferred)
1. 
Phase token/dollar cap from D.5.1; item cap from 
--max-budget-usd
.
2. 
Maximum failed retries per job.
3. 
“No new passing features in N sessions” halts the loop and writes a report for the owner.
4. 
A phase that exits the budget with failing exit tests is not marked complete; it is escalated (Part E).
5. 
A spend rate check: if one phase consumes more than its share before reaching 50% of its features, it pauses for a plan review.

D.6 THE GPU PLAN (the owner granted full use)
The owner’s GPU is a resource Claude Code uses as fully as is safe. The hardware is unknown to the notes (gap): no source gives render times, power or thermal limits for the owner’s machine. The
plan therefore starts with a measurement.
D.6.1 Measure first (Phase 0)
Claude Code probes the GPU model, VRAM, driver, CPU, RAM, disk and cooling headroom with command-line tools, records them in the hardware profile, and runs a short benchmark (an empty scene,
then a crowd scene at rising agent counts, then a single film-grade frame). Every later estimate of hours is taken from this profile and from the vertical-slice measurements. No GPU-hour figure is
promised before then.
D.6.2 Maximizing utilization
1. 
Keep the GPU busy.
 GPU jobs are serial per GPU (inferred), so the queue is never empty during the owner’s allowed windows: while a bake runs, CPU-bound extraction, review and code run in
parallel, with a concurrency cap to avoid rate limits.
2. 
Overnight scheduling.
 Long jobs (LOD and terrain bakes, light bakes, offline renders, video-pose extraction) are scheduled for night windows. Claude Code can run scheduled and unattended
work (routines, desktop scheduled tasks, a loop; sourced), but scheduled prompts cannot ask clarifying questions, so each prompt defines its success test.
3. 
Batch sizes.
 Choose the largest chunk that fits VRAM without paging, determined by the benchmark: large tiles for terrain, large frame chunks for renders. Smaller chunks are used when
thermals or the resume interval demand it.
Gettysburg Total War animation plan, version 2 | page 128 of 144


=== PAGE 129 ===
4. 
Resumable chunks.
 Every GPU job is split into chunks sized to finish in a short fixed interval (for example 5 to 20 minutes, a tuning parameter). Each chunk writes its output and a manifest
entry. After a crash, power loss or pause, the runner restarts at the first missing chunk (D.5.10).
5. 
Preview before final.
 Low-resolution previews run first; finals only after the review gate.
6. 
Order by value.
 GPU work for the current vertical slice outranks everything else.
D.6.3 Monitoring and thermal protection (inferred; specific commands unsourced)
1. 
A watchdog polls GPU temperature, power draw, clock throttling flags and memory with 
nvidia-smi
 (or the equivalent for the vendor).
2. 
Thresholds are set conservatively from the vendor’s published limits, read from the card’s own reported maximum, not guessed. Above the soft threshold the runner reduces batch size or inserts
cool-down pauses; above the hard threshold it checkpoints, pauses and resumes when temperature is back under the resume threshold.
3. 
A power limit (for example with 
nvidia-smi -pl
) can be set at the start so sustained runs stay in a comfortable envelope. The value is chosen from the benchmark. Setting a power limit may need
administrator rights on the owner’s machine; if so, the plan runs without it and relies on batch-size throttling (flagged; the note-writer marked this as background knowledge).
4. 
Disk space is monitored so caches do not fill the drive; the runner stops and reports before that point.
5. 
Fail-safe: any unexplained driver error, repeated chunk failure, or watchdog silence ends the queue safely and writes a status line the next session reads first.
D.6.4 Licence-aware GPU use
1. 
The reference 3D Gaussian Splatting code needs a CUDA GPU with 24 GB VRAM for paper-quality training and is licensed for non-commercial research and evaluation only (sourced). It is used
for prototypes at most; shipped assets use other routes.
2. 
SMPL-parameterized video pose extraction (4D-Humans, WHAM) uses the GPU but inherits SMPL’s non-commercial terms; outputs are tagged non-commercial-tainted and kept out of the release
unless licensed (D.7 phase 9, D.8).
3. 
Unreal Engine: free for individuals and small businesses under USD 1 million annual gross revenue; the fetched licence page did not address film or linear media (sourced, gap).
D.7 Phases (A-to-Z at plan level)
Each row: what Claude Code does; outputs; exit tests (machine-checkable where possible); budget guard (share of the allocation from D.5.1, plus the stop rule). Part E gives the operating instructions
for each workstream. Every phase ends by updating the JSON feature list and the Evidence-Gap Register.

D.8 The one-time account and licence steps, and the owner decision list
D.8.1 The ONLY owner actions: one-time legal acts
These are acts that software cannot lawfully perform on the owner’s behalf. Nothing else is requested of the owner. The notes’ stance (inferred) is that an agent must not create accounts or click
through licences on the owner’s behalf without explicit instruction. Each item is done once.

D.8.2 Decisions only the owner makes (Decision Log)
Claude Code records the question, the options, the effect on each of the 21 requirements, and a recommended default. It does not choose any of these.
1. 
Scope reductions.
 Any narrowing of the 21 requirements. The Unbreakable Law means no reduction is assumed; only the owner can accept one.
2. 
Battle window.
 Whether “start to finish” is June 26 to July 14 (the notes’ span) or narrower.
3. 
Automated review in place of historians.
 Whether the owner accepts automated review (D.4.3) as the review standard, with the product labeled “not reviewed by historians,” or instructs
Claude Code to add a further automated review tier. No source in the notes shows LLM review is equivalent.
4. 
Vertical-slice acceptance.
 Whether each slice is accepted as an interim deliverable.

PART E. Claude Code operating instructions (paste into CLAUDE.md or the project brief)
The text between the two horizontal rules below is addressed to Claude Code. It is a reasoned recommendation by the report author, informed by the cited research; it is not itself a research finding.
Keep CLAUDE.md short in practice (Anthropic’s guidance is to prune it; the research notes cite roughly 200 lines as a working ceiling): paste THE UNBREAKABLE LAW and Sections A-Z in
compressed form, and keep long reference material (schemas, the source list, the claim ledger, the register) in files that CLAUDE.md points to, so the Law is always loaded and never buried.
References to “Part B”, “Part C” and “Part D” point to the sections of this report with those names (Part C.10 is the list of autonomous substitutes; Part D.8 is the one-time owner account and licence
list).
THE UNBREAKABLE LAW
1. All 21 requirements are non-negotiable.
 The 21 requirements in Section A (the original seven, R01-R07, and the fourteen version-2 demands, R08-R21) are binding in full. Nothing is negotiable.
There are zero exceptions. No task is too laborious. All tasks are within the scope of the effort to be expended. You must never drop, water down, defer indefinitely, or silently narrow a requirement, a
sub-requirement, a data item, a unit, a soldier, a fence, a tree, a minute of the timeline, or a quality bar. “Too much work,” “too slow,” “too many units,” “too many soldiers,” “too many fences,” “too
many trees,” “good enough,” and “for now” are never reasons to skip anything. If work is large, you break it down, schedule it, parallelize it, and do it.
2. Claude Code itself does ALL the work, with no owner input.
 Every task in this project is done by Claude Code: research, source acquisition, reading the full free primary-source library, data
modelling, terrain reconstruction, kit design, motion, sound, simulation, rendering, testing, review and packaging. You have the owner’s full permission to use the owner’s GPU, and the rest of the
owner’s machine, as heavily and as long as the work needs, subject only to hardware safety (Section I). Never stop to ask the owner for motion capture, scans, recordings, crews, historians, permits or
compute. Use the autonomous substitutes in Part C.10 and label them honestly in the product. The only owner steps are the one-time account, credential, billing and licence-acceptance steps in Part
D.8 (legal acts that software cannot perform), plus decisions recorded in the Decision Log (Section H). A missing owner-supplied input is never a reason to stop; it is a reason to apply the substitute.
3. Where the historical record or physics makes a literal 100% impossible, you do not claim an exception and you do not fabricate.
 Examples: no record exists of an individual soldier’s
face, or of an individual soldier’s exact position and action at a given minute; no 1863 audio exists; no 1863 photograph shows most of the ground; the exact 1863 atmosphere and wind are unknown; a
single true ordering of chaotic combat does not exist; the exact state of every crop, tree and rail is unrecorded. The owner has accepted that 100% of the battle is not known. In every such case you
must:
a. 
Pursue the strongest evidence-based reconstruction
 available, exhausting the free primary-source library, photographs, maps, museum collections, GIS and lidar before settling for
less.
b. 
Register it
 in the Evidence-Gap Register (Section R) with a confidence tier: 
documented
, 
inferred
, or 
plausible-procedural
.
c. 
Mark it clearly in the product
, so that a viewer can see what is documented, what is inferred, and what is plausible-procedural.
d. 
Escalate it
 to the owner in the Decision Log. You do not decide the scope question; you keep working on everything else.
4. Never fabricate.
 You never present a generated, inferred or remembered item as documented. You never label anything “documented” without a source in the provenance record that supports
exactly that claim. You never invent a quotation, a measurement, a name, a unit detail, a time, a number, a citation or a URL. A failed lookup is recorded as a failed lookup.
5. A belief in an exception is a problem to solve.
 If you believe you have found an exception, treat that belief as a problem to be solved or escalated, never as permission to skip. The standard is
“the strongest possible reconstruction, honestly labeled, with every gap visible and every scope decision made by the owner,” and that standard has no exceptions either.
6. Only the owner decides scope reductions.
 You may propose options. You may not implement a reduction, deprioritize a requirement indefinitely, or reinterpret a requirement until the owner has
recorded a decision in the Decision Log. Absent a recorded decision, the requirement stands in full.
7. Honesty outranks completeness of appearance.
 A visible, marked gap is required where evidence is missing. A concealed gap is a violation of this Law.
8. Efficiency never lowers the bar.
 The efficiency rules in Section C exist to make rapid, accurate progress with the least usage. They never justify skipping a requirement, a check or a source.

A. Assignment and the 22 requirements (binding text)
You are building, over many sessions, the following. The IDs are permanent. 
requirements.json
 (Section F) is the machine-readable copy; never edit its wording; only the owner may.
Original seven
R01.
 A Total War-style, Hollywood-grade CGI animation of the entire Battle of Gettysburg from start to finish that anyone can fly around freely and watch any spot at any moment.
R02.
 A 1:1 3D digital recreation of the 1863 battlefield (terrain, rocks, hills, fences, earthworks, gun emplacements, trees, buildings, roads).
R03.
 100% historically accurate hyper-realistic visuals, sounds, combat mechanics, environment.
R04.
 Individual units in hyper-realistic combat; every unit’s uniform and equipment correct; commanders lieutenant to general with correct appearance.
R05.
 Animation of preparatory events such as Buford scouting, soldiers building earthworks, casualties, casualties carried to field hospitals.
R06.
 Empty woods where nothing is happening, but accurate gunfire and artillery sound audible from anywhere with correct distance.
R07.
 The entire battle from start to finish.
Version-2 demands
R08.
 Remove all owner-supplied-input dependencies. Claude Code does everything itself, using the owner’s GPU as hard as needed. Physical capture is replaced by autonomous equivalents,
clearly labeled; the only owner acts are the one-time account, credential, billing and licence steps in Part D.8.
R09.
 Be incredibly efficient with the owner’s usage while making rapid but totally accurate progress.
R10.
 Research what each individual unit wore, the different types of soldiers in each unit including sharpshooters, and how their uniforms, equipment and weapons looked; if a unit carried a
specific rifle (the Springfield variants, Sharps rifles, and so on), say so.
R11.
 A greatly expanded master timeline, the backbone for scripting.
R12.
 Identify any and all reputable terrain sources for a July 1863 landscape, modern and from the 1860s.
R13.
 Far more detail about every 1863 terrain element: buildings, fences, earthworks, defenses, gun positions, field hospitals and the rest.
R14.
 If a fence existed at the start and was damaged during the battle, the animation shows that damage at the right time. Earthworks are not pre-placed; they are shown being constructed.
R15.
 Photorealistic weather and environmentals.
R16.
 Include cavalry.
R17.
 Every soldier has a historically accurate, realistic appearance for how that soldier would have been dressed and equipped (not a photo replica of a real person). Every unit shows what it was
actually doing at each time, including marching to fighting positions. Units that arrived on day 2 must not appear before they arrived.
R18.
 A 24/7 day and night animation. Night is clearly night with a dimmed sky, but everything and all activity stays visible, only dimmer. Soldiers are shown doing whatever they were doing at
night: sitting around a fire, digging defenses, and so on.
R19.
 Users can navigate back and forth to different points in the battle.
R20.
 Research how trees and plant growth looked during the battle, as accurately as all known knowledge allows.
R21.
 Read the full free online library of primary sources, witness accounts, biographies and more; audit everything for accuracy; take the animation’s historical accuracy as far as that library
allows.
R22 (added by the owner after the version 2 review).
 The default film path ends with the July 4 events at Gettysburg (casualty collection, burial, the truce, the Confederate withdrawal). At
the end of July 4 the animation swirls and jumps to the Gettysburg cemetery on November 19, 1863, and from the point of view of a person in the front row shows an accurate version of Lincoln
taking the stage and giving the Gettysburg Address. Events outside Gettysburg are not shown. Specified in B.12.
The owner accepts that no one can know every soldier’s action at every moment. The plan therefore commits to extract everything the library supports and to record every remaining gap in the
Evidence-Gap Register with a confidence tier. It does not claim knowledge the record lacks and does not drop any requirement.
B. Boundaries and definitions
Owner
: the human who decides scope, budget policy and every escalated item, and who performs only the Part D.8 one-time steps. Only the Decision Log records owner decisions.
Confidence tiers
: 
documented
 (a specific non-lead source states or shows it and the claim matches the source); 
inferred
 (follows from documented facts by stated reasoning, recorded);
plausible-procedural
 (generated to fill a gap, consistent with the documented context, seeded and reproducible). Open gaps are not a tier: they are register entries.
Datum
: any fact used in the product (a position, time, uniform part, count, height, sound property, vegetation state).
Claim ledger
: the table of atomic claims behind data (Section P). Every datum traces to one or more claims.
Product marking
: any visible or audible indication that distinguishes tiers.
Autonomous substitute
: a Claude Code-executable method that replaces a human-supplied input (Part C.10). Its output is always labeled as a modern reconstruction or model, never as 1863
capture.
Boundary of authority
: Claude Code decides how to do the work. The owner decides what the work is (scope), what is shown by default when sources conflict, and legal acts. Carry source
conflicts as conflicts; store all values with sources; mark which one the default view uses and who decided.
No-owner-input rule
: nothing in this file may be read as permission to ask the owner for physical capture, access or labor. If an instruction seems to need it, find the substitute (Part C.10) or
open a register entry and continue other work.
C. Context and session protocol, and the efficiency rules (R08, R09)
Session start.
 Read this file, 
progress.md
, 
requirements.json
, the open register entries relevant to the task, 
budget/phase_budget.json
, and the last 20 lines of 
git log
. Run 
init.sh
 and the baseline
check. Work on ONE feature per session. At the end: update 
progress.md
, flip only the 
passes
 flag you have actually verified, commit. Clear context and restart after two failed corrections on the same
issue. Never rely on memory of earlier sessions; the files are the memory.
Efficiency rules (these implement R09; they are inferences from Anthropic’s published cost and harness guidance cited in the research notes, not measured results for this project).
1. 
Budget caps.
 Every phase has a token and dollar cap in 
budget/phase_budget.json
 (Appendix F). Every scripted job runs with a turn limit and a spend limit (the CLI has 
--max-turns
 and 
--max-
budget-usd
; the latter can overshoot, so keep a margin). A driver script sums reported cost per phase and halts the phase when the cap is hit, writes a report to 
progress.md
 and moves to other
work. A cap hit is logged to the Decision Log only if finishing the phase would exceed it; the cap is raised only by the owner. Caps pace work; they never remove work.
2. 
Headless runs for bulk work.
 Bulk, repetitive tasks (source extraction, claim checking, kit manifests per regiment, per-chunk render jobs) run as scripted headless jobs (
claude -p
, structured
JSON output with a schema, stateless flags, restricted tool lists), one job per work item, tested on 2-3 items before the full run. Interactive sessions are for design, adjudication and integration.
3. 
Model routing.
 Small, cheap models for bulk extraction (OCR clean-up, report splitting, citation parsing, metadata, alias matching). The default coding model for pipeline and engine code and
for standard drafting. The strongest model only for adjudicating conflicting sources, designing schemas and simulation rules, and the final audit of high-impact claims. Fix the model for a session
at its start; do not alternate models mid-task (switching invalidates the prompt cache).
4. 
Caching.
 (a) Prompt cache: batch related work in short bursts, keep the stable prefix (CLAUDE.md, schemas) unchanged, avoid toggling tools or compacting mid-task, set the cache lifetime
deliberately where the billing mode allows it. (b) Work cache: key every expensive artifact (extraction record, terrain tile, kit asset, animation clip, simulation snapshot, render chunk) by a
content hash of inputs plus tool version plus parameters plus seed; skip when the key exists. (c) Extraction once: read each source once, emit schema-validated claim records, and thereafter
query the ledger, never re-read the source.
5. 
One feature per session.
 Each session advances exactly one 
requirements.json
 sub-requirement or one source batch. Clear between unrelated tasks. Never run a “kitchen-sink” session.
6. 
Context hygiene.
 Keep CLAUDE.md short; load long reference material on demand through skills and file pointers; use subagents for noisy investigation and independent review, returning
condensed summaries; use hooks or scripts to trim logs before Claude reads them; use worktrees for parallel workstreams.
7. 
Verification loops instead of supervision.
 Every feature has a runnable check (Section Q). A Stop hook or CI job runs it. Long unattended runs are possible because passing is machine-
decided.
8. 
Vertical slice first.
 Run one short slice through the entire pipeline, including review and render, before scaling; measure it; set the Appendix F caps from it. The slice is a milestone, never a
substitute for the whole.
9. 
Stop conditions.
 Per-phase budget; maximum retries per job; “no new passing sub-requirements in N sessions” halts the loop and writes a stuck-report. Halting is not abandoning: the stuck-
report names the substitute attempted and the next one to try.
10. 
Parallelism limits.
 GPU rendering and simulation are serial per GPU and are scheduled by the job runner (Section I). Parallelize CPU-bound extraction and review with capped concurrency.
Avoid agent-team modes for bulk work (the research notes report about 7x token use for teams).
11. 
Overnight use.
 The owner’s GPU is used heavily, including unattended overnight runs, through the job runner. Claude Code need not be running during a render; it starts jobs, then checks
results.
D. Data: the history layer
