A versioned database is the single source of truth. Tables: 
units
 (army, corps, division, brigade, regiment, battery, troop, company), 
people
, 
places
, 
events
, 
orders
, 
positions
 (unit or person, time
window, area, uncertainty), 
movements
 (route, start and end time, rate, formation), 
activities
 (unit, time window, activity class, source), 
arrivals
 (unit, arrival time window, place, sources), 
departures
, 
equipment_items
, 
kit_manifests
, 
uniform_specs
, 
ordnance
, 
structures
 (buildings, fences, walls, works, with state schedules), 
terrain_features
, 
vegetation_states
, 
weather_states
, 
sources
, 
claims
, 
claim_support
, 
conflicts
, 
register_entries
. Positions are regions and time windows with uncertainty, never false-precision points. Renderers read the history layer and never write to it. Seed content
from the research notes only after verifying each line against the primary source (Section E).
E. Evidence rules
1. 
Source hierarchy.
 Tier 1: contemporaneous orders, dispatches, returns, and photographs of the period. Tier 2: official reports written within weeks, the Official Records (Series I, vol. 27),
contemporary maps (Warren, Bachelder, Cope, Hopkins), museum objects, NPS reports and GIS. Tier 3: letters, memoirs and monument-commission volumes written years later (Bachelder
correspondence, Southern Historical Society Papers, Century articles, state monument volumes, postwar memoirs). Tier 4: later compilations and popular accounts. Scholarly works (Coddington,
Pfanz, Busey and Martin, Gottfried, Warner) are treated as strong secondary sources and as pointers to primary ones. Encyclopedias, forums, blogs, auction listings and wikis are leads only.
Track the date written, not just the date published.
2. 
Never mark anything 
documented
 on the strength of Wikipedia, a forum, an auction listing, a blog or your own memory. Use them to find primary sources.
3. 
Items the research notes label “unverified,” “from memory,” “background,” or forum-sourced are leads, not facts. Verify before use.
4. 
Known corrections you must preserve.
 5th NY (Duryee’s Zouaves) was not at Gettysburg. The 140th NY wore regulation blue at Gettysburg (Zouave dress came in January 1864). The 146th
NY (Zouave style from June 3, 1863) and the 114th PA (Collis Zouaves) wore Zouave dress. The McPherson barn did not burn on July 1 (it survives; the house burned in 1895). The Bliss barn and
house were burned deliberately on the morning of July 3 by the 14th Connecticut. The stone wall at the Angle was a pre-existing farm wall, not a Union construction. Spencer carbines were not
on Buford’s line July 1 (the 5th Michigan and part of the 6th used them July 3). The Heth shoe story first appears in 1877. Cope’s survey dates August to October 1863. The Devil’s Den
sharpshooter photograph was staged (July 6, O’Sullivan and Gardner). Wheat’s Louisiana battalion was gone by 1862, so Hays’ regiments are dressed as ordinary Army of Northern Virginia
infantry. Stuart reached the field on the afternoon or evening of July 2 (sources conflict).
5. 
Known disputes you carry as disputes
 (store every value and source; the default view is an owner decision): Spencer carbines at Buford’s July 1 fight; Napoleon range figures (shot 1,619-
1,680 yd; canister 350 or 400 yd); Whitworth figures (forum, low reliability); cannonade timing (about 1 to 3 hours) and gun counts (Confederate 130-170, Union 80-120); Lee’s arrival (1:30 or
2:30 p.m.); Pender’s assault (3:30 or about 4:00); infantry step-off (about 2:00 or 3:00 p.m.); Camp Letterman opening (July 20 to 22) and closing (November 20, 1863 or January 1864);
Confederate casualty totals (22,709 to 28,063); Lee’s height (5 ft 10 in to about 6 ft 1 in); the Pittsburgh acoustic claim and the Ellis attribution (unverified); Union and Confederate clocks
differing by about 20 minutes (unnamed estimate); Rodes’ and Ewell’s timing; Stuart’s arrival; wind speed on July 1-2 (12 mph versus 2 mph); every other conflict in the Part B.2.5 conflict
register.
6. 
Never invent a quotation, a measurement, a name, a unit number, a time, or a citation. Quote at most a short phrase from any source (under 15 words); paraphrase everything else. Public-domain
texts (pre-1929 works, US government works) may be held in full in a private working store; copyrighted works (Busey and Martin, Coddington, Pfanz, the Ladd edition of the Bachelder papers,
Dean Thomas’ appendix, Jensen, Coco, Gottfried, Warner) are cited and consulted where lawfully reachable but never reproduced or bulk-copied.
7. 
Forum and weak-source quarantine.
 Anything from a forum, a blog, a Wikipedia table, or an unreferenced auction page enters the ledger as 
lead
 and cannot support a datum alone.
F. Features and requirement traceability (R01-R21)
requirements.json
 lists the 21 requirements by ID (R01-R21, Section A), each decomposed into numbered sub-requirements (for example R17.0412: “20th Maine Infantry kit manifest, July 2, 1863”;
R14.007: “Rose Farm fence segment 7 damaged state at its documented time”). Each sub-requirement has fields: 
id
, 
requirement_id
, 
text
, 
source_status
, 
register_ids
, 
gate_ids
 (Section Q), 
passes
(true only when its gate test passes), 
owner_decision_ids
. You may change only 
passes
 and links; you may add sub-requirements; you may never delete or reword one. Every commit message references
the sub-requirement IDs it advances. A coverage report must show that all 21 requirement IDs have sub-requirements, and that 100% of sub-requirements are either passing or linked to an open
register entry awaiting an owner decision with work continuing on the best reconstruction; anything else is unfinished work, not an exception. Expected sub-requirement populations (generate them
from the data, do not hand-type): one per regiment, battery and troop for kit (R10, R17); one per timeline row (R11); one per structure and per state change (R13, R14); one per terrain source (R12);
one per vegetation polygon class (R20); one per library source batch (R21).
G. Geospatial and terrain pipeline (R02, R12, R13, R20)
1. 
Elevation.
 Acquire the best available modern lidar for Adams County through the free routes in the research notes: the USGS 3DEP point clouds in the public AWS EPT bucket (no account
needed with unsigned requests), the South Central Pennsylvania 2017 QL2 collection (0.7 m nominal spacing, as the notes report), and PAMAP 2006-2008 (1.4 m spacing) as the fallback. Prefer
the free EPT bucket; the requester-pays bucket needs the owner’s billing and is a Part D.8 matter only if truly required. Record vintage, accuracy and project name (the exact USGS project folder
name for Adams was not verified in the notes; confirm it).
2. 
1860s maps.
 Ingest in this order: the Library of Congress 1886 tracing of the Warren map (1:2400, 4-ft contours, with roads, buildings, fences, vegetation and fortifications; IIIF), then the NARA
Record Group 77 Warren sheets, then NPS’s georeferenced 1863 layers if obtainable, then Bachelder, Cope, Hopkins 1858 (owners and roads) and the Rumsey Warren 1895 sheet
(noncommercial reuse only; view and georeference, do not redistribute). Georeference with buildings unchanged since 1863 as control points (the NPS method used nine control points then
rubbersheeting; Knowles hand-traced the contours). Treat Warren vegetation as “approximately 1868” and cross-check with photographs, Cope and Bachelder.
3. 
Reconcile modern and historic ground.
 Compare lidar to the Warren TIN; where modern grading is known (railroad cuts, avenues, quarries, monuments, parking, road work on Little Round
Top), correct to Warren and record the correction. The notes report hilltop agreement within about 50 ft and larger creek-bed differences; record residuals per area.
4. 
NPS layers and reports.
 Fetch the NPS GIS layers, Cultural Landscape Reports (including Record of Treatment volumes), historic structure reports and farm histories that are publicly
reachable (npshistory.com and nps.gov); record rights. Where a layer is not public, record the negative result and rebuild it from Warren and the photographs.
5. 
Rocks and fine geometry.
 Lidar spacing is too coarse for Devil’s Den, Little Round Top and Culp’s Hill boulders. Autonomous substitute (Part C.10): procedural boulder fields constrained by
lidar slope and by period photographs, validated by photo-match at documented camera positions; archival-image photogrammetry is poorly conditioned for 1860s views and is used only where
the notes’ conditions allow. Tag procedural rock as plausible-procedural.
6. 
Vegetation.
 Polygons from Warren, Bachelder and NPS woodlot, orchard and vegetation layers; trees scattered by seeded procedure with species and age distributions backed by sources;
chestnut and other post-1863 changes handled as a documented correction layer (the notes treat the chestnut blight claim as unverified background; verify before use). Crops are attributes per
field with documented-or-assumed status and height ranges, never a single invented value (Section L for state schedules).
7. 
Buildings, fences, roads.
 Each building, fence segment, wall and road segment is a 
structures
 row with type, material, dimensions, a state schedule keyed to time (Section F rules R13, R14),
sources and tier. Surviving buildings from lawful scans and photographs; lost buildings from photographs and maps.
8. 
Every terrain tile and object carries 
tier
, 
source_ids
 and an error estimate. Terrain source catalog and audit status are tracked per source (Section X).
H. Human decisions and escalation
Maintain 
decision_log.md
: every question for the owner, the options with costs and consequences, your recommendation, and the owner’s answer with date. Escalate by writing the question to the log
and continuing other work. Escalation triggers: a register entry opened; a source conflict that changes a visible result; any request, from anyone, to cut scope; any change to the Part D.8 account list;
any budget cap that would be exceeded; the default timeline when sources conflict; how uncertain content is displayed; whether historian review is wanted (Section X); the vertical slice acceptance.
You never ask the owner for physical capture, scans, recordings, crews, historians, permits or compute (Law, clause 2). Do not proceed on a guess; proceed on the other work.
I. Infrastructure and the GPU job runner
1. 
Git with Git LFS (or Perforce) for binaries; a single repo layout with 
/history
, 
/terrain
, 
/kit
, 
/anim
, 
/audio
, 
/engine
, 
/vegetation
, 
/weather
, 
/library
, 
/tests
, 
/budget
, 
/docs
, 
/register
;
deterministic builds; 
init.sh
 that sets up the environment; headless benchmark scenes; CI that runs every machine check in Section Q. Use worktrees for parallel workstreams. Commit often
(checkpoints do not track shell-made changes).
2. 
GPU job runner.
 A small Python job runner owns all heavy compute. Jobs are chunked (per shot, per frame range, per tile, per simulation interval), each described by a manifest with an input
hash; a chunk whose output exists and whose hash matches is skipped. A priority queue orders jobs by milestone; low-resolution previews run first and final quality only for chunks that passed
review. Retries are counted; a watchdog kills hung jobs; failures are logged, never deleted. The runner polls GPU temperature and power (for example through 
nvidia-smi
) and pauses or throttles
to protect the hardware; it does not shrink the work. Run the owner’s GPU at maximum utilization whenever work is queued, including overnight. Resume-after-failure is a property of the runner,
not of the renderer.
3. 
Rendering is headless and batch (the notes report command-line, no-interaction render modes in Blender and Unreal Movie Render Queue; exact flags are unverified and must be confirmed on
first use). The engine choice is recorded in a decision memo after the benchmark; engine licence acceptance is a Part D.8 step.
4. 
Anything that needs an account, key, billing or licence click is requested once, through Part D.8, never mid-task.
J. Journal and progress
progress.md
 is a dated log: what was done, what is verified, what failed, what is blocked and why, spend against cap, next steps. Never delete a failure. Never mark work complete that is not verified. A
short 
claims_changed.md
 lists claims added or revised per session, so reviewers need not re-read everything.
Gettysburg Total War animation plan, version 2 | page 134 of 144


=== PAGE 135 ===
K. Kit: every soldier correct (R04, R10, R16, R17)
1. 
Kit manifest per unit.
 For every regiment, battery, troop and company-level unit in the verified OOB (including cavalry regiments and batteries, horse artillery, engineers, signal, provost,
ambulance, ordnance, headquarters and medical staff), build a 
kit_manifests
 record: headgear, coat or jacket type and color range, shirt, trousers, footwear, belts and accoutrements (cartridge
box, cap pouch, bayonet scabbard, haversack, canteen, knapsack or blanket roll), badges (Union corps and division badges only where the unit is verified to have worn them in July 1863; adoption
was patchy), regalia, arms, ammunition load, flags and guidons, colors and dye ranges, with sources and tiers.
2. 
Soldier types within a unit.
 Each manifest lists the soldier types present and their proportions: line infantry, color guard, officers, noncommissioned officers, musicians, sharpshooters or
skirmishers, pioneers, stretcher bearers, ambulance personnel, teamsters, cannoneers, drivers, cavalry troopers, orderlies. Each type has its own dress, equipment and weapon.
3. 
Arms per unit.
 Assign the specific rifle to each unit from the best available source: Springfield 1855/1861/1863 variants, Enfield 1853 and its variants, smoothbore muskets, Sharps rifles and
carbines, Burnside, Gallager, Smith, Merrill, Spencer, Colt revolving rifles, Henry, Whitworth, revolvers, sabers. Dean Thomas’ arms appendix in Coates and Thomas, 
An Introduction to Civil War
Small Arms
 (print only), lists primary armament of every Union regiment at Gettysburg and is the highest-value single source; obtain it lawfully. Regiments with no retrievable arms datum (the
research notes list the 1st Minnesota, 14th Brooklyn, 114th PA, 26th NC, Texas Brigade remainder and Pickett’s brigades among them) get an inferred or plausible-procedural arm with the
evidence stated and a register entry. Do not choose the arm by assumption and label it documented.
4. 
Sharpshooters.
 Berdan’s 1st and 2nd U.S. Sharpshooters (modified blue, Sharps rifles), the Confederate sharpshooter battalions (for example Blackford’s 5th Alabama Battalion; Whitworth rifles
are a forum-weak claim to verify), and individual marksmen (Devil’s Den) are separate unit types with their own manifests. The Devil’s Den photograph is staged and cannot be used as a candid
reference for posture or arms.
5. 
Rifle variants per unit
 are recorded with the evidence class: regimental return, ordnance report, monument-volume statement, period photograph, or inference. Where a unit carried mixed
arms, store the proportion as a distribution and tag plausible-procedural with the documented range.
6. 
Distribution rendering.
 Where dress is only known statistically (jackets versus frock coats, hat types, trouser color spread, Confederate depot jacket types and homespun), render the
distribution procedurally per soldier with a seed, within the documented range, and tag plausible-procedural. Every soldier is individually dressed from the unit’s manifest; no two adjacent
soldiers need be identical, and none may be outside the manifest.
7. 
Commanders, lieutenant to general.
 Create a 
people
 entry for every officer named in the verified OOB and the Official Records, then research appearance (height, build, hair, facial hair,
dress, horse, equipment, insignia) from photographs, CDVs, descriptive rolls, memoirs and contemporaries. The notes document appearance for only a handful (Lee, Meade, Ewell, Pickett,
Hancock, Custer, fragments) and show contradictions (Lee’s height). For anyone without evidence, register the gap, build the strongest reconstruction from regimental dress norms, age and rank
insignia, mark it, and escalate. Portraits of real individuals are reconstructions from evidence; enlisted soldiers are never photo replicas of real people.
8. 
Cavalry kit
 is first-class: Union cavalry and Confederate cavalry get the same manifest depth, plus horse furniture, horse color and type distributions, guidons, and weapons (sabers, carbines,
revolvers). Spencer, Sharps, Burnside, Gallager, Smith, Merrill and Colt arms are assigned per regiment with the July 1 versus July 3 Spencer distinction preserved.
9. 
Anachronism guard.
 Every kit item has a 
valid_from
 and 
valid_to
 date; the build rejects items outside July 1863 for the unit (for example the 140th NY Zouave dress, the 5th NY at Gettysburg,
Confederate Wheat’s Tigers dress) (Gate Q03).
10. 
Rank insignia, shoulder straps, sashes, swords, horse furniture and flags are sourced and tiered.
L. Lighting, weather, vegetation and the 24/7 night (R15, R18, R20)
1. 
Ephemeris.
 Compute sun and moon positions and phases from an ephemeris for the exact latitude, longitude and times for the whole window. Do not trust a website table; verify the moon phase
independently. All sky, shadow and moonlight values derive from the ephemeris (Gate Q11).
2. 
Weather state timeline.
 Build 
weather_states
 from documented records: temperatures and conditions per day, fog at July 2 dawn, afternoon drizzle July 2, thunderstorms July 3, heavy rain from
late July 3 into July 4 (1.39 in on July 4 per the notes’ source), Plum Run overflow. Carry the source conflict on temperatures and wind (12 mph versus about 2 mph; the notes recommend
rendering light 2-6 mph pending resolution). Do not use the unsourced “88F on July 1” or climatological records as 1863 observations. Humidity, dew point, pressure and visibility are not
documented in the notes; model them physically, tag plausible-procedural, expose the parameters.
3. 
Ground and weather effects.
 Rain wets ground, raises mud, floods low ground, dampens powder and affects ricochet and artillery movement as the sources document; the dry July 2-3 ground
versus soggy July 4 ground is a state change tied to the weather timeline (Section V).
4. 
Smoke.
 Black-powder smoke density is not documented; model it physically, expose parameters, tag plausible-procedural. The documented July 3 cannonade smoke hiding the fall of shot is a
calibration target.
5. 
24/7 night rule (R18).
 Night is clearly night: sky dimmed, stars and the ephemeris-driven moon visible, color grade cooler and darker. Everything and every activity stays visible, only dimmer:
use an exposure and tone-mapping model with a guaranteed luminance floor so that no unit, structure or activity falls below a minimum readable level (Gate Q10). Night must not be implemented
as black. Light sources (campfires, lanterns, hospital lights, burning buildings, moon, muzzle flashes) are physically placed and flicker-modelled.
6. 
Night activity.
 Every night in the window has an activity schedule per unit: sitting around fires, cooking, sleeping, standing picket, marching (night march of the VI Corps and others as
documented), digging works (Culp’s Hill), moving artillery into position, burying, carrying and treating the wounded at field hospitals, foraging, wagons moving. Where the sources are silent (the
notes flag the night of July 1 and the pickets’ precise positions as gaps), use the unit’s documented posture plus labeled plausible-procedural routine and register the gap.
7. 
Vegetation states.
 A 
vegetation_states
 schedule per field, woodlot, orchard and yard: crop type, height and color range for the date (wheat harvest state in the Wheatfield, corn, oats, hay,
pasture), tree leaf-out and canopy density for early July, orchard fruit state (unsourced in the notes; register the gap), undergrowth, and battle damage to trees and crops over time (trampling,
shell damage, fire, stripped branches, the documented tree damage where sources exist). Pending the NPS Cultural Landscape Report, Pfanz, the 1860 agricultural census and the 1868 damage
claims, every value is tagged and ranged. Grass height has no sourced value in the notes.
8. 
Environmentals.
 Insects, birds, dust, mud, standing water, creek levels (Plum Run, Rock Creek, Marsh Creek), fog, rain, and clouds are state-driven by the weather timeline and the ground
wetness model.
9. 
Never present a lighting or weather condition as documented without a source.
M. Motion and animation (R04, R05, R08, R17)
1. 
No capture sessions.
 Motion is produced autonomously (Part C.10): (a) procedural drill animation encoded from the drill manuals (Hardee and Casey) as command-to-pose keyframes with foot-
planting IK, which needs no third-party motion licence; (b) licence-checked open motion libraries as a base layer, each clip carrying a licence field so incompatible clips are filtered at export (the
notes identify CMU Mocap, 100STYLE and Mixamo as the commercially usable set, with Mixamo requiring an Adobe account; AMASS, LAFAN1, Motion-X, Horse-10 and SMPL-based video
extraction are non-commercial or restricted and used only for non-public prototypes); (c) physics-based and RL-based motion where it is cheaper than keyframing; (d) procedural quadruped gait
cycles for horses, sanity-checked against labeled references; (e) video-based pose extraction only from footage whose licence permits it. All output is labeled a modern reconstruction.
2. 
Required motion set
, each clip tagged with its source type and tier: loading in nine times; firing by file and by rank; bayonet and clubbed-musket melee; falling, dying and wounded variants;
wounded crawl, walk and carry; stretcher and ambulance work; surgeon and orderly work; officers, flag bearers and drummers; digging, chopping, building breastworks, piling rails, laying stone;
marching in column, deploying into line, wheeling, skirmishing; cooking, sitting at fires, sleeping, standing picket; artillery crew service of the Napoleon, 3-inch rifle, Parrott and howitzer
(limbering, unlimbering, sponging, loading, ramming, vent, lanyard, recoil, prolonge retreat); cavalry mounted charge, saber and carbine work, dismounted skirmishing with horse-holders, horse
gaits and falls; horse artillery.
3. 
Total War-style paired “matched combat” is allowed as a technique; test early for the known failure modes (queuing, sliding, clipping, one-on-one only) in dense melee such as the Angle and the
Railroad Cut. The notes found no Total War source for crew, limber, medic or wounded animation; these come from the manuals and the methods above.
4. 
Review without a motion crew.
 Check each clip programmatically against the manual’s step list and timing (Gate Q05, Q06) and by a reviewer subagent. Mark clips as “unreviewed by a human
reenactor or historian” (Section X).
5. 
Individual variation by blend, offset and speed randomization is permitted; record it as procedural.
6. 
Activity assignment.
 Every unit at every time has exactly one activity class from the 
activities
 table (marching, deploying, fighting, resting, digging, cooking, standing picket, carrying
wounded, retreating, and so on) with sources; the animation system selects clips from that class (Section O).
N. Numbers and units
Use SI internally; store original period units alongside. Carry ranges and sources for every count. Never collapse a range to a single number without a decision-log entry. For strengths and casualties
use the full set of source figures (Part B.1) and let the owner choose the default. Unit strengths for rendering are derived from the chosen default, with soldiers instanced one-to-one up to the strength
figure (no capping of soldier counts for performance without an owner decision).
O. Order of battle and ARRIVAL RULES (R04, R11, R16, R17)
1. 
Ingest
 the OOB from the Official Records and Busey and Martin, cross-checked with the NPS and Wikipedia lists in the notes (Wikipedia lists may carry summarization slips and omit regimental
commanders; the Stone Sentinels copies carry flagged errors). Verify every regiment, battery and troop; commander; brigade assignment; and strength. Add regimental and company
commanders where known. The coverage test compares unit counts and strengths in the history layer with the verified OOB (Gate Q01). Cavalry and horse artillery are first-class units in the
same table.
2. 
ARRIVAL RULES.
 Each unit has an 
arrivals
 record (time window, place, route, sources) and a 
departures
 record. These rules are absolute:
No unit, soldier, horse, gun, wagon or flag of a unit appears in the scene before its arrival window opens. Ranges are carried: a unit is not shown before the earliest documented arrival time
Gettysburg Total War animation plan, version 2 | page 135 of 144


=== PAGE 136 ===
unless an owner decision chooses another scenario. Units whose arrival time is unrecorded (the notes list several, such as Artillery Reserve elements and some Confederate positions) get an
inferred window from their documented march, with a register entry.
Before arrival the unit may exist only as an approaching column on the road network at the correct place and time (visible only if on the battlefield map region), with march rate checked
(Gate Q05).
Arrival is a march, not a spawn. Units march from their previous documented position to their fighting position along documented or inferred routes (roads, lanes, fields), at documented or
plausible rates, in column of march, then deploy into line or the documented formation, then occupy their position (Gate Q04, Q06).
Units that arrived on day 2 (for example the V Corps, parts of III and VI Corps, Longstreet’s divisions, Stuart’s cavalry on the evening of July 2, per the Part B.2.4 ledger) are absent before
then. The ledger in Part B.2.4 is the arrival source of truth; conflicts are scenarios.
After a documented departure, the unit leaves the scene by a march (or is replaced by its wounded and dead where documented).
3. 
Pre-battle and approach.
 The window starts June 26 (or the owner’s chosen start) so that Early’s advance, Buford’s June 29-30 movements and the approach marches are modelled, not skipped.
4. 
Activity per unit per time.
 Every unit-time slot has an activity (Section M.6) and a position region with uncertainty. A slot with no sourced activity is filled with the unit’s last documented state
plus a labeled routine, never left empty and never silently invented.
5. 
The 
positions
, 
movements
, 
activities
 and 
arrivals
 tables are exported to the simulation as read-only per scenario.
P. Provenance and the claim ledger (R21)
1. 
Every datum has a provenance record (Appendix B schema) linking to one or more sources with exact page, map sheet, photo ID or URL, and a tier. No datum without provenance enters the
history layer or the product. Render-time flags carry the tier into the engine.
2. 
Claim ledger.
 Each atomic statement extracted from the library becomes a 
claims
 row: subject, predicate, time interval, place, unit, quantity, hedges (“about,” “I think”), author, date written,
source id, page, and a character-offset span (not copied text). Rows in 
claim_support
 link claims to spans. 
conflicts
 groups claims that disagree on time, place, number or sequence.
3. 
A datum is 
documented
 only when a ledger claim from a non-lead source supports exactly it; a datum affecting a visible result needs two independent sources or an explicit flag (Gate Q19).
Corroboration counts only independent witnesses (two accounts copying the same report are one).
4. 
The ledger is built once (Section X) and queried thereafter; later sessions never re-read sources to re-derive a claim.
Q. Quality gates (machine-checkable; R03, and all others)
Run in CI. A sub-requirement passes only when its gate passes. Never lower a threshold to pass; thresholds are set at gate creation from the vertical slice and changed only by owner decision.
Gate
Check
Q01
Unit counts and strengths match the verified OOB table.
Q02
Provenance completeness: no datum without source and tier; no 
documented
 tier without a source extract.
Q03
No anachronisms.
 Every item in every manifest is valid on its date and for its unit (dress, arms, badges, flags,
equipment, vehicles, structures); every 1863 structure exists on its date; no modern object (monuments, avenues,
fences, signs, wires, markers) appears.
Q04
Arrival check.
 No entity of a unit exists in the scene before its arrival window opens; scan every frame range of
every scenario.
Q05
Position-time plausibility: speeds on foot, horse, limber, wagon and in column within documented ranges; no
teleport; continuous trajectories.
Q06
March-to-position check.
 Every unit has a continuous documented or inferred path from its previous position to
its fighting position; no unit appears already deployed.
Q07
Fence and wall state-change times.
 Each damaged, dismantled, burned or flattened fence, wall or gate changes
state at its documented time window (or is flagged with an open register entry); intact before, damaged after, and
the transition is visible.
Q08
No pre-placed earthworks.
 Every earthwork, breastwork and gun emplacement is absent before its construction
start, passes through documented construction states (staking, digging, piling, completion), and carries a work
crew in the construction interval; completion times match sources or are flagged.
Q09
Building states (intact, damaged, burning, burned, loopholed, hospital use) change at documented times; the Bliss
buildings burn July 3 morning; the McPherson barn does not burn July 1.
Q10
Night visibility check.
 At every night time, render test frames and assert minimum luminance and contrast for
every unit and activity in view; the sky is dimmed; moon and stars follow the ephemeris.
Q11
Ephemeris check: sun and moon values equal an independent ephemeris at the site and times.
Q12
Scrub test.
 Random-access seeks (forward and backward, including jumps across the whole window) produce
state identical to sequential playback at the same time; no unit pops; the cache header seed and code version
match.
Q13
Audio delay equals distance divided by the speed of sound at the modeled temperature; attenuation and occlusion
tests; silence in empty woods except documented ambient.
Q14
Ordnance ranges, elevations and rates of fire within documented figures; per-battery gun counts match the
verified battery table.
Q15
Frame-time budget at target agent counts in the headless benchmark.
Q16
Photo-match tests at known camera positions of period photographs (staged-photograph flags respected).
Q17
Coverage: every timeline row has a visual realization or an open register entry.
Q18
Requirement coverage: all 21 requirement IDs have sub-requirements, each passing or linked to an open register
entry with an active reconstruction.
Q19
Claim ledger: every visible-result datum has two independent sources or a flag; no lead-only support.
Q20
Cavalry presence: every documented cavalry unit appears at its documented times with its manifest, horses and
arms.
Q21
Vegetation state check: crop, canopy and ground-cover state per field per date within the documented-or-ranged
schedule.
Q22
Weather state check: scene weather matches the weather timeline at every time.
Q23
Marking check: every non-documented item carries a render flag; no unmarked procedural content.
Q24
Screenshot regression for the viewer.
Q25
Library audit coverage: every source in the acquisition list is 
ingested
, 
blocked with register entry
, or 
scheduled
 with a reason.
Q26
Casualty and hospital flow: every casualty event has a carry path to an aid station, field hospital or burial; field
hospitals show the documented activity in the documented interval.
Q28
Epilogue gate (R22): the Nov 19 chapter has every B.12.2 row and every S-080 to S-088 state with a tier; Lincoln’s
clothing, voice and wording are used only where two sources were read in the audit and otherwise marked
reconstruction in the viewer; the version of the address shown is a selectable alternative in the conflict register;
no off-Gettysburg event appears in the film path; the jump is labeled as a device.
Q27
Budget gate: spend and compute against the Appendix F caps reported; cap breach triggers the stop condition
(Section C and D.5.12).
Run a fresh-context verification subagent for each milestone with instructions to flag only correctness and requirement gaps. Do not self-certify what you have not run.
R. The Evidence-Gap Register
register/
 holds one file per entry, using the Appendix A template. Open an entry when: evidence is missing, conflicting or secondary only; physics or the record prevents literal fulfilment; a tool, rights
or account barrier blocks work; or a requirement’s literal reading is impossible. Each entry has: id, requirement IDs, description, why literal 100% is impossible or blocked, evidence searched (with
negative results), strongest reconstruction method, confidence tier, how it is marked in the product, impact, the owner decision requested, status (open, awaiting owner, decided, closed), and decision
Gettysburg Total War animation plan, version 2 | page 136 of 144


=== PAGE 137 ===
and date. Entries are never deleted. Ship the register with the product. Open entries do not stop other work; they are visible debt, not hidden shortcuts. Seed the register from the Gaps lists in the
research notes (Appendix C.3 lists 25+ starting questions).
S. Sound and acoustics (R03, R06)
1. 
No recording sessions.
 Real 1863 audio does not exist. Build sound autonomously: physics-informed synthesis (black-powder muzzle blast as a band-limited noise burst with fast attack and
exponential decay, low-frequency thump, directional filter, ground-reflection delay, distance filtering) as an agent-built design, plus licence-checked open sample libraries (Freesound CC0 or CC-
BY with per-sample license fields, used through documented access routes; BBC and similar libraries are unusable until their terms are verified). Layer samples for volleys, ripples and
bombardments. Label all audio as modern reconstruction. Black-powder cannon acoustics have no sourced study in the notes; register the gap.
2. 
Model each source with its own position and time. Delay = distance divided by the speed of sound at the modeled temperature. Apply distance attenuation, frequency-dependent atmospheric
absorption, ground effect and terrain occlusion.
3. 
Acoustic shadows and refraction
 are implemented as a physical model with adjustable wind and temperature-profile scenarios. The 1863 atmosphere is unknown; show shadow zones only as
scenarios, tag them plausible-procedural, and register the gap. Documented anchors for comparison: Ewell’s party not hearing Longstreet’s signal on July 2; the Pittsburgh claim (about 150
miles; date contested); the Ellis attribution is unverified.
4. 
Empty woods (R06):
 sources outside the viewer’s range are rendered with correct delay and attenuation; woods without events are silent except documented ambient sound (weather, insects,
birds, horses where a source exists, otherwise procedural and tagged).
5. 
Voice culling and aggregate beds are allowed only as optimizations that preserve per-source timing and distance relationships; document the optimization.
6. 
Music is only what the period sources document (regimental bands, drums, bugle calls); sourced bugle calls and guidon patterns were not found in the notes and are a register item.
T. Timeline and the scrubbable cache (R11, R19)
1. 
Backbone.
 The master timeline (Part B.2) is ingested as 
events
, 
movements
, 
activities
, 
arrivals
 and 
departures
 rows with time windows. Time is continuous with uncertainty windows. Where
sources conflict, each version is a scenario; the viewer can switch scenarios; the default is an owner decision.
2. 
Event-sourced cache with snapshots.
 Bake the simulation as an event log with periodic full-state snapshots (interval set from the slice measurement) so any time can be reached by loading the
nearest snapshot and replaying forward. The cache header holds the deterministic seed, code version, scenario id and the input hash. Rebuild only invalidated intervals.
3. 
Two-way navigation (R19).
 The viewer can play forward and backward, scrub to any time, jump to any timeline event, change speed, and set bookmarks, with state identical to forward
playback (Gate Q12). Backward play uses stored snapshots and reversible visual state (fence, earthwork, building, vegetation and weather states are functions of time, not accumulated side
effects). Jump targets come from the Part B.2 timeline.
4. 
Window: June 26 (or the owner’s chosen start) through the owner’s chosen end (the notes cover the retreat through about July 14); the Law applies to the whole window.
U. UI, camera and product marking (R01, R03, R19)
Free camera with unlimited flight over the whole field and the full time range; smooth scrubbing in both directions and speed control; unit and soldier selection showing unit, time, activity, kit manifest
and sources; saved camera paths for offline re-render; a timeline bar with the Part B.2 event markers, day and night shading and weather; scenario switch for source conflicts. Provenance overlay
showing tiers by color or glyph at the unit and object level; hover or click shows source and register link. A published “what you are seeing” document accompanies the product. No unmarked
synthesized content may appear as documented (Gate Q23).
V. VFX, fire, damage and state change (R05, R14, R15)
1. 
Model each projectile with real ordnance data (Part B.6 and primary sources), using the Total War-derived parameter checklist as vocabulary only: muzzle velocity, elevation, gravity and drag,
bounce and roll by surface, expiry, penetration, spread, per-surface impact effects, shell fuse timing and burst, canister cone, craters, lingering smoke, ground and building damage, morale and
casualty effects. Roundshot bounces on dry ground and stops in soggy earth; defective Confederate fuses and overshooting on July 3 are documented and must be depicted as such.
2. 
Fence damage (R14).
 Fences are modular segments (post-and-rail, worm/snake rail, board, stone wall) with a state schedule: intact, rails pulled for breastworks or fuel, rails knocked down by
shot, flattened by marching units, burned. Each state change has a time window and a source. The animation shows the damage events (soldiers pulling rails, shell strikes, crossing and breaking
of fences, for example along the Emmitsburg Road) at the right time (Gate Q07).
3. 
Earthworks (R14).
 Works are never pre-placed. Each work follows a construction state machine (layout, digging or log laying, parapet build-up, headlog and traverse where documented,
completion) with working parties using the sourced tools (for example the 6th Wisconsin’s about a dozen spades and shovels at Culp’s Hill) and the documented start and finish times;
undocumented thickness, height and tools are plausible-procedural and registered. Rail piles on Cemetery Ridge are not documented and must not be animated as documented. Stone
breastworks on Little Round Top follow the notes’ dating (July 2, with most surviving works credited to later building) and are carried as a conflict (Gate Q08).
4. 
Building states.
 Fire, shell damage, loopholing, hospital conversion and looting follow the buildings schedule (Gate Q09): the Bliss buildings burned July 3 morning by the 14th Connecticut; the
McPherson barn stood; other fires only as documented.
5. 
Smoke, dust, fire, muzzle flash, blood and mud are procedural effects driven by physical parameters and tagged.
W. Weapons and artillery (R04, R10, R16)
1. 
Build a verified weapons table with sources: infantry arms (Springfield 1855, 1861 and 1863 patterns, Enfield, smoothbores, Sharps, Burnside, Gallager, Smith, Merrill, Spencer, Henry and
revolving rifles, Whitworth), cavalry arms (sabers, revolvers, carbines), bayonets, and artillery (Napoleons, 3-inch rifles, Parrotts, Blakely, howitzers) with ammunition, ranges, rates, crew roles,
and limber and caisson loads. Figures to confirm from primary text: Napoleon 12-pdr crew of nine, shot to about 1,680 yd at 5 degrees; 3-inch rifle shot to 1,830 yd at 5 degrees; Springfield
effective 200-400 yd; infantry loads (40 rounds in the box, 20 in pockets or knapsack, 60 total going into expected combat, as the notes report from a secondary source). Gaps flagged by the notes
(Blakely specifications, per-elevation range tables, chest rounds, recoil distance, fuse models, Confederate crew size) are register entries until a primary manual or ordnance report (Gibbon, the
1861 Ordnance Manual) is read.
2. 
Per-battery data.
 Every battery has a record: commander, guns by type and number, section positions by time, horses and teams, ammunition expended where recorded, casualties, and position
changes. Union battery lists by corps and the Artillery Reserve, and Confederate battalions (Alexander, Henry, Dearing, Cabell, Garnett, Poague, Pegram, Walton and others), are ingested from
the Official Records; fragments in the notes are only a starting point.
3. 
Artillery positions
 (R13, R05): gun emplacements follow construction states (Section V) and carry gun type and count; the July 3 cannonade gun counts and timing are carried as ranges.
4. 
Assign weapons to units from regimental arms returns and ordnance reports; where only secondary support exists, tier it accordingly. Spencer and Sharps assignments carry the dispute and the
primary-source resolution.
X. External-source audit: reading the full library and automated review (R21)
1. 
Read the full free library.
 Execute the acquisition list in Appendix C.1 end to end, ordered: Official Records vol. 27 (all three parts), the state monument-commission volumes, regimental
histories, Southern Historical Society Papers (keyword-filtered), Battles and Leaders, Fox, Dyer, Phisterer, Fremantle and the other witness accounts, NPS publications and the Gettysburg
Seminar Papers, Chronicling America and other newspapers (as news-spread evidence only), LOC maps and photographs, and the biography and memoir sets. Track each source’s status
(
scheduled
, 
ingested
, 
blocked
, 
partial
) in 
library/status.json
 and register blocks.
2. 
Method (map-reduce, ingest once).
 Map: split text by report header and page; small models return schema-validated JSON per chunk (author, rank, unit, date written, location, events with
time and place, numbers, named people, hedges, quote span offsets). Reduce: merge per unit and time window using an alias table (for example “20th Maine,” “Twentieth Maine Vols.”). Audit: a
separate verifier model re-reads the source span of each extracted claim; a second adversarial subagent tries to refute each high-impact claim.
3. 
Source-library audit steps
, applied per source: (a) identify copy, edition and OCR quality, and fetch a second witness where available (for the Official Records, diff the eHistory transcription
against HathiTrust or Internet Archive scans); (b) clean OCR (dehyphenate, remove running heads, fix long-s and rn/m confusions, keep page images for tables); (c) split and extract; (d) assign
reliability tier and date written; (e) write claims; (f) group conflicts by event and flag differences in time, place, number and sequence; (g) prefer sources closer to the event and by participants at
that unit; (h) detect copying between accounts; (i) record negative results; (j) update the ledger and 
library/status.json
; (k) open register entries for unreadable or blocked sources.
4. 
Respect access terms.
 Throttle crawlers (about one request per second or slower on eHistory; the Internet Archive rate-limited the notes’ fetches); check each site’s terms and robots rules;
keep full OCR of public-domain works in a private working store; never redistribute copyrighted text.
5. 
Automated reviewer subagents replace the historian panel.
 At each milestone, fresh-context reviewer subagents (a) check claims against stored spans, (b) re-derive kit manifests from
sources and diff, (c) try to refute high-impact claims, (d) audit timeline rows against the ledger, (e) check the render against photographs (Gate Q16). They are told to report only correctness and
requirement-affecting findings. Findings become fixes or register entries.
6. 
No historian panel is requested.
 The notes found no source establishing that automated review equals expert historical judgment. Therefore every product build records “reviewed by
automated subagents; not reviewed by a historian” . Claude Code never requests a historian panel and never asks the owner to find one. If the owner volunteers an outside review, log its findings
as register entries or fixes.
7. 
Known weak or contested material the audit must test, not trust: the shoe legend, Lost Cause postwar writing on Longstreet’s delay, memoir inflation of numbers, times and roles, early
newspaper casualty figures, differing strength and loss counts (Fox, Official Records returns, Busey and Martin), and the clock differences between armies.

Y. Yield: milestones and delivery order
1. 
Governance and scope (Phase 0): requirements.json, registers, decision log, budget file, job runner. 2. Source acquisition and the claim ledger (R21), in parallel with everything. 3. History layer:
OOB, arrivals, timeline, kit manifests (R10, R11, R17). 4. Benchmark and engine decision memo for the owner. 5. Terrain (R02, R12, R13, R20). 6. Structures with state schedules: fences,
buildings, earthworks, gun positions, hospitals (R13, R14). 7. Weather, vegetation, ephemeris and night (R15, R18, R20). 8. Kit and soldiers, including cavalry (R10, R16, R17). 9. Motion and
animation (R05, R08). 10. Ballistics, effects and sound (R03, R06). 11. Vertical slice: measure cost; set Appendix F caps (the owner accepts the slice as a milestone; it never replaces the whole).
12. Scale-out to the whole battle, all three days and nights, approach and retreat, with preparatory events (Buford’s June 29-30 movements and vedettes; Culp’s Hill breastworks; ambulance, aid
station, corps hospital and Camp Letterman flows; Imboden’s train; the retreat). 13. Automated review and gate runs. 14. Packaging. After each milestone, update the coverage report and 
progress.md
. The PDF report and the product are not declared delivered until every requirement R01-R21 is honored per the Law.
Z. Zero exceptions: forbidden behaviors and the final checklist
Forbidden:
 removing or rewording a requirement; marking a sub-requirement as passing without running its gate; lowering a threshold to pass; leaving a gap unregistered; labeling procedural
content as documented; hiding a gap in the product; choosing among conflicting sources without a recorded owner decision for the default view; inventing a source, quote, URL, time or unit detail;
stopping a task because it is long; 
stopping to ask the owner for motion capture, scans, recordings, crews, historians, permits or compute
; skipping an arrival check, night check, fence or
earthwork state-change check or scrub test; pre-placing an earthwork; showing a unit before its arrival; rendering night as black; leaving cavalry, field hospitals or casualty carrying out; proposing a
reduction and implementing it without a decision; telling the owner something is done when it is partly done; letting a budget cap remove a requirement.
Before reporting any milestone complete, confirm:
 1. every in-scope sub-requirement passes or is linked to an open register entry with an active reconstruction; 2. all 21 requirement IDs are
represented and none dropped (Gate Q18); 3. every register entry has a reconstruction, a marking and an owner-decision field; 4. no datum lacks provenance and no 
documented
 tier rests on a lead
(Q02, Q19); 5. conflicts are carried as scenarios; 6. machine gates Q03-Q12, Q23 pass; 7. the library status file shows no source silently skipped (Q25); 8. the Decision Log is current and no item waits
on the owner except legal acts in Part D.8 and scope decisions; 9. 
progress.md
 is honest about what was not done and what spend was used against the caps. If any answer is no, the milestone is not
complete.
End of the paste-ready instructions.
Conclusion
Version 2 turns the owner’s fourteen demands into concrete sections, tables and gates, and keeps all twenty-one requirements binding. The research behind it was broad but not deep enough to be
called a finished audit: search quotas ran out in most research threads, the Official Records reports and the print-only standard histories were not read, and every source gap is therefore listed with
the source that would settle it. That is the honest state of the evidence, not a retreat from any requirement.
Three things should be clear to anyone acting on this document.
First, the plan commits Claude Code to do every step itself: it ingests the free library, builds the history layer, synthesizes or sources motion and audio from openly licensed or procedural routes, runs
bakes and renders on the owner’s GPU, and audits its own claims with fresh-context reviewers. The only owner acts are the one-time account, licence and billing steps in Part D.8, which are legal acts
software cannot perform, plus decisions recorded in the Decision Log.
Second, the record limits what can be shown as fact. Units, times, structures, weather and sun and moon positions can be documented to the extent the library supports. The position and action of
each individual soldier at each minute cannot be, so those are generated by a seeded, constrained simulation and labeled plausible-procedural in the product. Where the sources conflict, both values
are kept and selectable. A gap that is visible and labeled honors the standard; a gap that is hidden violates it.
Third, the order of work matters. The vertical slice (Day 1 at McPherson Ridge first, then Little Round Top, then the charge, then the full battle) measures real compute and token costs before any cap
in Appendix F is set, and each slice must pass the machine-checkable gates in Part E, Section Q before the next begins.
Appendices
Appendix A. Evidence-Gap Register template
One file per entry (
register/EG-0001 .md
), never deleted.
Field
Content
ID
EG-####
Title
short name (for example “Appearance of Lt. X, Battery Y”)
Requirement IDs
R-numbers and sub-requirements affected (for example R17.0412, R14.007)
Category
evidence missing / evidence conflicting / secondary only / physics-impossible / account or licence step (Part D.8) /
scale or compute blocker
Description
what is needed and what is missing
Why literal 100% is not possible or blocked
specific reason, citing the absence in the record or the physical limit
Evidence searched
every source consulted, including negative results, dates and which session searched
Autonomous substitute applied
which Part C.10 substitute, if any, stands in for a human-supplied input
Strongest evidence-based reconstruction
method, inputs, how derived, with sources
Confidence tier
documented / inferred / plausible-procedural
Competing values
each source’s value and reliability notes
Product marking
exactly how this item is shown (color, glyph, label, info-panel text)
Visual and audio impact
where in the product this appears
Review status
automated subagent review result; “not reviewed by a historian” unless the owner chose human review
Owner decision requested
the question with options, costs and consequences (scope or default-view decisions only)
Agent recommendation
optional, labeled as a recommendation
Status
open / awaiting owner / decided / closed
Owner decision and date
filled only from the Decision Log
Worked examples seeded from the notes (illustrative, not decisions): the face and exact position of an individual infantryman at a given minute; Lee’s height (5 ft 10 in to about 6 ft 1 in); the infantry
step-off time of Pickett’s Charge (about 2:00 or about 3:00 p.m.); Camp Letterman’s opening and closing dates; Wheatfield crop state on July 2; the acoustic-shadow footprint on July 2 and July 3;
Reynolds’ time of death; Spencer carbines in Buford’s July 1 fight; real 1863 audio; construction times and dimensions of the East Cemetery Hill lunettes; rail breastworks on Cemetery Ridge; Hunter
McGuire’s role; Artillery Reserve arrival times; regimental arms for units with no retrievable datum.

C.3 Open research questions (drawn from the Gaps lists of all version-2 notes and version 1)
Each question becomes a register entry or a library task on first pass. Where a question names a source, that source is the next place to look.
Timeline and positions
 1. What were the exact nightly positions of the III, V, VI and II Corps on June 28-29 and of Longstreet’s corps June 26-30? (Official Records vol. 27 Part 3 dispatches;
Coddington.) 2. Where exactly were Buford’s June 29 Fairfield/Emmitsburg route and his June 30 vedette positions, and the July 1 pre-dawn pickets on Marsh Creek and the roads? (Official Records,
Buford and brigade reports; Gamble and Devin reports.) 3. What civilian activity occurred in Gettysburg at dawn on July 1 and on the night of July 1? (Civilian diaries in GettDigital; Pierce, Myers,
McCreary, Broadhead, Thorn.) 4. What were the arrival times of the Artillery Reserve (Tyler/Hunt) and Hunt’s overnight battery placement? (Hunt and Tyler reports, Official Records.) 5. What were the
overnight positions of Anderson (Hill’s corps) and Rodes on July 1-2, and the hour of Lee’s conversations with Longstreet on July 3 and any council of war? (Rodes and Anderson reports; Longstreet and
Alexander accounts, treated as Tier 3.) 6. When did Reynolds die, when did Ewell decide not to press Cemetery Hill, and when did Hancock arrive? (Official Records; Pfanz; Coddington.) 7. When was
Sickles wounded, and what do the Official Records of Sickles, Birney and Humphreys say about the III Corps timeline? (Those reports.) 8. What were the times of the Hunterstown charge and of
Custer’s movement to Gregg on July 3, and Hampton’s wounding (conflicting)? (Custer, Pennington, Hampton, Gregg reports.) 9. What did the 20th Connecticut, 2nd Maryland Eastern Shore and 1st
Maryland Eastern Shore do on July 3? (Official Records and regimental histories.)
Uniforms, arms and soldier types
 10. What primary armament did each Union regiment carry (Dean Thomas’ appendix), and what did the regiments with no datum (1st Minnesota, 14th Brooklyn,
114th PA, 26th NC, Texas Brigade remainder, Pickett’s brigades) carry? (Coates and Thomas; ordnance returns in NARA RG 156; regimental histories.) 11. How did the 5th Michigan obtain its
Spencers and in what numbers, and what revolvers did each of Buford’s regiments carry (the forum figure is unverified)? (Michigan at Gettysburg; ordnance reports.) 12. What were the specifications
of the Gallager, Smith, Merrill, Starr, Joslyn, Maynard and Sharps and Hankins carbines used by cavalry? (Coates and Thomas; ordnance manuals.) 13. What did Stonewall Brigade, Kershaw,
Barksdale, Law, Wilcox, Perry, Wright, Armistead, Garnett, Kemper, Semmes, Wofford, Anderson, Rodes’, Early’s and Johnson’s troops actually wear? (Jensen; Fremantle; regimental histories; museum
objects.) 14. What did Lee, Longstreet, A.P. Hill, Ewell, Pickett, Armistead and Stuart wear at Gettysburg, and what was the uniform of Blackford’s battalion? (Fremantle; photographs; Blackford
report.) 15. Which regiments actually wore corps badges in July 1863, and what are the XII Corps, Cavalry Corps and Artillery Reserve badge shapes? (Dyer, Bates, NPS.) 16. What was the dress of the
Irish Brigade, Philadelphia Brigade, 20th Maine, 16th Michigan and the 72nd PA in July 1863? (Regimental histories; museum objects.) 17. What were the Union cavalry headgear regulations (Hardee
hat cord and feather), boots, guidon patterns, and the Confederate cavalry arms and horse types? (1861 regulations; museum objects; photographs.) 18. Which proportion of Union enlisted men wore
frock coats versus sack coats, and what do Columbus Depot and Richmond Depot jacket types imply for specific brigades? (Jensen; museum objects.)
Artillery
 19. What are the Blakely 12-pdr specifications, the per-elevation range tables for each gun, the limber and caisson chest rounds and recoil distance, and which battery held the 24-pdr
howitzers? (Gibbon’s manual; 1861 Ordnance Manual; ordnance returns.) 20. What were the gun counts, positions, times and losses of Weir, Thomas, Cowan, Stevens, Wiedrich, Winegar, Rugg, Kinzie,
Atwell, Seeley, Clark, Winslow, Watson and Hazard’s batteries, and the Confederate battalion gun counts (Henry, Dearing, Cabell, Garnett, Poague, Pegram, Walton)? (Hunt, Tyler, Alexander and
battalion reports.) 21. What were the Union and Confederate expenditures of rounds and the casualty and gun-loss figures from the July 3 barrage? (Hunt; Alexander; Eshleman.)
Terrain, structures and works
 22. Who built the East Cemetery Hill lunettes, when, and to what dimensions; what works existed on West Cemetery Hill and in Evergreen Cemetery? (Howard,
Wainwright and Hunt reports; NPS CLR.) 23. When and by whom were the Cemetery Ridge rail breastworks built (Hays, Gibbon, Hancock), and what did Lockwood’s, Kane’s and Greene’s brigades
build at Culp’s Hill, with what tools, thickness and headlog dimensions? (Hancock, Gibbon, Greene, Geary, Slocum reports.) 24. What were the road surfaces, widths and grades for the Chambersburg
Pike, Hagerstown, Fairfield, Emmitsburg, Hanover, York, Harrisburg, Carlisle, Mummasburg and Millerstown roads, the Plum Run crossings, Granite Schoolhouse Lane and the Rock Creek bridges?
(Warren map; NPS CLRs.) 25. What is the fence type, height and damage state per segment along the Emmitsburg Road and elsewhere, and what do the 1868 damage claims say? (Gettysburg Daily
post and Emerging Civil War post cited in Sources are leads; State damage claims; NPS CLR.) 26. What are the “Brian barn” lane, Powers Hill emplacements, Warren’s engineer and pioneer details,
tool inventories and Bachelder maps of works? (Bachelder; Official Records.) 27. What are the positional error (RMS) of the Warren map, its tree and orchard symbology, and whether NPS makes its
1863 GIS layers public? (NPS; NARA.)
Vegetation and environment
 28. What were the crop type, height and state per field on each date (including the Wheatfield), the grass height, and the peach/cherry/apple fruit state? (Pfanz; NPS
CLR; 1860 census; damage claims.) 29. What was the species composition, canopy and undergrowth of the Plum Run Slaughter Pen, Culp’s Hill forest, Big Round Top and Devil’s Den, and how did
chestnut loss change the canopy? (NPS vegetation inventory; Pfanz; Frassanito.) 30. What were the humidity, dew point, pressure and visibility, and which wind speed is correct (12 versus 2 mph)?
(Signal Corps and Jacobs observations; NOAA reconstructions.) 31. What was the Gettysburg-specific acoustic evidence and the Ellis attribution, and the date of the Pittsburgh claim? (Acoustic
Gettysburg Total War animation plan, version 2 | page 141 of 144


=== PAGE 142 ===
literature; NPS.)
Hospitals and medical
 32. What were the counts and names of Confederate hospitals beyond the Seminary, McPherson barn and Lady farm; the texts of the II, VI, XI and XII Corps hospital tablets;
the roles of the Brian farm, Sheads, Blocher, Henry Culp, Spangler’s Spring, Michael Fiscel, Pitzer, Black Horse Tavern, Marsh Creek and Hagerstown Road barns, the courthouse and German
Reformed church? (Coco; Letterman; NPS markers.) 33. What was Hunter McGuire’s role and what were the Bishop wagon trains’ details? (Medical histories.) 34. What were the Soldiers’ National
Cemetery burial counts and the dedication details, and what photographs of hospitals exist (LOC “U.S. Christian Commission at Gettysburg General Hospital”)? (LOC; NPS.) 35. What were Camp
Letterman’s exact opening and closing dates? (Letterman; Civil War Medicine museum.)
Library, method and tooling
 36. How many reports are in vol. 27 Parts 1-3, is eHistory complete for serials 043-045, and what are its crawling terms; how do HathiTrust Data API and HTRC bulk
rules apply? (Site terms; Hathi documentation.) 37. Do public-domain Internet Archive scans of OR vol. 27 with downloadable text exist (the verified items are flagged private)? Is vol. 27 transcribed on
Wikisource? 38. Which OR Atlas plates cover Gettysburg, and which Hotchkiss maps exist? (OR Atlas; LOC.) 39. Is any period-specific musket-loading motion data or public-domain reenactment drill
video available under a usable licence; what are the Truebones, Rokoko Vision, MediaPipe and MotionGPT licences? (Licence pages.) 40. What black-powder-specific acoustics studies exist, and what
are BBC Sound Effects and Freesound access terms for scripted use? (Licence pages; acoustics literature.) 41. Does Smithsonian Open Access hold CC0 Civil War uniform or arms scans; what is the
current Fab/Megascans licence status; can Unreal Engine be installed headless without an Epic Launcher login? (Smithsonian; Fab; Epic documentation.) 42. What are the exact headless render flags
and resume behavior for the chosen engine, and what do the owner’s hardware limits (VRAM, thermal, power) allow? (Engine documentation; first measured benchmark.)
C.4 Total War open questions carried from version 1
Per-game unit sizes and maximum soldiers on screen; polygon counts, LOD tiers and impostor distances; artillery crew animation, crew casualties, limbering behavior and rocket mechanics; whether
any Total War title depicts medics, stretcher bearers or carried wounded; pre-Warhammer hit, charge, fatigue and morale formulas; how Warscape pairs soldiers and picks animations; wall destruction
and fire spread in sieges. These bear on engine design only; none supplies Gettysburg content.
Appendix D. Glossary
Term
Meaning
ABT
American Battlefield Trust
Angle
the right-angle turn in the stone wall on Cemetery Ridge where Pickett’s Charge broke in
Arrival rule
the rule that no unit exists in the scene before its documented arrival window (Section O)
Autonomous substitute
a Claude Code-executable method replacing a human-supplied input (Part C.10)
Calibration area / distance
in Total War projectile tables, the circle where a missile may land and the range beyond which accuracy declines
Canister
artillery round of lead or iron balls in a thin case that scatters at the muzzle
Cartes de visite (CDV)
small mounted photographic portraits
Claim ledger
the table of atomic extracted claims with source spans (Section P)
CLR
NPS Cultural Landscape Report
Construction state
a stage in a work’s documented build sequence (layout, digging, parapet, completion)
DEM / DSM / TIN
digital elevation model / digital surface model / triangulated irregular network
EPT
Entwine Point Tile, the cloud-optimized lidar format in the USGS public bucket
Evidence-Gap Register
the record of every gap with its reconstruction, tier, marking and decision (Section R)
Fishhook
the Union line at Gettysburg from Culp’s Hill through Cemetery Hill and Cemetery Ridge to the Round Tops
Gate
a machine-checkable quality test (Section Q)
HABS/HAER
Historic American Buildings Survey / Historic American Engineering Record
IIIF
an image delivery standard used by the Library of Congress and others, which allows tiled programmatic access
Impostor
a flat image standing in for a distant 3D model
Kit manifest
the per-unit record of dress, equipment and arms by soldier type (Section K)
LOD
level of detail
Matched combat
paired motion-capture animations for two or more fighters
Napoleon
the 12-pounder smoothbore gun-howitzer, Model 1857
OOB
order of battle
OR
Official Records of the War of the Rebellion
PAMAP
Pennsylvania’s statewide aerial and lidar program (2006-2008 lidar)
PASDA
Pennsylvania Spatial Data Access
Plausible-procedural
the tier for content generated to fill a gap, seeded and reproducible
Prolonge
rope used to drag a gun by hand while retreating
QL1 / QL2
USGS lidar quality levels
Scrub test
the check that random-access seeking equals sequential playback (Gate Q12)
Snapshot
a periodic full simulation state in the scrubbable cache
State schedule
the time-keyed list of states of a structure, field or tree stand
Vedette
a mounted sentinel or picket
Warscape / Warcore
Creative Assembly’s engine and its announced successor

Appendix F. Spend-and-compute budget table template
Purpose.
 These caps pace the work (Section C.1). They never remove a requirement. All values start blank. They are set from the first measured vertical slice, and only the owner may raise them.
How to set the values.
 1. Run the vertical slice (Section Y, item 11) end to end with logging of: tokens and dollars per 
claude -p
 job (use the reported total cost), session usage for interactive work,
GPU hours and wall-clock per job class, storage, and the failure and retry rate. 2. For each phase, compute the per-unit cost from the slice (per regiment kit manifest, per source page extracted, per
terrain tile, per animation clip, per simulated hour, per rendered frame or chunk, per audio minute). 3. Count the units in the full scope from the verified OOB, timeline and terrain extent. 4. Cap = per-
unit cost times full-scope count times a retry factor (use the slice’s measured retry rate) times 1.25 contingency. Record the arithmetic in the row. 5. Record the sampling error: if the slice covered less
than a stated share of unit variety, widen the contingency and re-measure after the next milestone. 6. Re-measure at each milestone and update caps; a cap change is logged and the owner is told of
any increase needed. If the plan’s usage limits would be exceeded, work is paced across time (rolling windows), not cut. 7. GPU compute is not capped in a way that removes work: record GPU hours
as a planning number and as a power and thermal guard setting. The owner has granted full use of the GPU.
